**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel configuration-based framework to unify the understanding of superradiant phase transitions in Dicke lattices. By classifying possible stable configurations based on lattice symmetry, it explains the emergence of multistability in dissipative systems. This unified picture successfully bridges the gap between nonequilibrium steady states and equilibrium ground states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful topological/combinatorial tool—the configuration classification—to organize complex non-equilibrium phase diagrams in quantum optics models like the Dicke lattice, unifying disparate physical regimes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unified, configuration-based understanding of how multistability arises in superradiant phase transitions within Dicke lattices.

**Main result.** It successfully classifies multiple stable superradiant phases based on lattice symmetry and demonstrates that the classification framework applies consistently across both dissipative (nonequilibrium) and closed (equilibrium) regimes.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field approximations, deriving semiclassical equations of motion for stability analysis in the dissipative case, and minimizing energy functionals for the equilibrium case.

**Model / system.** The system is the Dicke lattice model, involving coupled atom-resonator units on a 1D lattice. The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation (dissipative) or analyzed via Hamiltonian minimization (closed).

**Key observables.** Order parameters characterizing superradiant phases (e.g., real photonic component $R_j$), ground-state energy ($E_{GS}$), and excitation gaps ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g$), photon hopping parameter ($\xi$), cavity decay rate ($\kappa$), and lattice size ($N=4$ analyzed in detail).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mean-field approximation, factorizing operator correlations $\langle AB angle \simeq \langle A angle \langle B angle$, and using the Discrete Truncated Wigner Approximation (DTWA) for dissipative dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing regions of multistability (up to fourfold for $N=4$) in the dissipative case, alongside plots detailing ground-state energy and excitation gaps versus coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the model and develops a configuration classification scheme. It then analyzes the dissipative system using steady-state solutions and stability analysis to map out the nonequilibrium phase diagram. Finally, it compares these findings with the equilibrium case (closed lattice) to establish a unified picture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The emergence of multiple superradiant phases in Dicke lattice models has attracted considerable attention in the quantum optics community. However, a unified understanding of the origin of multistability and its relation to different superradiant phases is still lacking. Here, we develop a configuration-based understanding to classify the superradiant phases in Dicke lattices. We show that photon hopping naturally organizes the possible superradiant configurations according to the lattice symmetry, providing a unified interpretation of the nonequilibrium phase diagram and the emergence of multistability. For the dissipative four-site Dicke lattice, we obtain the complete phase diagram and identify the coexistence of up to four stable superradiant phases. The proposed classification is further extended to five- and six-site lattices. Moreover, we demonstrate that the same configuration-based understanding also applies to the closed Dicke lattice, where the ground state uniquely selects one of the allowed configurations. Finally, we show that different configurations may belong to either same or distinct nonequilibrium universality classes in the dissipative Dicke lattice, while they share the same equilibrium universality class in the closed Dicke lattice. Our results provide a unified picture for understanding equilibrium and nonequilibrium superradiant phase transitions in Dicke lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores polaron-polaritons, quasiparticles formed by strongly correlated excitons coupled to photons. It demonstrates that incorporating dissipative light-matter coupling leads to the emergence of anomalous dispersion and exceptional points (EPs) in the system's spectrum. These EPs provide a tunable platform for studying non-Hermitian many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO physicists working on open quantum systems and quantum optics because it uses engineered dissipation (non-Hermiticity) to control fundamental spectral features like exceptional points in interacting many-body platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how combining strong electronic correlations with dissipation affects collective quantum excitations, specifically focusing on polaron-polariton quasiparticles.

**Main result.** The interplay between many-body correlations and non-Hermitian coupling generates anomalous dispersion relations and exceptional points (EPs) in the polaron-polariton spectrum. These EPs are tunable by the dissipative coupling strength and relative decay rates.

**Method.** The analysis employs finite-temperature Green's function formalism, solving for the polariton spectrum via the determinant of the inverse Green's function matrix after analytic continuation.

**Model / system.** The system models exciton-polaritons in semiconductors exhibiting strong interactions mediated by a biexciton resonance. The Hamiltonian includes non-interacting terms, light-matter coupling (coherent and dissipative), and two-body interaction terms.

**Key observables.** Exceptional Points (EPs), anomalous dispersion relations, polaron-polariton branches ($E_{UP}/E_{LP}$), and level attraction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipative coupling ($\Omega_{Im}$), relative decay rates ($\gamma_x, \gamma_c$), biexciton energy ($\epsilon_B$), and system density.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA) and relies on approximations like the ladder approximation for self-energy calculation in some parts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate polaron branches vs. momentum/detuning, showing how increasing dissipative coupling causes curvature changes, negative mass effects, and the emergence of EPs; other figures map EP onset based on decay rates or BEC density.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem involving correlations and dissipation to deriving the full Hamiltonian and Green's function formalism. It then calculates the self-energy using pair propagators before analyzing the resulting spectrum, identifying EPs as key signatures of non-Hermitian physics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how strong correlations and dissipation combine to shape collective quantum excitations is a central challenge in many-body physics. We investigate the effect of dissipative light-matter coupling on strongly interacting exciton-polaritons in the presence of a biexciton resonance, which gives rise to polaron-polariton quasiparticles. We show that the interplay between many-body correlations and non-Hermitian coupling generates anomalous dispersion relations and exceptional points in the polaron-polariton spectrum. The location and coexistence of exceptional points are controlled by the dissipative coupling and the relative decay rates of the excitonic and photonic constituents, allowing them to emerge across different polaron-polariton branches. These results identify dissipative polaron-polaritons as a versatile platform for exploring non-Hermitian many-body physics with tunable light-matter quasiparticles.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes how monitoring and quantum jumps affect the statistics of work done on an Ising chain. It shows that frequent measurements drive the work distribution toward a Gaussian shape, regardless of initial quench structure. This provides a powerful tool for understanding energy dissipation and fluctuation theorems in measurement-driven many-body dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed quantitative framework connecting measurement backaction (monitoring) directly to the statistical properties of energy exchange, offering insights into non-equilibrium thermodynamics in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how work statistics evolve in monitored quantum systems subjected to both quantum quenches and stochastic measurement processes.

**Main result.** Work distributions transition from discrete, comb-like structures (under weak monitoring) towards an essentially Gaussian form as the number of measurements or monitoring strength increases, consistent with Central Limit Theorem behavior.

**Method.** The analysis employs a trajectory-resolved generating function derived for generalized measurements and evaluates it using a fermionic Gaussian-state formalism.

**Model / system.** The model is a transverse-field Ising chain ($\hat{H}_{QI}$), whose dynamics are governed by stochastic quantum jumps or continuous monitoring, allowing the study of non-equilibrium work extraction.

**Key observables.** Work distribution $P(W)$, work generating function $G(u)$, mean work $\langle W angle$, and variance $\sigma^2_W$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Monitoring strength ($\gamma_t$), number of jumps ($M$), and the quench field strength ($h_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Central Limit Theorem (CLT) mechanism dominating at large measurement rates, causing higher cumulants to be suppressed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crossover of $P(W)$ from multi-peaked/comb-like structures towards Gaussianity as monitoring strength or jump number increases; they also contrast work statistics under controlled jumps inside versus outside the light cone.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes work statistics by first deriving the generating function for general measurements, then applying it to stochastic jump dynamics (showing crossover), followed by analyzing deterministic controlled jumps (highlighting causality/light-cone effects), and finally examining monitored quenches.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate work statistics in monitored transverse-field Ising chains subject to both a quantum quench of the transverse field and either stochastic quantum jumps or controlled measurement sequences. For generalized measurements, we derive a trajectory-resolved generating function for work statistics in the two-point energy measurement scheme. Evaluating it using a fermionic Gaussian-state formalism, we show that, under stochastic jump dynamics, the work distribution crosses over from a comb-like structure to an essentially Gaussian form with shrinking sub-Gaussian tails, as the number of detection events grows. For controlled jump protocols, the energy added by each jump is constant when successive jumps are causally disconnected but decreases and then saturates when they lie within each other's light cone, yielding linear and sublinear growth of the average work, respectively. For monitored quenches, continuous observation washes out the fine structure of the isolated-quench distribution and again drives the statistics toward Gaussian behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel impurity solver for nonequilibrium Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) by combining a weak-coupling expansion with Tensor Cross Interpolation (TCI). This technique efficiently handles high-dimensional integrals on the Keldysh contour, circumventing the sign problem that plagues standard Monte Carlo methods. It allows accurate simulation of quantum quenches in correlated models like the Hubbard model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a robust, non-stochastic solver for nonequilibrium DMFT is crucial for studying open quantum systems driven far from equilibrium, bridging the gap between weak-coupling perturbation theory and complex time evolution simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating nonequilibrium quantum many-body systems, which suffer from exponential computational complexity with real time.

**Main result.** The developed TCI solver successfully implements an impurity solver for nonequilibrium DMFT, accurately reproducing thermalization dynamics and remaining controlled away from the sign problem region.

**Method.** It combines a weak-coupling expansion with the Tensor Cross Interpolation (TCI) algorithm to efficiently evaluate high-dimensional integrals arising in the Keldysh formalism without stochastic sampling.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to nonequilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated electron systems, specifically using the mass-imbalanced Hubbard model on a lattice structure within the framework of Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT).

**Key observables.** Nonequilibrium Green's functions ($G_\sigma(z, z')$), spectral functions, and thermalization behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), filling fraction ($\delta\mu$), maximum simulation time ($t_{max}$), and the weak-coupling expansion order ($n_{max}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on approximating high-dimensional integrals using a tensor-train decomposition (TCI) and assumes sufficient smoothness for extrapolation across the Keldysh contour.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the L-shaped Kadanoff–Baym contour, convergence of TCI results with increasing order ($n_{max}$), and phase diagrams showing solver performance across different coupling regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the complex Green's function formalism on the Keldysh contour to implementing the weak-coupling expansion. It details the use of TCI for integral evaluation, applies this to both transient dynamics (quenches) and steady states, and concludes with benchmarking against known limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating nonequilibrium quantum many-body systems remains a major challenge due to the exponential growth of the computational complexity with real time. Here we implement a nonequilibrium impurity solver based on the weak-coupling expansion and the tensor cross interpolation (TCI), and apply it to nonequilibrium dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). The method approximates the integrands of the high-dimensional integrals arising in the weak-coupling expansion in a tensor-train form, enabling efficient evaluations without stochastic sampling and thereby mitigating the sign problem affecting continuous-time quantum Monte Carlo (CT-QMC) methods. Benchmark calculations for an exactly solvable nonequilibrium impurity model agree well with the exact results and reveal a low-rank structure of the integrands. When applied to interaction-quench problems in the half-filled Hubbard model, the method reproduces fast thermalization at a critical interaction strength with accuracy comparable to CT-QMC. Away from half filling, where the sign problem becomes even more severe, the present approach remains well controlled, revealing a crossover instead of a sharply defined fast thermalization point in the 3/4-filled case. The solver can also be applied to steady-state DMFT problems, yielding accurate spectral functions in the metallic regime without analytic continuation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously develops the mathematical theory behind continuous quantum measurement by classifying the generators of associated evolution semigroups. It extends classical stochastic process theorems to the quantum domain using weak convergence and characteristic functions. The result provides a fundamental decomposition theorem for these generators, linking them to specific physical processes like diffusion and discrete jumps.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical foundation for understanding quantum measurement in continuous time. The decomposition of the generator $L$ into distinct physical components (diffusion vs. jumps) is crucial for modeling realistic open quantum systems, especially those involving photon counting or dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an alternative, probabilistic proof for Holevo's classification theorem concerning continuous quantum measurement processes (stationary quantum trajectories). It aims to characterize the generators of these observation semigroups.

**Main result.** It establishes that any generator $L$ arising from a weakly continuous semigroup of instruments must decompose into four components: shift, free evolution, diffusive, and jump terms. Furthermore, it proves key equivalences linking operator algebra conditions to integral conditions on associated measures.

**Method.** The proof heavily relies on advanced functional analysis tools, including weak convergence of measures (especially for non-compact spaces like $\mathbb{R}$), Lévy's Continuity Theorem, and the characterization of convolution semigroups via characteristic exponents.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum system described by observables in a $C^*$-algebra $A$ on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space. The dynamics are governed by continuous observation processes modeled mathematically by convolution semigroups of instruments $\{M_t\}_{t\ge 0}$.

**Key observables.** The characteristic exponent $L(\omega)$ and the associated path space measure $\mathcal{M}_T$ are key diagnostics. Specific components include the jump measure $J$, the diffusive term, and the Lindblad generator structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $t \ge 0$, outcome space $X$ (simplified to $\mathbb{R}$), and parameters defining the generators ($\alpha, \sigma, b$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary mathematical tools assume weak convergence of measures and rely on extending classical Lévy-Khinchin formulas to the quantum instrument framework. The analysis often simplifies by assuming $X=\mathbb{R}$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses logically: first, establishing weak convergence tools for measures on $\mathbb{R}$ (Chunk 2); second, characterizing weakly continuous semigroups via their generators $L(\omega)$ and relating them to basic Davies generators (Chunks 3 & 4); third, decomposing the general generator $L$ into its four constituent parts using measure theory (Chunks 5 & 6); and finally, proving key equivalences between different mathematical conditions defining these processes (Chunk 7).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a series of papers in the 1980's Alexander Holevo proved a classification theorem for continuous quantum measurement processes, or, as they would today be called, stationary quantum trajectories in continuous time. His main tools were functional analytic in character: starting from a Bochner-type inequality he employed dilation techniques for positive definite kernels. Here we give an alternative, more probabilistic proof: we use weak convergence of measures and employ Levy's Continuity Theorem. We clarify the boundedness conditions in Holevo's theorem, and supply a simple example from quantum optics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves the Wigner's Friend paradox by proposing that wavefunction collapse is a dynamical outcome of the Schrödinger equation when quantum mechanics is formulated on a hybrid space combining continuous ($\mathbb{R}$) and discrete ($p$-adic, $\mathbb{Q}_p$) dimensions. By modeling observers as classical apparatuses, it shows that definite measurement outcomes are absolute features of the dynamics, thus bypassing observer-dependent paradoxes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work tackles foundational issues in quantum measurement theory (the measurement problem) using advanced mathematical structures ($\mathbb{Q}_p$), which is highly relevant for theoretical physicists exploring the foundations of QM beyond standard continuous models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve the Wigner's Friend paradox by providing a quantum mechanical framework that eliminates observer-dependent assumptions.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that wavefunction collapse is a dynamical consequence of the Schrödinger equation on a hybrid space, leading to definite readings for all observers without requiring conscious agents or information exchange.

**Method.** The authors construct a theory using Quantum Mechanics formulated on the product space $\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{Q}_p$, where $\mathbb{Q}_p$ models discrete microscopic space.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by quantum mechanics evolving on $L^2(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{Q}_p)$, incorporating non-local Hamiltonians. The paradox is tested using toy models of particle measurement in both continuous and p-adic spaces.

**Key observables.** Wavefunction localization onto compact supports during measurement interactions, leading to definite pointer readings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the $p$-adic numbers ($\mathbb{Q}_p$) at the Planck-Bronstein scale; non-locality vs. locality trade-off.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that space at short distances is fundamentally discrete, modeled by $\mathbb{Q}_p$, and collapse emerges dynamically from the Schrödinger equation rather than being an independent postulate.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the hybrid space model, develops the dynamical mechanism for wavefunction localization via non-local Hamiltonians, applies this to resolve Wigner's Friend paradox by showing sequential, independent collapses, and concludes by demonstrating consistency with existing no-go theorems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a resolution of the Wigner's Friend paradox within a framework of quantum mechanics (QM) on the hybrid space RxQ_{p}, where Q_{p} denotes the field of p-adic numbers, regarded as a model of discrete microscopic space at the Planck-Bronstein scale. In this framework, wavefunction collapse is not an independent postulate but a dynamical consequence of the Schrödinger equation with non-local Hamiltonians: wavefunctions localize onto compact supports during measurement interactions, producing definite pointer readings without the intervention of observers or the exchange of information between subsystems. We model both Wigner and his Friend as classical apparatuses and show that each produces a definite reading through independent applications of the collapse mechanism, thereby eliminating the conflict between their descriptions of reality. The framework is consistent with the principal no-go theorems in finite- and infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces associated with extended Wigner's Friend scenarios -- including those of Frauchiger-Renner, Brukner, Bong et al., and Guérin et al. -- since it requires no agents capable of recording or reasoning about outcomes, thereby vacating the observer-dependent assumptions that drive those theorems. We illustrate the collapse mechanism explicitly through a toy model of a particle in a box, comparing the standard description with the new one. The non-locality intrinsic to QM on L2(RxQ_{p}) permits realism at the cost of locality, and the Absoluteness of Observed Events holds in our framework without requiring observer independence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theorem defining when certain parts of a system's Hamiltonian are inert regarding local dynamics. By testing this theorem against various qubit chain models, the authors show that violating the required commutation relations allows for an 'emergent coupling.' This emergent coupling can generate measurable entanglement between subsystems that do not interact directly in the original Hamiltonian.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insight into how seemingly irrelevant Hamiltonian terms can become dynamically relevant through non-commuting interactions, which is crucial for understanding decoherence and information flow in complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to establish a rigorous theorem identifying Hamiltonian terms that do not influence local dynamics or observables, while also exploring cases where this assumption breaks down.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that when specific commutation relations are violated (e.g., by applying a transverse field), non-local interactions can generate an 'emergent coupling' leading to measurable entanglement between previously uncoupled subsystems.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the reduced density matrix using partial tracing over environment degrees of freedom, comparing results derived under strict commutation conditions versus those where they fail.

**Model / system.** The models involve coupled qubit chains (Ising interactions) interacting with a central local qubit. The system is analyzed by varying coupling strengths and external fields to test the limits of the theoretical theorem.

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrix ($\hat{ho}_0(t)$), Concurrence ($C(\hat{ho}_{02})$) for entanglement measurement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse magnetic field strength ($\omega_1$), coupling strengths ($G_{ij}$), and the ratio of these parameters relative to each other.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial theorem relies on specific commutation relations $[\hat{H}, \hat{H}_{E2}] = 0$; the entanglement analysis assumes initial unbiased probability amplitudes for certain states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 illustrates the time-evolution of concurrence, showing that significant entanglement generation occurs when the transverse field ($\omega_1$) is comparable in magnitude to the primary coupling strengths ($G_{ij}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes a theorem for local dynamics independence based on commutation relations. It then tests this with multiple qubit chains (confirming the theorem) and subsequently analyzes a third system where breaking the commutation relation leads to emergent, non-local entanglement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This study establishes a theorem that provides sufficient criteria for identifying terms in any time-independent Hamiltonian that have no influence on local dynamics, local observables, or any local phenomena, without any approximation. The usefulness of this theorem is demonstrated by predicting the behavior of three systems. The first system consists of multiple qubit chains with Ising interactions. The second system is formed by a single qubit chain, differentiated by the substitution of Dzyalonshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction for the last Ising interaction. The predictions were verified by analytically deriving the reduced dynamics of both systems. A third system was also considered, namely, a short qubit chain with a transverse magnetic field on the intermediary environment qubit. This transverse magnetic field signals that the commutation relation required by our theorem no longer holds. The third system's local dynamics were also derived analytically, demonstrating that all Hamiltonian constituents contributed to the reduced dynamics. The physical consequences of our theorem's inapplicability were also explored using this third system by analyzing the entanglement dynamics between the qubits that do not directly interact. The results showed that the entanglement is caused by an \textit{emergent coupling} between the two non-directly interacting subsystems. This \textit{emergent coupling} arises from the non-commutativity of the intermediate interactions connecting the two subsystems. When searching for these \textit{emergent couplings}, our theorem can eliminate intermediate interactions that will not produce them.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the robustness of quantum discord in a double quantum dot system undergoing non-equilibrium electronic transport. By using advanced theoretical techniques like the quantum Langevin equation, the authors show that quantum correlations persist robustly even when entanglement vanishes. This establishes nonequilibrium transport as a controllable mechanism for engineering nonclassical features in open fermionic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in mesoscopic devices, providing quantitative tools (like Discord) to characterize nonclassicality under realistic thermal and bias conditions, which is highly relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum correlations, specifically Quantum Discord, behave during non-equilibrium electronic transport processes.

**Main result.** Quantum discord remains finite in the steady state over a broad parameter range, demonstrating that nonequilibrium transport provides a robust mechanism for controlling nonclassical correlations.

**Method.** The analysis employs the quantum Langevin equation formalism to derive the exact reduced density matrix and calculate correlation measures like Quantum Discord and Classical Correlation.

**Model / system.** The system is a double quantum dot (DQD) coupled to fermionic reservoirs, modeled using a full Hamiltonian structure ($H = H_S + H_B + H_I$).

**Key observables.** Quantum Discord $D(ho_{AB})$, Classical Correlation $C(ho_{AB})$, and the ratio $D/C$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System-reservoir coupling strength ($\Gamma$), spectral bandwidth ($W$), thermal bias ($\Delta T$), and reservoir asymmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes a Lorentzian spectral density for fermionic reservoirs and employs the quantum Langevin equation to retain non-Markovian memory effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate time evolution of correlations showing convergence to steady states, dependence on coupling strength ($\Gamma$) and bandwidth ($W$), and how thermal gradients affect discord magnitude.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the transient dynamics first, establishing that initial state does not determine the final steady-state correlation values. It then explores parameter dependencies (coupling, bandwidth, asymmetry) to show robustness and control mechanisms for quantum correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum discord captures quantum correlations beyond entanglement and can remain finite even when the entanglement vanishes. We investigate the transient nonequilibrium dynamics and steady-state behavior of quantum discord and classical correlations in a double quantum dot (DQD) system coupled to fermionic reservoirs. By employing a quantum Langevin equation formalism, we obtain the exact reduced density matrix of the system, enabling a comprehensive analysis of its quantum and classical correlations under nonequilibrium conditions. The influence of system-reservoir coupling strength, spectral bandwidth, thermal bias, and varying initial state on both the transient dynamics and steady-state correlations is systematically analyzed. Quantum discord remains finite in the nonequilibrium steady state over a broad parameter range. Although thermal gradients reduce the overall magnitude of correlations, quantum discord persists and exhibits greater resilience. These results demonstrate that nonequilibrium electronic transport, together with the environmental spectral properties and reservoir asymmetry, provides an effective means of controlling nonclassical correlations in mesoscopic systems and establishes quantum discord as a robust hallmark of open fermionic quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using a single photon interferometer with birefringent quartz to simulate arbitrary open-system dynamics for a qubit encoded in polarization. By treating the frequency mode as an environment, they derive protocols that reproduce complex noise models, including non-Markovian behavior. This establishes the photonic setup as a versatile tool for quantum process simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly versatile, all-optical method for quantum process tomography on qubits, which is crucial for understanding decoherence mechanisms in photonic quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is simulating arbitrary single-qubit open-system dynamics using a controllable photonic platform.

**Main result.** The setup successfully models complex decoherence processes, including non-Markovian dynamics and structured environmental coupling, by engineering the interaction between polarization and frequency degrees of freedom.

**Method.** The authors use an analytical approach involving partial tracing over the continuous frequency environment to derive the reduced density matrix evolution for the qubit polarization state.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single photon propagating through an open Mach-Zehnder interferometer containing a birefringent quartz plate. The qubit is encoded in polarization, and the frequency acts as the effective environment.

**Key observables.** Qubit populations ($ho_{00}, ho_{11}$) and coherences ($ho_{01}$), detection probabilities ($p_j$), and mean fidelity of the simulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Controllable parameters include the spectral width ($\sigma$), mean frequency ($\mu$), quartz thickness ($\ell$), and initial polarization amplitudes ($C_H, C_V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a Gaussian distribution for the photon frequency spectrum ($|g(\omega)|^2$) and relies on tracing out the continuous frequency degree of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (MZI with quartz), show probability distributions vs. tuning parameters ($\mu, \sigma$), and compare simulated qubit evolution against known noise models like depolarization and non-Markovian dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical setup and deriving the initial reduced density matrix via partial tracing over frequency (Appendix A). It then develops the simulation framework by identifying controllable parameters. Finally, it benchmarks the protocol by simulating specific open dynamics, including Markovian damping and non-Markovian coherence revivals.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a photonic platform for simulating arbitrary single-qubit open-system dynamics using a single photon in an open Mach-Zehnder interferometer. A birefringent quartz plate induces a coupling between the polarization and frequency degrees of freedom. By treating the latter as an effective environment, we analytically derive the reduced polarization dynamics. We show that the resulting evolution is characterized by a controllable interplay between populations and coherence, instead of the usual dephasing caused by quartz plates. By adjusting the photon frequency distribution and interferometric parameters, we demonstrate that target single-qubit states can be efficiently reproduced through a tunable optical protocol expected to work under accessible experimental conditions. The simulator is benchmarked against paradigmatic open-system evolutions, including depolarization and non-Markovian dynamics, achieving high accuracy. Our results establish polarization-frequency engineered photonic interferometers as a versatile protocol for simulation of open quantum systems.

</details>

**Authors:** Feng-Li Lin, Ching-Yu Huang

**Summary.** The paper develops a comprehensive diagnostic toolkit to study thermalization in periodically driven quantum spin chains. By simultaneously analyzing subsystem equilibration via relative entropy and the statistics of work done using fluctuation theorems, the authors demonstrate that local and global indicators track the same dynamical crossover across different driving frequencies. This establishes a unified and experimentally accessible method for characterizing prethermal regimes and energy absorption in interacting quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, unified theoretical framework for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in driven quantum matter, linking local measurements (subsystems) to global thermodynamic quantities (work), which is highly relevant for open quantum systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how periodically driven quantum systems thermalize, focusing on characterizing both local equilibration (subsystem) and global energy absorption (work statistics).

**Main result.** Both subsystem thermalization diagnostics and work statistics consistently reveal the same dynamical crossover: from prethermal behavior at intermediate driving frequencies to eventual heating or steady states.

**Method.** The authors combine two operational diagnostics—analyzing reduced density matrices for local equilibration and calculating fluctuation theorems/characteristic functions of work performed over a Floquet cycle—to characterize thermalization.

**Model / system.** The system is an N-site open Ising spin chain subjected to periodic driving (Floquet dynamics). The analysis compares results derived using different effective Hamiltonians ($H_J$, $H_{	ext{eff}}$, and $H_{	ext{ave}}$).

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrices ($ho_A$), relative entropy ($S(ho_A || ho_A^{eta})$), work distribution function (WDF) characteristic functions, and fluctuation theorem deviation measures ($	ext{CFT}_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\omega$) is crucial, defining regimes: low $\omega$ (infinite T heating), intermediate $\omega$ (prethermal plateau), and high $\omega$ (finite T state).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing results derived from different choices of the effective Hamiltonian ($H_0$), with $H_{	ext{ave}}$ being shown to be the most consistent choice.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the stroboscopic evolution of coherence measures ($	ext{CW}_n, 	ext{C}\chi_n$) and relative entropy ($S$) across varying driving frequencies, showing characteristic crossovers. Fluctuation theorem deviations ($	ext{CFT}_n$) are also plotted.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the model (Floquet Ising chain) and then develops the analysis by applying two complementary diagnostics: subsystem thermalization using relative entropy, and global characterization via work statistics and fluctuation theorems. The results are presented by comparing how these two methods track the same frequency-dependent crossover.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Floquet thermalization in a periodically driven quantum non-integrable Ising chain by combining two operational diagnostics: subsystem thermalization and work statistics. For generic interacting Floquet systems, stroboscopic dynamics lead to heating toward an infinite-temperature state at low driving frequencies, to a prethermal state during the crossover regime, and then to a finite-temperature state at high driving frequencies. We show that reduced density matrices of small subsystems provide a precise measure of local equilibration and clearly resolve prethermal plateaus. In parallel, we analyze the statistics of work performed over a Floquet cycle using both the characteristic function of work with and without the two-point measurements, and the related fluctuation theorem, which captures coherent contributions and deviations from thermal equilibrium. By comparing these two diagnostics within the same Floquet setting, we demonstrate that work statistics encode the same dynamical crossover that governs subsystem thermalization. Our results establish a unified and experimentally accessible framework for characterizing Floquet thermalization, prethermal regimes, and coherent energy absorption in interacting quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the effect of local dissipation on the one-dimensional Mott transition in interacting fermions. It shows that for certain bath couplings ($s < 3/2$), the single transition splits into two distinct steps: a BKT transition to an intermediate Dissipative Phase (DP), followed by a new commensurate-incommensurate transition to the Mott Insulator. The analysis uses advanced field theory and RG techniques to characterize these novel critical behaviors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how environmental coupling (dissipation) can qualitatively alter fundamental quantum many-body physics phenomena like Mott transitions, generating novel intermediate phases and critical behavior relevant to strongly correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is understanding how coupling a quantum system undergoing a Mott transition to local dissipative baths modifies the nature of this phase transition.

**Main result.** Dissipation fundamentally reshapes the phase diagram, splitting the conventional Mott transition into two distinct critical phenomena: an LL-to-DP BKT transition followed by a DP-to-MI commensurate-incommensurate transition for bath exponents s < 3/2.

**Method.** The authors employ bosonization to derive an effective field theory action, followed by Renormalization Group (RG) analysis and variational calculations on the resulting critical kink dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional interacting fermionic system exhibiting a Mott transition between a Luttinger liquid (LL) and a Mott insulator (MI), with dissipation introduced via local coupling to harmonic oscillator baths characterized by an exponent s.

**Key observables.** Compressibility ($\kappa$), Superfluid stiffness ($ho_s$), critical exponents ($eta, 
u, z$), and the existence/nature of intermediate phases (DP).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bath exponent $s$, which dictates the spectral density $J(\omega) \sim |\omega|^s$; the critical threshold $s_c = 3/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on bosonization, integrating out bath degrees of freedom exactly, and performing perturbative RG expansions around effective field theories for kink fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the phase diagram showing the splitting of the transition (LL $	o$ DP $	o$ MI) based on $s$, and show finite-size scaling collapses confirming BKT universality at certain points.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and its effective action via bosonization, through RG analysis to determine phase boundaries and critical exponents for different regimes of $s$, culminating in detailed derivations for the two distinct transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how dissipation modifies quantum phase transitions is a central challenge in many-body physics. A paradigmatic example is the one-dimensional Mott transition, which in isolated systems separates a conducting Luttinger liquid (LL) from a Mott insulator (MI). Here, we study the fate of this transition in the presence of dissipative baths locally coupled to the density. Using bosonisation and an exact integration of the bath degrees of freedom, we show that dissipation fundamentally reshapes the phase diagram for bath exponents $s<3/2$, where $s$ characterises the low-energy bath spectrum. Rather than undergoing a direct LL-MI transition, the system develops an intermediate dissipative phase (DP) that is compressible and gapless, yet has zero superfluid stiffness. As a result, the conventional Mott transition splits into two distinct critical phenomena: a Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless transition from the LL to the DP, followed by a new commensurate-incommensurate transition from the DP to the MI. We derive an effective field theory for the latter transition and characterize its universality. For $1<s<3/2$, the critical exponents vary continuously with the bath exponent as $β=ν=1/z=s-1$, while for $s<1$ the transition is governed by $β=ν=1/z=0$ and the doping vanishes sharper than any power law. State-of-the-art Monte Carlo simulations quantitatively support our predictions. These results demonstrate that dissipation can qualitatively alter the nature of the Mott transition and generate novel critical behaviour in strongly correlated one-dimensional systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper compares measurement-based shadow tomography against coherent Quantum CNNs for predicting low-energy subspace overlaps in a quenched Heisenberg spin chain. It finds that the best prediction strategy is highly dependent on the quench strength, with physics-informed QCNNs offering superior robustness across different dynamical regimes compared to local shadow measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how to extract complex, non-local information (like subspace overlaps) from noisy quantum simulations using machine learning, providing a roadmap for applying QML to characterize thermalization and dynamics in strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on developing supervised machine learning techniques to predict the overlap of time-evolved quantum states with specified low-energy subspaces in many-body systems.

**Main result.** The optimal prediction strategy is highly dependent on the quench regime: measurement-based shadow methods excel during moderate quenches, while physics-informed Quantum CNNs offer more robust performance across weak and strong dynamical regimes.

**Method.** The authors compare two quantum information extraction strategies—measurement-based learning (shadow features processed by classical CNNs) and coherent quantum learning (Quantum CNNs)—to estimate the subspace overlap $\mathcal{O}_K$.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a 10-qubit one-dimensional Heisenberg spin chain undergoing time evolution under a local perturbation (quench). The analysis spans weak, moderate, and strong quench regimes.

**Key observables.** Subspace overlap $\mathcal{O}_K$, quantified by the coefficient of determination $R^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quench strength ($h$), Overlap Order ($K$), Measurement Shot Count (500 to 5,000), and specific quench regimes (D1-D5).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations identified are the dominance of state preparation errors on current hardware and the regime dependence of the optimal quantum information extraction strategy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate distributions of $\mathcal{O}_K$ across different quench regimes, compare predicted vs. true overlaps ($R^2$ scores) for various models across D1-D3, and detail resource costs (shots, gates) for both shadow and QCNN approaches.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of predicting $\mathcal{O}_K$, details the two competing quantum learning methods (Shadow vs. QCNN), presents results showing regime dependence in performance ($R^2$), compares resource costs, and concludes with hardware validation findings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Predicting the overlap of quantum states with specified low-energy subspaces is a key diagnostic for quantum many-body dynamics, with direct applications in state preparation, subspace-based algorithms, and the study of thermalization. We study the supervised prediction of subspace overlaps O_K between time-evolved states and K-dimensional low-energy eigenspaces of a 10-qubit Heisenberg spin chain following a local perturbation. We compare two quantum information extraction strategies: measurement-based learning, in which classical shadow features are processed by convolutional neural networks, and coherent quantum learning, in which quantum convolutional neural networks process the state directly. We further introduce physics-informed variants for both approaches, including Hamiltonian-aware shadows and QCNN gates aligned with the Heisenberg exchange structure. Across five dataset configurations spanning weak, moderate, and strong quench regimes, physics-informed QCNNs achieve stable performance, with mean test-set coefficients of determination R^2 = 0.753-0.846. Shadow-based methods show stronger regime dependence: they outperform QCNNs in the moderate-quench regime, reaching R^2 = 0.886, but underperform in weak and strong quenches at default shot budgets, where the best shadow results are R^2 = 0.615 and 0.672, respectively. Hardware validation on Quantinuum and IBM noise models shows that arbitrary state preparation is the dominant limitation, requiring approximately 2,044 two-qubit gates and causing near-complete depolarization before inference. These results identify a regime-dependent tradeoff between measurement-based and coherent quantum learning, with shadow methods excelling when the target remains locally accessible and physics-informed QCNNs providing more robust performance across dynamical regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a one-dimensional electronic chain coupled to an optical cavity field. It demonstrates that this coupling modifies the system's low-energy physics into a 'strange Luttinger liquid,' breaking standard theoretical relations. Furthermore, it maps out a phase diagram showing how electron-electron interactions interact with the cavity field, affecting topological phases like those hosting Majorana modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges cavity QED (AMO physics) with strongly correlated electron systems, showing how light-matter coupling fundamentally alters the universal low-energy description of 1D quantum materials, which is highly relevant for engineered quantum simulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how coupling a one-dimensional electronic chain to a quantized vacuum field modifies its low-energy physics, particularly when electron-electron interactions are present.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a 'strange Luttinger liquid' in the noninteracting limit, breaking standard velocity relations; for finite interactions, the cavity field significantly shifts phase boundaries and modifies Majorana-like zero modes.

**Method.** The authors employ bosonization techniques, low-energy effective Hamiltonians derived via perturbation theory, and exact diagonalization (ED) to map out phase diagrams.

**Model / system.** The model is a tight-binding electronic chain coupled to a single quantized cavity mode. The Hamiltonian includes terms for photon energy ($\omega_c$), light-matter coupling ($g$), and electron-electron interaction ($U$).

**Key observables.** Low-energy excitation spectrum, local density of states, charge susceptibilities (for CDW/PS transitions), and spectral weight associated with Majorana-like zero modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), coupling strength ($g$), cavity frequency ($\omega_c$), and hopping amplitude ($t$). Key regimes include $U=0$ (noninteracting) and finite $U$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving low-energy effective descriptions, using perturbation theory in the light-matter coupling ($g$), and assuming a large but finite chain size.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electronic phase diagram showing transitions between LL, CDW, and PS phases as functions of interaction strength and cavity coupling. Another figure likely shows spectral signatures related to Majorana modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the noninteracting limit ($U=0$) leading to a strange Luttinger liquid description using bosonization. It then extends this analysis to finite electron-electron interactions, mapping out a phase diagram and analyzing topological features like Majorana zero modes via exact diagonalization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a one-dimensional electronic chain coupled to a homogeneous quantized vacuum field and electron-electron interactions. In the absence of the latter, we derive a low-energy effective description in the presence of light-matter coupling, which we identify as a strange Luttinger liquid. Although it retains a formal resemblance to conventional Luttinger liquid theory, the coupling to the quantum field qualitatively modifies the low-energy sector and breaks the standard velocity relation underlying Luttinger universality. For finite electron-electron interactions, we recover a phase diagram featuring several phases as a function of interaction strength and hopping amplitude, including a phase hosting Majorana-like zero modes. Using exact diagonalization, we compute observables that characterize the phase boundaries and show that the cavity field significantly shifts them. We also study the fate of Majorana-like states under the influence of the cavity field, highlighting their modification by light-matter coupling. Finally, we investigate whether the strange Luttinger liquid description identified in the noninteracting regime continues to hold when electron-electron interactions are introduced.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper establishes that any abstract local active dynamics can be physically simulated using engineered thermal baths. It details two mechanisms—the Brownian pump and the Brownian ratchet—which use periodic driving or steady heat currents to generate non-equilibrium behavior in spin systems. This work fundamentally connects the principles of statistical mechanics, thermodynamics, and computation theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, physical realization framework showing how fundamental thermodynamic resources (heat flow, time periodicity) can power complex information processing, bridging statistical mechanics with computation theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that two simple, physically realizable driving mechanisms—the Brownian pump (time-periodic) and the Brownian ratchet (static heat current)—can universally simulate any local active dynamics found in spin systems.

**Main result.** The authors prove rigorous theorems showing that both pumps and ratchets can approximate arbitrary probabilistic cellular automata (PCA) dynamics, thereby connecting abstract rule-based activity to concrete non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

**Method.** The simulation is achieved by mapping the local update rules of PCAs onto the time evolution governed by engineered Hamiltonians coupled to thermal baths (Glauber dynamics).

**Model / system.** The models involve spin systems with 'leader' and 'follower' spins, driven either periodically in time (pump) or by coupling to two reservoirs at different temperatures (ratchet). A concrete example is the ferromagnetic Ising ratchet on a bilayer lattice.

**Key observables.** Steady heat current, memory retention time ($t^*_{	ext{mem}}$), order parameter ($\langle m^2 angle$), and simulation closeness ($\delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature difference ($\delta T = T_\ell - T_f$), coupling strengths ($J, K$), driving period ($	au$), and bath temperatures ($T_\ell, T_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulations are robust approximations that become exact in specific limits (e.g., $\delta 	o 0$ or $1 \ll 	au \ll e^{eta J}$), and the methods are best suited for noise-robust PCAs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing ordered/disordered states based on temperature differences, and plots demonstrate memory decay rates diverging at a critical field ($h_c$) when active dynamics are present.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from simple examples (biased diffusion) to general theorems: first establishing the ratchet for continuous-time PCA ($	ext{A}_{	ext{async}}$), then using the pump for discrete-time PCA ($	ext{A}_{	ext{sync}}$). The final sections generalize these constructions and discuss quantum extensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active systems can exhibit a broad range of phenomena forbidden in equilibrium. Their dynamics are often specified by abstract local update rules, and it is generally unclear when the same behavior can arise from physically natural driving. Here we show that two simple driving mechanisms can universally simulate any local active dynamics in spin systems. The first is the familiar setting of a time-periodic Hamiltonian coupled to a cold bath, which we call a "many-body Brownian pump." As a second mechanism, we promote the Brownian ratchet, traditionally a mechanism for transport, to a "many-body Brownian ratchet": a static Hamiltonian coupled to a hot bath and a cold bath, where the resulting steady heat current can be harnessed not only to drive transport but also to generate local active dynamics. Using probabilistic cellular automata as an explicit model, we prove that for any continuous-time (or discrete-time) local active dynamics, there is always a many-body Brownian ratchet (or pump) that approximates the dynamics, up to noise that can be made arbitrarily weak by tuning energy scales and other parameters. As a concrete demonstration, we construct a simple ferromagnetic Ising ratchet on a bilayer lattice. When the two layers are coupled to baths at different temperatures, this model serves as a robust classical memory even under a symmetry-breaking field, something impossible in equilibrium. More broadly, our work shows that ratchets can use steady heat currents to autonomously generate and stabilize novel collective behavior, realizing a new static setting for nonequilibrium many-body dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report on the successful creation and characterization of optically active single hole spins in ZnSe quantum wells by using an acceptor impurity instead of traditional p-doping. They confirm single-photon emission via antibunching and model the spin dynamics using rate equations derived from optical measurements. This establishes a viable solid-state platform for developing semiconductor-based quantum information devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses solid-state quantum memory and spin qubits by demonstrating electrical control over hole spins via optical pumping in a semiconductor material. The combination of DFT modeling with time-domain spectroscopy is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to realize optically active single hole spins in ZnSe, overcoming limitations associated with conventional p-doping methods.

**Main result.** They successfully isolated a single-hole spin bound to an acceptor impurity and demonstrated its optical activity, establishing a promising platform for spin qubits and single-photon sources.

**Method.** The study combines first-principles DFT calculations to predict defect levels with advanced optical measurements like time-resolved photoluminescence, photon autocorrelation ($	ext{g}^{(2)}(	au)$), and magnetospectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single-hole spin bound to a shallow acceptor impurity (likely Nitrogen) within a ZnSe quantum well heterostructure. The dynamics are modeled using rate equations describing transitions between ionized, neutral, and exciton states.

**Key observables.** Effective hole g-factor ($\approx 0.7$), optical resonance linewidth ($26.7 	ext{ GHz}$), fast radiative lifetime ($\sim 244 	ext{ ps}$), and antibunching in $	ext{g}^{(2)}(0)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Acceptor ionization energy ($	ext{E}_{	ext{A}}$) and the relative rates of optical excitation, resonant pumping, and charge trapping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that optically activating acceptors circumvents p-doping challenges; a limitation noted is that coherence remains limited by spectral diffusion from fluctuating charge environments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of impurity formation energies (DFT), PL spectra showing bound excitons, power dependence confirming two-level saturation, time decay curves, and $	ext{g}^{(2)}(	au)$ plots demonstrating antibunching.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical challenge (p-doping limitations) to proposing an optical activation mechanism. It details spectroscopic characterization (g-factor, linewidth), confirms single-photon emission ($	ext{g}^{(2)}$), and models the population dynamics using rate equations based on varying excitation powers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconductor hole spins offer a pathway to extended coherence times by decoupling from nuclear magnetic noise, while their spin-orbit coupling enables fast all-electrical control. In ZnSe, however, realizing this potential has been limited by p-doping challenges. Here, we circumvent this limit by optically activating acceptors within the ZnSe quantum well. We isolate a single-hole spin bound to a shallow acceptor, confirmed by antibunching and accessed via the fast (244 ps) radiative recombination of a bound exciton. Magnetic and Raman spectroscopy of the ground state reveal an effective hole g-factor of 0.7 and an optical resonance linewidth of 26.7 GHz. Complementary first-principles simulations, together with the experimental results, provide evidence that points toward nitrogen as the most likely acceptor impurity. These results introduce a promising new platform for optically active spin qubits and single-photon sources in ZnSe.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores the role of quantum squeezing in boosting dissipation-induced nonreciprocal interactions within coupled cavity systems. It demonstrates that squeezing can exponentially enhance metrics like coupling strength and energy storage in quantum batteries and optical isolators. The findings establish a tunable method for realizing enhanced, directional quantum signal processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for engineering non-Hermitian effects (nonreciprocity) using non-classical resources like squeezing, which is highly relevant for designing advanced quantum optical devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum squeezing can enhance nonreciprocal coupling induced by dissipation in coupled quantum systems.

**Main result.** Squeezing enhances the effective coupling and improves performance metrics for quantum batteries and optical isolators, though this enhancement is highly dependent on the specific system configuration and parameter regime.

**Method.** The analysis uses master equations and quantum Langevin equations to derive the time evolution of expectation values for coupled cavity modes interacting with a common reservoir.

**Model / system.** The model involves two coupled cavity modes ($a$ and $b$) subjected to dissipation via a common reservoir, potentially incorporating $\chi^{(2)}$ nonlinearity. Squeezing is introduced into the system (cavity or reservoir) via unitary transformations.

**Key observables.** Effective nonreciprocal coupling strength (EC), Enhancement Effect (EE), stored energy, charging power, ergotropy, and optical transmission coefficient ($T_{ab}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), coupling strength ($J$), reservoir decay rates ($\Gamma$), and detuning ($\omega_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on solving the evolution equations for expectation values derived from the master equation in the squeezed frame.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy evolution, charging power enhancement factors, steady-state energy vs. squeezing parameter ($r$), and transmission coefficients ($T_{ab}$) showing exponential amplification with squeezing.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes five different cases based on where quantum squeezing is applied (cavity mode, reservoir, or both) to quantify its effect on nonreciprocal coupling, followed by specific applications to quantum batteries and optical isolators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We systematically investigate the role of squeezing in improving dissipation-induced nonreciprocal coupling mediated by a common reservoir, by incorporating a $χ^{(2)}$ nonlinearity into either the cavity mode, the reservoir, or both. Squeezing is capable of exponentially enhancing the effective coupling, but this enhancement is not universal in such a nonreciprocal interaction. Instead, it depends on the system configuration and specific parameter regimes. Specifically, the squeezing of the reservoir injects additional energy into the system via the dissipation channel, modifying the underlying dynamics in a nontrivial manner. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the proposed approach enhances the performance of the quantum battery, including stored energy, charging power, and ergotropy. We provide the corresponding analytical expressions, together with a systematic analysis of energy transport and parameter optimization. Extending the framework to optical isolation, we observe an exponentially amplified output signal. Our results open a new avenue for nonreciprocal quantum information processing and nonreciprocal quantum device design.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'extreme volume monogamy' by controlling how information leaks between three parties in an open quantum system. By selectively engineering bound states in the local environments of two trusted parties but not a third untrusted party, they demonstrate that the unreliability (steerability) from the untrusted party can be completely suppressed. This offers a powerful tool for designing secure quantum communication channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physical mechanism—selective environmental coupling—to enforce strong quantum correlation constraints (monogamy) in open systems, which is highly relevant to secure quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to achieve 'extreme volume monogamy' by selectively suppressing information leakage from an untrusted third party while maintaining quantum steering between trusted parties.

**Main result.** Selective engineering of bound states in local qubit-environment subsystems can cause the Quantum Steering Ellipsoid (QSE) volume associated with the untrusted party to decay to zero, while preserving finite QSE volumes for the trusted parties.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Quantum Steering Ellipsoid (QSE) volume as a diagnostic tool within an open quantum system framework governed by non-Markovian master equations.

**Model / system.** A tripartite open quantum system involving three qubits (Alice, Bob, Charlie), each coupled to its own local environment modeled by harmonic oscillators. The dynamics are analyzed using time-dependent coefficients derived from solving auxiliary differential equations.

**Key observables.** Quantum Steering Ellipsoid (QSE) volumes ($v_{Y|X}$), which quantify steerability; the presence or absence of bound states in local qubit-environment subsystems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength and spectral density of the environment, particularly focusing on regimes where bound states form ($\eta > 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes independent coupling to zero-temperature bosonic environments with Ohmic-family spectral densities; it compares non-Markovian dynamics against the Born-Markov approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate QSE evolution, showing collapse when bound states are absent (Markovian limit) and preservation of finite volumes when bound states are selectively engineered on trusted parties but absent on the untrusted one.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces volume monogamy constraints. It then models the open system dynamics using a non-Markovian master equation. The core development involves showing that selective bound state engineering allows for the suppression of information access from an untrusted party (zero QSE volume) while maintaining correlations between trusted parties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum steering ellipsoid (QSE) provides a faithful representation of a two-qubit state. When extended to tripartite systems, the steerability from a trusted party to different receivers is subject to volume monogamy relations, which only constrain the total steerability but cannot individually eliminate the steerability of an untrusted third party, leaving a potential channel for information leakage via steering. Here, we show that this residual steerability can be completely suppressed by selectively engineering bound states in local qubit-environment subsystems, without compromising the steerability between trusted parties. Specifically, when bound states are formed in the subsystems formed by the trusted parties and their environments but absent in the untrusted one, the untrusted party's QSE volume decays to zero, while the trusted party's QSE volume remains finite. Our results establish selective bound-state engineering as a mechanism for extreme volume monogamy, with potential applications in secure quantum communication with an untrusted third party.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel closed-loop control scheme for two-qubit gates in trapped ion systems, moving beyond simplified open-loop models that fail under noise. By continuously monitoring an ancillary 'spectator ion' and employing reinforcement learning, the system can correct disturbances in real time. This method promises significantly higher gate fidelity with reduced calibration overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant step toward fault-tolerant quantum computation by addressing environmental noise dynamically. The integration of machine learning (RL) directly into the control loop for physical hardware like trapped ions is highly relevant to modern AMO research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve the fidelity of two-qubit gates (2QG) in trapped ions by replacing unreliable open-loop control with a real-time, closed-loop feedback mechanism.

**Main result.** A proposed Closed-Loop Position Monitoring (CLPM) scheme, utilizing reinforcement learning on a spectator ion's position, is shown to significantly reduce 2QG gate infidelity compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Method.** The approach combines quantum trajectory theory (QTT) with reinforcement learning (RL), where an RL agent learns the optimal control sequence by maximizing a reward function based on the spectator ion's geometric phase.

**Model / system.** The system is trapped ions in a linear Paul trap, modeled using a stochastic quantum master equation that accounts for motional drift, thermal effects, and photon scattering. A key addition is monitoring an ancillary 'spectator ion' via fluorescence detection.

**Key observables.** Two-qubit gate fidelity ($I$), the geometric phase of the spectator ion ($\chi_{qr}$), and the position/fluorescence signal from the spectator ion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate duration ($	au_g$), required precision (e.g., $0.3\Delta x_0$ resolution), and noise sources like motional heating or trap voltage fluctuations ($\delta f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The feasibility of continuous position monitoring is assumed, and the control system's ability to process real-time feedback from the measured fluorescence signal is central.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase-space trajectories for ion chains under various gate schemes (SotA vs. proposed), showing how closed loops imply minimal residual correlation between electronic states and motional modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of open-loop control, then mathematically modeling the full dynamics using a stochastic master equation incorporating monitoring terms. It details the RL framework for on-the-fly correction, culminating in performance comparisons showing the superiority of the closed-loop approach.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

State-of-the-art two-qubit gates with trapped ions employ open-loop control that rely on simplified models to precompute control sequences. Our aim is to introduce closed-loop control for two-qubit gates to correct disturbances as they occur during the gate implementation. We introduce a spectator ion into the ion chain used for quantum logic processing, where it couples with the other ions through collective motional modes. The spectator ion's position is continuously monitored by driving dipole transitions and detecting the resultant fluorescence. We show that incorporating a spectator ion is feasible for linear Paul trap implementations and is expected to reduce the two-qubit gate Bell-state preparation infidelity by an order of magnitude with the deleterious effects of position monitoring being negligible compared to the thermal effects that exist in the system, even in the absence of the spectator ions. Mathematically, we describe driven ion-trap dynamics, including the spectator ion, by a stochastic quantum master equation involving the amplitude-modulation multimode-motional coupling gate, motional drift, thermal effects, recoil from photon scattering, spontaneous decay, and light shift. Our on-the-fly control method employs reinforcement learning with the reward function based on the actual geometric phase of the spectator ion. A key advantage of our approach is that we introduce a control method that involves `learning' and correcting disturbances happening in the trap on-the-fly, thus achieving high-fidelity gates. Our approach will lead to a significantly higher two-qubit gate fidelity at a reduced calibration overhead owing to the small parameter drift in the control system.

</details>
