**Summary.** This work proposes qReduMIS, an advanced hybrid algorithm that combines classical graph reduction with QAOA measurements to solve the computationally hard Maximum Independent Set problem derived from financial correlation data. By iteratively using quantum insights to guide classical simplification, it significantly outperforms standalone QAOA on real-world market graphs tested on trapped-ion hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is finance-related, the core technical contribution—developing a robust hybrid quantum-classical framework to tackle NP-hard problems by mitigating sampling limitations of direct quantum optimization—is highly relevant for developing practical algorithms on near-term quantum hardware like trapped ions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is solving Portfolio Diversification, which maps to finding a Maximum Independent Set (MIS) on asset correlation graphs. This is an NP-hard combinatorial optimization challenge.

**Main result.** The proposed qReduMIS pipeline significantly outperforms standalone QAOA by leveraging quantum measurements to guide provably optimal classical graph reductions, achieving higher success probabilities and better scaling exponents.

**Method.** It employs a recursive hybrid quantum-classical algorithm (qReduMIS) that uses the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) on a smaller kernel graph derived from the full market correlation graph. This guides subsequent classical reduction steps to find the MIS.

**Model / system.** The methodology is validated using real financial data from major market indices and executed on Quantinuum's 98-qubit trapped-ion Helios system, with systematic benchmarking performed on a noisy emulator (H2-1).

**Key observables.** End-to-end success probability (PMIS), average approximation ratio ($\langle AR angle$), and the optimal time-to-solution scaling exponent ($	ext{optTTS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of QAOA layers ($p=2, 6$), the quantum shot count ($	ext{qshots}$), and the correlation threshold ($\lambda$) used to define graph edges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The success of qReduMIS relies on exploiting 'frozen nodes' identified from marginal probability distributions in QAOA measurements, rather than solely sampling the optimal solution directly.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare PMIS and $	ext{optTTS}$ between standalone QAOA and qReduMIS across various indices (S&P 100, Nikkei 225), showing qReduMIS's superior performance and scaling advantage. One figure illustrates the iterative process using node selection based on marginal probabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the MIS formulation for portfolio optimization, details the hybrid qReduMIS algorithm (classical reduction $ightarrow$ quantum subroutine $ightarrow$ classical refinement), benchmarks its performance against standalone QAOA on real market data and systematic graph ensembles, and analyzes scaling laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Portfolio diversification - a cornerstone of modern investment management - can be formulated as a Maximum Independent Set (MIS) problem on asset correlation graphs. Solving this problem at scale is computationally challenging, motivating the exploration of quantum algorithms for practical financial optimization. We propose an end-to-end pipeline leveraging qReduMIS, a recursive hybrid quantum-classical algorithm. Rather than using quantum optimization to directly produce a final solution, qReduMIS leverages independent set measurements from the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) to identify frozen nodes - vertices likely to belong to optimal solutions - thereby guiding and unblocking subsequent (provably optimal) classical reductions on the remaining graph. We benchmark qReduMIS on real financial data from four major market indices with up to 225 assets, executing experiments on Quantinuum's 98-qubit trapped-ion Helios system, with QAOA circuits acting on kernels of up to 78 qubits and 1016 two-qubit gates. While standalone QAOA fails to find the optimal solution for two of the largest indices (S&P 100 and Nikkei 225), qReduMIS achieves success probabilities of $0.40$ and $0.95$, respectively, with average approximation ratios $\geq 0.96$ across all four indices. We perform a systematic benchmark on the Quantinuum H2-1 noisy emulator over 73 asset correlation graphs of varying size showing that, for $p=2$ QAOA layers, the optimal time-to-solution scaling exponent of qReduMIS is $3.2$ times smaller than that of standalone QAOA.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on $	ext{Ba}_2	ext{La}_2	ext{MnTe}_2	ext{O}_{12}$, demonstrating it hosts strongly frustrated 2D magnetism within a 3D crystal structure. By combining multiple experimental probes, the authors establish that the magnetic ground state features $120^\circ$ AFM order confined to the ab-plane while remaining disordered along c. This challenges existing models of magnetic ordering in related perovskites.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores how dimensionality constraints (forcing 2D magnetism into a 3D crystal) can stabilize exotic, frustrated quantum states that deviate from simple mean-field predictions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the exotic nature of strongly frustrated two-dimensional (2D) magnetism occurring within a three-dimensional (3D) hexagonal perovskite structure.

**Main result.** The material exhibits an ideal 2D magnetic ground state characterized by $120^\circ$ Antiferromagnetic (AFM) order in the ab-plane, while remaining magnetically disordered along the c-axis.

**Method.** A combination of advanced experimental techniques ($\mu$SR, Neutron Scattering, NMR) and theoretical modeling (Triangular Lattice Heisenberg Model, Tensor Networks) was employed to characterize the magnetic state.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Ba}_2	ext{La}_2	ext{MnTe}_2	ext{O}_{12}$, a triangular-lattice magnet. The magnetism is modeled using the Triangular Lattice Heisenberg Model (TL-$	ext{XXZ}$), focusing on strong spin fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Magnetic transition temperature ($T_N \approx 4.4$ K), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$), specific heat anomaly, and nonmagnetic asymmetry ($A_{	ext{NM}}$) from $\mu	ext{SR}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The frustration ratio $f=14.2$, the effective moment of $	ext{Mn}^{2+}$ ($S=5/2$), and the dimensionality (2D in ab-plane, 3D host).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic state is approximated by an effective model ($	ext{NN TL-XXZ}$), and the interpretation relies on distinguishing between true long-range order and quasi-2D fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structure, temperature dependence of susceptibility and specific heat, $\mu$SR asymmetry vs. T, and NPD patterns showing magnetic Bragg peaks at wavevectors like $(1/3, 1/3, 0)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically combines structural characterization (NPD) with probes sensitive to spin dynamics ($\mu	ext{SR}$, NMR) and thermodynamic measurements ($C(T)$) to build a case for quasi-2D magnetic behavior in the perovskite lattice.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exotic quantum phenomena are often found to occur in spin systems that exhibit low-dimensional magnetism. By combining nuclear magnetic resonance, neutron scattering, and muon-spin spectroscopy ($μ$SR) techniques, we report a rare instance of strongly frustrated two-dimensional (2D) magnetism in a three-dimensional (3D) hexagonal perovskite. Here, Ba$_2$La$_2$MnTe$_2$O$_{12}$, a triangular-lattice magnet, is shown to undergo a magnetic transition at $T_\mathrm{N} \approx$ 4.4 K, below which the manganese moments form a 120$^{\circ}$ AFM order within the $ab$-plane, while staying disordered along the $c$-axis. This exotic ground state, which exhibits ideal 2D magnetism, is highly consistent with the persistently strong spin fluctuations and the large internal field distributions revealed by zero-field $μ$SR. Further, the 2D magnetism also leads to a significant frustration, much larger than that of most known magnetically-ordered frustrated systems. Our work on Ba$_2$La$_2$MnTe$_2$O$_{12}$ not only challenges the interpretations of magnetic order in other 3D hexagonal perovskites, but it also provides insight into how the dimensionality affects the exotic magnetic states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a versatile analytical model to accurately predict the performance parameters ($f_r$ and $Q_c$) of resonators coupled to superconducting qubits. By integrating four-port network analysis with conformal mapping, the authors provide a fast alternative to computationally intensive simulations. The model is rigorously validated against both full-wave EM simulations and physical measurements on test chips.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides essential tools for designing superconducting quantum hardware, directly impacting the feasibility and scalability of qubit readout circuits in quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a fast and accurate analytical model to determine the resonance frequencies ($f_r$) and coupling Q-factors ($Q_c$) of feedline-coupled resonators used in superconducting qubit readout circuits.

**Main result.** The proposed analytical model successfully predicts $f_r$ and $Q_c$ with high accuracy, showing excellent agreement when compared against both 3D full-wave simulations and physical cryogenic measurements.

**Method.** The methodology combines four-port microwave network analysis, conformal mapping techniques to calculate partial capacitances, and the derivation of closed-form expressions for the transmission coefficient ($S_{21}$).

**Model / system.** The system involves superconducting $\lambda/4$ resonators coupled via Coplanar Waveguides (CPW) feedlines on a chip, which can be planar or 3D heterogeneous. The model treats this as a four-port network reduced to a two-port analysis.

**Key observables.** Resonance frequency ($f_r$), coupling quality factor ($Q_c$), and the transmission coefficient $S_{21}(f)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Geometric parameters such as gap spacing ($h_s$), resonator lengths ($l_s, l_o$), and conductor thickness ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include using conformal mapping which assumes an infinitely thin conductor ($t=0 	ext{ nm}$), and simplifying the complex geometry into manageable network components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate 3D models of the readout system, E-field distributions for even/odd modes in CPW lines, and comparisons (heat maps) showing calculated vs. simulated characteristic impedances across varying geometries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem to deriving the analytical model using network theory and conformal mapping; it then validates this model by comparing predictions against high-fidelity FEM simulations and concluding with experimental measurements on a fabricated test chip.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting quantum chips commonly utilize quarter-wavelength (λ/4) transmission line resonators as readout circuits. An analytical model for the accurate determination of resonance frequencies and coupling Q-factors of feedline-coupled superconducting resonators is introduced. The model leverages four-port microwave network analysis, integrating boundary conditions and conformal mapping techniques to compute even- and odd-mode impedances in edge-coupled coplanar waveguide (CPW) structures. Its versatility allows application to both planar and 3-D heterogeneous architectures, making it a powerful tool for resonator design. To validate the model, a test chip with λ/4 resonators of varying geometries is fabricated and measured in a cryogenic environment. Comparisons with finite element method (FEM) simulations and experimental measurements confirm the model's accuracy, with resonance frequencies and coupling Q-factors aligning closely across configurations. This proposed model facilitates the design of superconducting resonators in readout circuits for more effective, scalable, and adaptable quantum computing architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a Parameter-Space Expansion (PSE) control technique to enhance Controlled-Z gates on superconducting qubits. This method overcomes the speed-fidelity trade-off caused by short-timescale waveform distortions. Experimentally, PSE significantly reduces coherent errors and maintains high fidelity even when operating at very fast gate speeds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental limitations in superconducting quantum hardware by providing a general, controllable method to maintain gate integrity at speeds previously thought impossible due to pulse imperfections.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the inherent speed-fidelity trade-off when implementing fast quantum gates, specifically CZ gates, due to short-timescale waveform distortions.

**Main result.** A novel cyclic control strategy called Parameter-Space Expansion (PSE) was demonstrated to suppress coherent errors significantly while maintaining high fidelity even in the fast-gate regime, breaking the conventional speed-fidelity limit.

**Method.** The authors propose a PSE protocol that introduces an auxiliary degree of freedom by splitting the detuning profile into two segments ($\Delta_1$ and $\Delta_2$) to restore controllability for cyclic evolution.

**Model / system.** The work is performed on superconducting qubits (transmons) coupled via tunable couplers, operating in a regime effectively reduced to a two-level subspace $\{|11angle, |20angle\}$. The control involves shaping the coupling strength ($g$) and detuning ($\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Coherent error rate, gate fidelity (measured by Cross-Entropy Benchmarking), leakage into higher energy states (e.g., $|20angle$), and CPhase accumulation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate duration ($	au < 50$ ns), distortion strength ($\alpha$), coupling strength ($g$), and detuning profiles ($\Delta_1, \Delta_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on reducing the dynamics to an effective two-level system due to large detunings suppressing leakage into other manifolds. The STD model is a numerical post-processing step.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate cyclic evolution failure under distortion, compare error rates of traditional vs. PSE-CZ gates across varying distortion levels and gate durations, and show XEB fidelity improvements demonstrating the speed limit breakthrough.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical necessity of restoring Time-Reflection Symmetry (TFS) constraints; it then presents the experimental implementation using the PSE protocol to achieve robust error suppression and high fidelity in fast CZ gates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-fidelity quantum gates are essential for scalable quantum computation. However, at short durations, short-timescale waveform distortions break the time-reflection symmetry of control pulses, preventing the precise closure of cyclic evolution. This mechanism renders conventional symmetric protocols intrinsically over-constrained. Conventional strategies typically rely on smoothing the pulse envelopes or embedding the interaction pulse within a longer qubit pulse to bypass short-timescale distortions, which inevitably leads to a persistent speed-fidelity trade-off. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a cyclic control strategy based on parameter-space expansion, which restores controllability by incorporating an additional degree of freedom. We experimentally demonstrate this approach in a superconducting controlled-Z gate, achieving robust suppression of coherent errors without increasing gate duration, reducing the average coherent error from 0.27% to 0.12% across multiple two-qubit gates, as validated by cross-entropy benchmarking. Our results establish a general route to fast, high-fidelity cyclic quantum gates beyond the conventional speed-fidelity trade-off.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper characterizes superconducting spiral inductors designed for RF readout in quantum computing. By combining two independent microwave measurement techniques, the authors successfully disentangle temperature and magnetic field effects on the inductor's inductance versus its capacitance. The results establish critical design metrics needed to build stable, miniaturized components capable of operating reliably at elevated temperatures and high magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations for scaling up solid-state quantum processors by providing a robust characterization framework for critical superconducting components under realistic operating conditions (high T, high B).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the need for reliable superconducting circuit elements, specifically spiral inductors, to function as readouts in future quantum computers operating at elevated temperatures and high magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The research successfully separated inductive and capacitive contributions using complementary measurement techniques, showing that temperature and field-induced shifts are dominated by changes in the inductor's kinetic inductance rather than capacitance.

**Method.** Measurements were performed using microwave reflectometry on NbTiN spiral inductors across varying temperatures (up to several Kelvin) and magnetic fields (approaching 1 T), employing both weakly coupled resonator and independent two-port extraction setups.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting spiral inductor, modeled as having geometric ($L_g$) and temperature/field-dependent kinetic inductance ($L_k$). The analysis uses complex fitting procedures to separate these components from parasitic capacitance effects.

**Key observables.** Resonant frequency ($f_0$), internal quality factor ($Q_i$), extracted inductances ($L_g, L_k$), and the field/temperature dependence of these parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature (T > 1 K), magnetic field strength (B approaching 1 T), kinetic inductance fraction ($\alpha = L_k/(L_g + L_k)$), and vortex penetration scale ($B_Q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the observed frequency/quality factor shifts can be quantitatively separated into purely inductive changes, allowing for the isolation of $L(T)$ and $L(B)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the measurement setups (Set-up 1 vs. Set-up 2), compare derived inductance values from both methods to confirm consistency, and plot the degradation of $Q_i$ versus temperature or applied magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the problem in quantum readout circuits, details two complementary microwave measurement setups (resonator coupling and two-port extraction), presents data showing the quantitative separation of inductive/capacitive effects, and concludes with design guidelines linking geometry to operational robustness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting spiral inductors are emerging as key components for radio-frequency (RF) reflectometry, a widely used readout technique for semiconductor spin qubits. Future scalable quantum-computing architectures are expected to operate at elevated temperatures and magnetic fields, placing new demands on the performance and stability of superconducting circuit elements. Here, we present a systematic study of NbTiN spiral inductors under temperatures of several kelvin and magnetic fields approaching 1 T. By combining weakly coupled resonator measurements with independent two-port inductance extraction, we separate inductive and capacitive contributions to device behaviour and directly identify the origin of resonance shifts and quality factor degradation. Furthermore, we establish practical design metrics linking geometry, temperature sensitivity, and magnetic-field robustness. These results provide a general framework for benchmarking superconducting inductors and guiding the design of future RF-reflectometry circuits for practical quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper experimentally investigates how surface charge accumulation on optical nanofibers degrades Rydberg excitation in trapped atoms. By observing time-dependent spectral shifts and suppressing them with an external AC field, the authors confirm that surface charges are the culprit. This work establishes crucial guidelines for mitigating these environmental electric fields in future integrated quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO physicists working on open quantum systems because it provides a concrete, experimentally validated mechanism (surface charging) that limits the scalability of integrated Rydberg quantum devices, offering practical engineering solutions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how uncontrolled surface electric fields, arising from charge accumulation on dielectric nanostructures, degrade Rydberg excitation processes in hybrid quantum systems.

**Main result.** Spectral deformations observed during Rydberg excitation are strongly attributed to DC energy level shifts caused by accumulated surface charges. Applying an external oscillating electric field successfully suppresses these spectral features.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of time-evolving Rydberg spectra with theoretical modeling using the Lindblad master equation to simulate charge effects.

**Model / system.** The system involves laser-cooled $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms excited into high-$n$ Rydberg states near an optical nanofiber (ONF). The model incorporates DC energy level shifts ($\delta_{DC}$) derived from surface charges and uses the Lindblad master equation to track population dynamics.

**Key observables.** Time evolution of the Rydberg excitation spectrum, spectral splitting/deformation magnitude, and remaining MOT population ($N'$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg state ($n	ext{D}_{5/2}$), trapping beam wavelengths (762 nm, 1064 nm), external AC field frequency (100 kHz), and surface charge density.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dominant mechanism for charge generation is identified as Rydberg-ground state collisional ionization enhanced by dipole trapping fields. The model assumes the ONF is single-mode at relevant wavelengths.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups, excitation schemes, time evolution of spectra showing deformation, and comparisons between spectra with and without external AC field suppression.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental setup and observing spectral anomalies under trapping fields. It then uses external fields to diagnose charge effects, models these shifts theoretically, and finally identifies collisional ionization as the source of the charges, providing mitigation guidelines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid quantum platforms based on highly excited Rydberg atoms coupled to nanophotonics devices offer a promising route toward scalable quantum networks and integrated quantum technologies. However, the close proximity of Rydberg atoms to dielectric nanostructures makes these systems particularly susceptible to uncontrolled surface electric fields that can lead to a degradation of the excitation process. Here, we experimentally investigate Rydberg excitation of laser-cooled $^{87}$Rb atoms via the evanescent field of an optical nanofiber in the presence of fiber-guided red- and blue-detuned light fields as used to trap ground state atoms in fiber-based dipole traps. We observe a time evolution of the Rydberg excitation spectrum when both the dipole trapping fields are on and the additional spectral features that appear can be suppressed by applying an external oscillating electric field to the system, strongly indicating that surface charge accumulation is responsible for the observed spectral feature. The experimental results are reproduced qualitatively by a model that incorporates DC energy level shifts arising from electric fields generated by charges deposited on the nanofiber surface. We identify Rydberg-ground state collisional ionization, which is enhanced by the dipole trapping fields, as the dominant mechanism for charge generation. These results provide new insight into charge dynamics at dielectric nanophotonic interfaces and establish practical guidelines for mitigating surface charge-induced electric fields in fiber-integrated Rydberg quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Susceptibility-Kinetic Uncertainty Relation (S-KUR) to set universal bounds on current measurement precision in open quantum systems. By incorporating a 'susceptibility' term derived from the system-reservoir coupling, it overcomes limitations of previous relations that failed under strong coherence or non-Markovian dynamics. This makes it a powerful tool for optimizing measurements in realistic quantum devices like quantum dots.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalized tool for quantifying fundamental limits on measurement precision in complex, non-equilibrium quantum devices, which is crucial for designing high-precision quantum sensors and computing elements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing a universal uncertainty relation that constrains measurement precision for current fluctuations out of equilibrium in open quantum systems.

**Main result.** The derived Susceptibility-Kinetic Uncertainty Relation (S-KUR) provides a general, tight bound on current precision that remains valid even when standard kinetic relations fail due to strong quantum coherence or coupling effects.

**Method.** The relation is derived by applying the quantum Cramér-Rao bound to observables whose dynamics are parameterized by the system-reservoir coupling strength ($	heta$).

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on open quantum systems coupled to reservoirs (baths), often modeled using techniques applicable to steady-state transport, such as in a Double Quantum Dot (DQD) setup.

**Key observables.** Current ($J$), Noise ($S(X)$), Susceptibility ($\dot{M}(X)$), and Partial Dynamical Activity ($A_V(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System-reservoir coupling strength ($	heta$), temperature ($T$), bias voltage ($\Delta\mu$), and the quantum Fisher information ($\mathcal{F}_{	heta	heta}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on generalizing bounds from weak-coupling Markovian limits to encompass strong coupling, coherence, and time-dependent driving protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the violation of classical local uncertainty relations (like the classical KUR) in quantum transport scenarios (e.g., DQD), while demonstrating that the S-KUR remains valid and provides a tight bound across varying biases and temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general principles using the quantum Cramér-Rao bound to define the Partial Dynamical Activity, leading to the universal S-KUR. It then applies this relation to specific physical models like steady-state transport in DQD systems to demonstrate its superiority over classical bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Kinetic uncertainty relations bound current precision of stochastic processes by dynamical activity. The extension of these bounds to quantum systems has been impeded by coherence, strong system-reservoir coupling, and the subtlety of defining dynamical activity in the quantum regime. Here, we introduce a partial dynamical activity through the quantum Fisher information associated with the rescaling of the system-reservoir coupling and show that it bounds current precision via a universal susceptibility-kinetic uncertainty relation. The general validity of this relation for any open quantum system is guaranteed by the natural contribution of a susceptibility term, which is experimentally accessible by tuning the system-reservoir coupling strength. We show how the partial dynamical activity encompasses previous definitions of activity in the weak-coupling Markovian limit and that it provides an information-geometric interpretation of correlator-based activities. We illustrate the tight constraint on precision that our bound provides with the example of steady-state transport through a double quantum dot, where quantum effects invalidate previously developed kinetic uncertainty relations. We expect our bound to provide a powerful tool for optimizing precision in arbitrary quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) can be understood as a resonant inelastic scattering process mediated by a quantized environmental excitation called a 'heliciton.' By coupling the electron to this heliciton, the static chiral potential gains access to energy and momentum exchange channels. This mechanism predicts full spin polarization in sidebands when specific resonance conditions are met, providing a robust theoretical framework for spintronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models a quantum transport phenomenon (CISS) using concepts from open quantum systems—specifically, coupling to an environmental bosonic mode (the heliciton)—which is highly relevant for understanding energy-dependent spin filtering in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a mechanism for Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) that incorporates energy exchange with the environment, moving beyond static chiral potentials.

**Main result.** The heliciton supplies both the necessary screw momentum and energy to convert the static handedness-conversion into a resonant, spin-selective inelastic scattering channel.

**Method.** First Born scattering theory is used on an electron coupled to a quantized environmental mode (heliciton), analyzing resulting sidebands.

**Model / system.** A confined Dirac electron interacting with a chiral potential ($\hat{V}_\chi$) whose quantization yields a 'heliciton' quantum. The system dynamics are governed by time-dependent and stationary eigenvalue equations involving the heliciton reservoir energy.

**Key observables.** Sideband polarization ($P_{sb}$), which reaches full spin polarization at specific resonance detunings ($\Delta_\pm = 0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Helhelicon frequency ($\Omega_q$), momentum transfer ($q$), and the linewidth ($\Gamma_q$) of the resonant sidebands.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on three ingredients: helical Dirac-current texture, quantized screw-symmetric environmental motion (heliciton), and resonant exchange of energy/momentum. No ad hoc spin-dependent potential is introduced.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitation of static potentials to introducing the heliciton quantization, applying first Born scattering theory to derive sideband polarization formulas, and finally confirming that the resonance mechanism provides the necessary spin selectivity signature upon reversing chirality ($q 	o -q$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a quantized chiral-field mechanism for chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS). The corresponding quantum is a heliciton: a helical mode with phase coordinate $φ-qz$, screw momentum $\hbar q$, and energy $\hbarΩ_q$. A helical electron can absorb or emit this quantum, converting the static chiral vertex developed in our preceding work into an inelastic resonant scattering process. Using first Born scattering theory, we show that an incident two-spin-channel state generates two heliciton-assisted sidebands. Absorption converts the $\uparrow k$ channel into the $\downarrow,k+q$ sideband, while emission converts the $\downarrow k$ channel into the $\uparrow,k-q$ sideband. Thus the heliciton supplies both the screw momentum and energy needed to turn the handedness-conversion into a resonant spin-selective channel.   The two sidebands inherit the same sampled-current overlap $J_χ(k)$ from the static theory, but acquire different kinematic weights and different resonance detunings. The sideband sector reaches full spin polarization at the respective isolated heliciton resonances, with $P_{\rm sb}(k,q)\simeq +1$ for $Δ_-(k,q)=0$ and $P_{\rm sb}(k,q)\simeq -1$ for $Δ_+(k,q)=0$. Reversing the screw handedness, $q\rightarrow -q$, interchanges the two sideband channels and reverses the polarization. No ad hoc spin-dependent potential is introduced. The spin selectivity comes from three ingredients: helical Dirac-current texture, quantized screw-symmetric environmental motion, and resonant exchange of screw momentum and energy. This identifies CISS as a heliciton-assisted resonance mechanism that produces spin polarization in the inelastic sideband sector.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper provides a rigorous quantum analysis of asymmetric light scattering from two interacting atoms with built-in gain. It demonstrates that the system exhibits non-reciprocal behavior, meaning the scattered power depends on whether the incident light approaches from the 'gain' or 'loss' side. This quantum finding directly contradicts predictions made by classical linear response theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it explores non-linear, non-reciprocal light scattering phenomena in open quantum systems driven by gain/loss mechanisms. The conflict between quantum and classical predictions highlights fundamental limitations of semiclassical approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the directionality (asymmetry) of light scattering from a binary atomic system that incorporates gain, comparing quantum predictions against classical linear response theories.

**Main result.** The fully quantum analysis reveals a lack of reciprocity in the optical response for side lighting, showing an asymmetry dependent on the incidence side and detuning, which contradicts classical expectations.

**Method.** The authors employ a time-dependent perturbation theory within the wavefunction formalism, calculating scattering power using diagrammatic expansions (e.g., Dyson series) to account for quantum interference effects.

**Model / system.** The system is a binary dimer of identical atoms, where one atom is incoherently pumped (creating gain/loss mechanisms) and interacts with incident and scattered photons via the dipole coupling Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Differential scattering power ($W_{sc}(	heta)$), forward vs. backward scattered power ratios ($rac{W_{fw}^G}{W_{fw}^L}$), and angular distribution asymmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump rate, detuning of the probe field ($\omega - \omega_0$), interatomic distance ($R$), and the ratio of gain/loss coupling strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include assuming the dipole moment lies perpendicular to the interatomic axis, using steady-state limits ($\gamma t \gg 1$), and distinguishing between single-atom and collective (both-atoms) scattering processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate angular distributions of scattered power for frontal vs. side illumination, showing ratios of forward/backward powers as functions of detuning and pump rate, comparing quantum results to classical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives expressions for differential scattering power ($W$) in different geometries (frontal $\perp$, side $G$, side $L$), contrasting the quantum calculation (using diagrams) with the classical linear response theory prediction at each stage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the the scattering of light by a binary system of identical atoms in which one of them is incoherently pumped. This system belongs to the kind of non-parity symmetric optical systems in which gains and losses are partially compensated. We carry out a fully quantum analysis of the directionality of the radiation scattered from the atoms when the incident light strikes the system either perpendicular or alongside the interatomic axis. We find that, generally, while the degree of asymmetry depends on the pump rate, the preferred direction for emission depends on the interatomic distance and the detuning of the probe field with respect to the resonant frequency. On physical grounds, for the case of frontal illumination, the asymmetry is the result of the interference of the photons emitted from different atoms. On the contrary, for side lighting, the asymmetry with respect to the side of incidence is caused by both the phase difference between the probe field photons that strike each atom and the interference between the photons emitted from different atoms. Further, for side lighting too, our quantum approach demonstrates that the forward scattered power depends on the side of incidence, which reveals the lack of reciprocity in the quantum optical response of the system. This result conflicts with what is obtained within a classical approach.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically models the confinement of Dirac fermions in WSe2 quantum dots subjected to localized magnetic fields. By solving the effective Dirac Hamiltonian, the authors show that the magnetic field efficiently suppresses Klein tunneling inherent to graphene. The results demonstrate that this setup allows for strong, tunable carrier localization and resonant transport crucial for next-generation spintronic and valleytronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for controlling electron transport using magnetic fields in 2D semiconductors, which is highly relevant to understanding engineered quantum potentials and open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study theoretically investigates how localized magnetic fields confine massive Dirac fermions within a WSe2 quantum dot structure.

**Main result.** The localized magnetic field effectively suppresses Klein tunneling and promotes the formation of stable, tunable quasibound states in WSe2, establishing a basis for spin-valleytronic devices.

**Method.** The authors derived exact wave functions and scattering coefficients by solving the effective Dirac Hamiltonian using Kummer's confluent hypergeometric functions and Bessel/Hankel functions.

**Model / system.** The system is monolayer tungsten diselenide (WSe2), modeled as a quantum dot subjected to an intrinsic band gap, strong spin-orbit coupling, and a localized perpendicular magnetic field. The Hamiltonian incorporates these features into the effective Dirac equation.

**Key observables.** Scattering efficiency ($Q$), carrier density distributions ($ho(x, y)$), and resonance peak locations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Incident energy (E), magnetic field strength (B), and quantum dot radius (R).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of an effective Dirac Hamiltonian and accounts for discontinuities in the magnetic vector potential at the boundary.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate scattering efficiency ($Q_{total}$) dependence on B, R, and E, alongside spatial density maps ($ho(x, y)$) showing carrier localization patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical model (WSe2 advantages over graphene), deriving the analytical solutions for wave functions in two regions, calculating scattering coefficients via boundary matching, and finally analyzing how varying B, R, and E tunes confinement and resonance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Monolayer tungsten diselenide (WSe$_2$) has become a suitable platform for quantum transport and spintronics and valleytronics applications because it possesses an intrinsic band gap and strong spin-orbit coupling and spin-valley coupling features. The electrostatic confinement of Dirac fermions proves challenging in graphene because of Klein tunneling, yet WSe$_2$ provides an environment that supports both carrier localization and the development of confined quantum states. In this work, we theoretically investigate the confinement of massive Dirac fermions in a WSe$_2$ quantum dot generated by a localized magnetic field. Using the effective Dirac Hamiltonian in the presence of a magnetic flux, we derive the exact wave functions and scattering coefficients by employing Kummer's confluent hypergeometric functions together with Bessel and Hankel functions. Our results show that the localized magnetic field provides an efficient mechanism to suppress Klein tunneling and promote the formation of stable quasibound states. We systematically examine the scattering efficiency and carrier density distributions as functions of the incident energy, magnetic field strength, and quantum dot radius. We find that low-energy carriers are strongly confined by the magnetic barrier, while the interplay between magnetic localization and geometric confinement gives rise to sharp and tunable resonance peaks. These results provide valuable insight into the control of spin-valley transport in transition metal dichalcogenide nanostructures and establish a theoretical basis for the development of quantum confinement devices and quantum information technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the symbiotic relationship between Artificial Intelligence and Quantum Information. It details how AI can be used to control and optimize quantum hardware, while simultaneously exploring how quantum principles (like entanglement or tensor structures) can enhance classical machine learning models. The paper concludes that true progress requires deep integration across theory, experiment, and hybrid systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level roadmap connecting quantum computation theory with modern machine learning paradigms, which is crucial for understanding future computational capabilities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews the rapidly co-evolving interface between Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Quantum Information (QI), focusing on how each field can advance the other.

**Main result.** Progress requires a tighter integration of theory, experiment, and hybrid quantum-classical systems to overcome challenges in reproducibility and hardware realism.

**Method.** The review organizes progress into two directions: AI for QI (e.g., optimizing quantum algorithms) and QI for AI (e.g., using quantum structures for neural network design).

**Model / system.** The core models involve quantum systems described by density operators ($ho$) on a Hilbert space, analyzed through measurements (POVMs) and expectation values.

**Key observables.** Reduced density operators ($ho_A$), expectation values $\langle O angle_ho$, and measurement probabilities $p_ho(s)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity of the system size ($n$) and the noise/measurement limitations are central constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Progress relies on mapping quantum information problems onto classical learning frameworks, which is helpful but limited by physical measurement constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper provides a comprehensive survey structured around the two-way street between AI and QI, detailing specific tasks in both directions (e.g., algorithm discovery, noise control, expressivity).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial intelligence (AI) and quantum information (QI) are rapidly co-evolving. AI is becoming a practical tool for learning, designing, controlling, and verifying quantum systems, while QI offers new computational models, representational structures, and learning-theoretic questions for AI. This survey reviews the interface from both directions. In the AI for QI direction, we organize recent progress around the central tasks of extracting information from limited measurements, training and discovering quantum algorithms, stabilizing noisy hardware, automating experimental and programming workflows, and extending learning-based methods to sensing and networking. In the QI for AI direction, we examine how quantum computation and quantum-inspired structures affect learning through algorithmic speedups, expressivity, trainability, generalization, neural-network design, and tensor-network representations. We close by identifying cross-cutting challenges in reproducibility, scalability, hardware realism, and co-design, arguing that progress will depend on tighter integration of theory, experiment, and hybrid quantum--classical systems.

</details>

**Authors:** Debjyoti Biswas, Nikhil Sharma, Alberto Salvador, Rui Wang, Mats Granath, Adithi Udupa, Giulia Ferrini

**Summary.** The paper introduces and rigorously analyzes the Dual-Rail Cat Code (DRCC), a novel concatenated bosonic code designed for fault-tolerant quantum computing. It demonstrates that DRCC inherits advantages from both dual-rail and cat codes to achieve bias-preserving logical gates and deterministic correction against photon loss. The methodology involves advanced techniques in open quantum systems, making it a significant theoretical contribution to photonic quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO physicists working on cavity QED or quantum optics because it provides a detailed theoretical framework for implementing robust quantum computation using bosonic modes (photons/cavity fields) as the physical substrate, directly addressing noise mitigation in superconducting or optical setups.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols that can simultaneously support universal logical operations, suppress hardware noise, and efficiently handle photon-loss errors.

**Main result.** The proposed Dual-Rail Cat Code (DRCC) offers a highly promising bosonic encoding for FTQC due to its deterministic erasure detection, absence of relative geometric phases during gates, and inherent bias preservation.

**Method.** The authors analyze the error-correction properties using the Knill–Laflamme condition and construct logical gates by projecting engineered Hamiltonians onto the code subspace via continuous dissipation.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes bosonic encodings, specifically the concatenated Dual-Rail Cat Code (DRCC), which combines an inner cat code with an outer dual-rail structure. The analysis involves modeling photon loss and dephasing noise using Lindblad master equations.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates ($\hat{X}, \hat{Z}$ errors), relative geometric phases, success probability of correction protocols, and the satisfaction of the Knill–Laflamme condition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon loss strength ($\kappa$), dephasing strength ($\gamma$), cat state amplitude parameter ($\alpha$), and the order of concatenation (e.g., two-qubit or three-qubit repetition code).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations such as the small-$\alpha$ limit for certain error detection capabilities, and assumes specific dissipation mechanisms can stabilize the desired code manifold.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Wigner functions of cat states, comparisons between different noise models, and schematics detailing multi-step Quantum Error Correction (QEC) protocols using joint parity checks.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the DRCC structure, analyzes its error correction under various noise channels (photon loss/dephasing), details the construction of universal logical gates via engineered dissipation, and finally proposes concatenated QEC protocols to achieve high-order fault tolerance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable fault-tolerant quantum computation requires quantum error-correcting codes that simultaneously support universal logical operations, suppress hardware-specific noise, and enable efficient handling of photon-loss errors. Bosonic encodings such as the dual-rail and cat codes each offer attractive features but also exhibit important limitations when used in isolation. The dual-rail code enables efficient single-photon-loss detection by converting leakage out of the computational subspace induced by photon-loss errors into an erasure error. In contrast, the cat code provides a resource-efficient, bias-tailored error-correction scheme with bias-preserving logical gate operations. Here, we introduce the dual-rail cat code (DRCC), a concatenated bosonic encoding that combines an inner cat code with an outer dual-rail structure, thereby inheriting and enhancing the advantages of both constituent codes. We analyse the error-correction properties of the DRCC and propose a deterministic single-photon-loss correction protocol by concatenating it with an outer repetition code. Exploiting the code's intrinsic noise bias, we construct a universal set of logical gates using only beam-splitter interactions and demonstrate that all logical operations preserve the erasure-biased noise structure. The DRCC offers several distinctive advantages, including the absence of relative geometric phases during gate operations, deterministic erasure detection and correction, and simultaneous syndrome extraction without interrupting stabilisation. These features make the DRCC a promising bosonic code for hardware-efficient, bias-preserving, and erasure-resilient fault-tolerant quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates qubit leakage as a major error source in transmons, developing quantitative tools to characterize leakage mobility. By applying advanced perturbative techniques, it proposes and analyzes two passive hardware schemes—one coupler-based and one readout-based—to actively suppress or remove these detrimental leakage excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed theoretical blueprints for mitigating decoherence sources specific to superconducting qubits by engineering the energy landscape to suppress unwanted leakage pathways.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Qubit leakage is a significant error source in quantum processors, necessitating methods to characterize its dynamics and implement passive removal units.

**Main result.** The authors propose two passive leakage removal units—one using a tunable coupler and pumped transmon, and another based on junction readout—that can selectively mobilize or localize excitations with realistic decay times.

**Method.** The study employs numerical and analytical methods, utilizing Schrieffer-Wolff perturbation theory to derive effective Hamiltonians for leakage dynamics in the dispersive regime.

**Model / system.** The system models superconducting transmons coupled via tunable couplers, analyzed across two-, three-, and extended transmon arrays. The Hamiltonian includes anharmonicity terms and various coupling interactions.

**Key observables.** Leakage hopping rates (0.8–10 MHz), effective hopping strengths ($g_{	ext{eff}}$), leakage decay times ($T_\star$), and qubit population dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frequency detuning ($\Delta_{jc}$), anharmonicity ($U$), coupling strengths ($g_c, g_q$), and the ratio $\lambda = |g_{jc}/\Delta_{jc}|$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily restricted to the dispersive regime where couplings are weak relative to detunings; higher-order terms are treated perturbatively.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of coupled systems, resonance conditions for leakage tunneling (e.g., $|201angle \leftrightarrow |102angle$), and time evolution plots comparing different passive leakage removal schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian, derives effective Hamiltonians using perturbation theory for various excitation transitions, identifies zero-coupling conditions via detuning control, and finally proposes and simulates two distinct passive leakage removal units.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qubit leakage is a noticeable source of errors for quantum computing. In quantum processors, leakage excitations traveling between qubits generate correlated errors and perturb gate implementations. Leakage mobility can also be utilized for creating dedicated leakage removal pathways and removal units. To quantitatively characterize leakage mobility and to guide better design of processor architectures, we study here leakage dynamics in transmons with tunable couplers through numerical and analytical methods. Even if the couplers are tuned to cancel the single-excitation exchange or the ZZ interaction, the leakage hopping rates still persists in the range of 0.8-10 MHz due to transmon nonlinearity. In typical operation regimes, however, transmon frequency detuning localizes leakage excitations. The next-nearest-neighbor transmons can be still be near-resonant opening leakage tunneling channels. To suppress longer-range hopping, we find that the frequency spread of the next-nearest-neighbor transmons needs to be in the range of 1-4 MHz. Utilizing leakage mobility, we propose two passive leakage removal units. One is based on a tunable coupler and a pumped transmon, and another on a junction readout scheme. Based on realistic experimental parameters, our results on selectively mobilizing or localizing leakage excitations are readily applicable in superconducting quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the failure of standard quantum metrology bounds when environmental noise is 'quenched' rather than averaged over. It introduces Tail-Certified Information ($	ext{ITC}_\alpha$) using Conditional Value-at-Risk to quantify precision based on rare, adverse measurement sessions. The findings demonstrate that optimizing for average performance can be catastrophically misleading, necessitating designs robust against the worst-case noise tails.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for quantifying the true performance limits of quantum sensors in realistic, noisy environments where noise correlations are not averaged out. It moves metrology from an idealized average bound to a robust operational guarantee.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard quantum metrology bounds based on averaged Fisher information fail when environmental noise is 'quenched' (frozen during a measurement session but varies between repetitions). The goal is to develop a robust certificate for achievable precision.

**Main result.** The true certified resource is determined by the Conditional Value-at-Risk ($	ext{CVaR}_\alpha$) of the inverse loss, which can be drastically lower than the average QFI. This leads to 'Fisher Glasses' where average metrics are misleadingly high.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced statistical tools like $	ext{CVaR}$ and extreme value theory applied to Fisher information derived from nuisance-projected quantum channels. It contrasts averaged channel assumptions with session-resolved, non-self-averaging statistics.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum sensors (e.g., NV centers, qubits) operating in quenched environments characterized by latent disorder ($\xi$). The core mathematical framework involves projecting the full Fisher geometry onto components transverse to the nuisance directions.

**Key observables.** Tail-Certified Information ($	ext{ITC}_\alpha$), Averaged Quantum Fisher Information ($	ext{I}_{	ext{ann}}$), Inverse Loss Exponent ($eta$), and Transverse Signal Action ($g^\perp$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tail exponent $eta$ (critical for determining if certification is finite, requiring $eta > 1$) and the confidence level $\alpha$ used in CVaR calculation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that nuisance parameters can be projected out using techniques like Schur-complement algebra, allowing the focus on the signal component transverse to the disorder plane.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare average QFI optimization against tail-certified designs (e.g., safe window/portfolio), showing significant recovery of certified information in the latter case. They also illustrate the dependence of the loss exponent ($eta$) on the design architecture.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically contrasts averaged vs. quenched metrology, develops the $	ext{CVaR}_\alpha$ certificate based on inverse-loss tails, establishes theoretical theorems regarding non-self-averaging, and applies these concepts to physical platforms like NV centers using specific design protocols (portfolios, reserves).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum metrological advantage is certified by averaged Fisher responses: contrast, susceptibility, or quantum Fisher information (QFI). This fails in quenched sensors, where slow environmental variables freeze within a session but vary between repetitions: shallow nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers, superconducting qubits with slow two-level fluctuators, and semiconductor spin qubits in drifting charge noise. They sample session-resolved Fisher geometries, not an averaged channel. Certification conditions on the latent session, projects nuisance directions, inverts to attainable loss, then tail-certifies; this inverse upper-tail loss defines quenched tail-certified information. A no-go theorem: no averaged Fisher data determine this certificate; ensembles sharing averaged Fisher matrix, QFI, and projected information have finite or zero certified precision. A Fisher-zero integrability transition governs collapse: the inverse-loss tail exponent $β$ sets the boundary, with nonintegrable certified loss for $β\le 1$, even when annealed information is large or scaling. The certified quantum resource is response transverse to latent disorder, not raw amplification sharing its generator; universal design laws: safe windows, nondegenerate portfolios, Fisher reserves, action separation, Fisher-cut criteria. A shallow-NV Ramsey tournament shows average-QFI optimization is tail-catastrophic, whereas tail-certified designs recover nearly three orders of magnitude in certified information at equal shot budget and latent ensemble. These non-self-averaging phases are Fisher glasses, governed by Fisher-zero rare-event statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a sophisticated magneto-infrared spectroscopy system featuring in-situ polarimetry capabilities. By employing specialized collimation optics, the setup overcomes polarization degradation caused by light reflections within high-field cryostats. This allows for continuous, quantitative measurements of polarization-dependent spectral features essential for studying quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly advanced experimental platform for measuring fundamental material properties related to electron band structure and magnetoelectric coupling under extreme conditions, which is crucial input for theoretical models of correlated electronic states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The full potential of magneto-infrared spectroscopy for probing Landau quantization is limited by the lack of continuous, in-situ polarization control due to severe polarization degradation from beam reflections.

**Main result.** The authors report a novel collimated system that achieves high polarization fidelity (extinction ratio up to 40:1) and enables continuous in-situ linear and circular polarimetry measurements under strong magnetic fields.

**Method.** They designed an optical architecture using incident and exit Kepler type collimation chambers coupled with a remotely controlled polarization module outside the high-field region.

**Model / system.** The platform integrates an FTIR spectrometer, superconducting magnet (up to 12 T), and interchangeable focusing modules supporting Faraday/Voigt geometries for transmission and reflection experiments within a small bore.

**Key observables.** Linear polarization extinction ratio ($\ge 40:1$), minimum root-mean-square noise ($\le 0.0033\%$), and polarization-dependent spectral signatures (e.g., $C_2$ symmetry).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength up to 12 T; sample bore diameter of $\approx 50 	ext{ mm}$; low temperature regime ($\approx 1.7 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that collimating the beam and suppressing multi-reflections significantly enhances polarization fidelity compared to conventional direct focusing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall system schematic, detailed designs of incident/exit collimation chambers, operational modes (linear/circular polarization), and performance metrics like extinction ratio vs. aperture size.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity for in-situ polarimetry, presents the novel Kepler-type optical design to suppress reflections, describes the modular components (focusing modules, polarization optics), and concludes with demonstrations of continuous measurements in both linear and circular polarization modes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magneto-infrared spectroscopy under strong magnetic fields provides a powerful probe of Landau quantization and field-induced collective excitations, yet its full potential has long been constrained by the lack of in-situ polarization control, because the highly divergent infrared beam propagating through narrow light tubes undergoes multiple wall reflections, leading to severe polarization degradation. Here we report a collimated magneto-infrared spectroscopy system that integrates continuous in-situ polarimetry. The system employs incident and exit collimation chambers forming a Kepler type optical architecture, which converts the large-aperture FTIR output into a low-divergence beam and strongly suppresses multi-reflection trajectories inside long gold-plated light tubes, thereby enhancing both optical throughput and polarization fidelity. A remotely controlled polarization module, consisting of an automated linear polarizer and a switchable Fresnel rhomb positioned entirely outside the high-field region, enables continuous in-situ tuning between linear, circular, and arbitrary elliptical polarization states without thermal cycling, manual realignment, or breaking vacuum. Interchangeable compact focusing modules further support Faraday and Voigt geometries in both transmission and reflection experiments within a 50 mm magnet bore, providing efficient beam focusing and signal collection while maintaining polarization fidelity. The setup achieves a minimum root-mean-square noise of 0.0033%, an average noise of 0.0082%, and a linear polarization extinction ratio up to 40:1. We demonstrate the capability through continuous in-situ linear polarimetry and broadband circular polarimetry in the magneto-infrared spectroscopy of various single crystals. This platform establishes a robust experimental framework for in-situ polarization-resolved magneto-infrared spectroscopy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes charge separation in organic solar cells, showing that while traditional models predict efficiency drops at low offsets, the inclusion of Gaussian energetic disorder can actually boost performance. By allowing thermalization within the disordered energy landscape, the system generates an effective driving force, enabling efficient operation even when the intrinsic material offset is small or negative.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit modeling of charge carrier thermalization within a disordered density of states to generate an effective driving force is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in complex quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge separation efficiency in organic solar cells depends on the energetic offset between local exciton (LE) and charge-transfer (CT) states, particularly when significant Gaussian energetic disorder is present.

**Main result.** Energetic disorder can significantly boost charge separation efficiency at intermediate and low offsets by creating an effective driving force through thermalization within the disordered density of states. This allows high performance even when the intrinsic offset is zero or negative.

**Method.** The work compares results from a macroscopic two-state rate model against detailed three-dimensional kinetic Monte-Carlo (kMC) simulations to analyze charge dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system models organic solar cells using donor-acceptor blends on a 3D lattice. Dynamics involve LE generation, exciton transport, and subsequent dissociation into CT states, all influenced by the offset $\Delta E_{LE-CT}$ and Gaussian disorder ($\sigma_{dis}$).

**Key observables.** Internal Quantum Efficiency (IQE), steady-state populations of LE and CT states, and the average energy of the occupied CT state $\langle E_{CT} angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exciton-Charge Transfer Offset ($\Delta E_{LE-CT}$), Disorder Strength ($\sigma_{dis}$), Temperature ($k_B T$), and material parameters like binding energies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rate model assumes constant rates and ignores triplets; the kMC simulations focus on singlet excitons and use specific hopping/transport equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show sigmoid curves of IQE vs. offset, demonstrating how disorder causes a crossover behavior: detrimental at high offsets but beneficial at low offsets by shifting $\langle E_{CT} angle$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the rate model's agreement with kMC in the absence of disorder, then introduces Gaussian disorder into the kMC simulation to map out the efficiency dependence on both offset and disorder strength.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

State-of-the-art organic solar cells increasingly rely on low-offset semiconductor blends, challenging the traditional requirement of a large energetic driving force for efficient charge separation. In these systems, the energetic offset $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$ between local exciton (LE) and charge-transfer (CT) states approaches the thermal energy, making exciton-CT hybridization and thermal repopulation of the exciton level critical to device performance. In this work, we directly compare a macroscopic two-state rate model with three-dimensional kinetic Monte-Carlo (kMC) simulations to investigate microscopic charge separation dynamics and the role of Gaussian energetic disorder. We demonstrate that in the absence of disorder, the analytical rate model accurately reproduces kMC predictions for the whole range of $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$. Specifically, the macroscopic model successfully explains horizontal shifts in the internal quantum efficiency curves that arise depending on how the energetic offset is physically realized in the constituent molecules. We show that these variations can be captured entirely through the ratio of degeneracies of the LE and CT states, respectively. Introducing Gaussian energetic disorder into the kMC simulation reveals a distinct crossover behavior depending on $ΔE_{\mathrm{LE-CT}}$. While disorder is mostly detrimental at large offsets, it can significantly boost efficiency at intermediate and low offsets. Thermalization of charge carriers within the disorder-broadened density of states creates an effective driving force allowing charge separation even at zero or negative energetic offsets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel method to train deep convolutional neural networks for inference on low-power thermodynamic hardware based on the Ising model. It achieves this by developing a scalable backpropagation algorithm that uses finite-temperature Gibbs sampling to approximate standard feed-forward computations. The work rigorously analyzes the trade-offs between computational cost (related to autocorrelation time) and classification accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physics-based pathway for realizing AI hardware by embedding computation within the statistical mechanics framework of Ising models, which is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics and computational limits in physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing scalable training methods for deep AI models intended to run on low-power, hardware-constrained thermodynamic computing devices based on the Ising model.

**Main result.** The authors developed a purely backpropagation-based algorithm that successfully trains deep convolutional networks, achieving high accuracy while demonstrating that inference cost can be bounded by a controllable tradeoff with performance.

**Method.** Training involves adapting standard CNNs into blocks whose computation is approximated by finite-temperature Gibbs sampling on an Ising system, using specialized loss functions (like fixed-point losses) to stabilize the training process.

**Model / system.** The model uses deep convolutional networks structured as a sequence of 'thermodynamic blocks,' where each block implements its function via stochastic dynamics (Gibbs sampling) on an Ising Hamiltonian. The system relies on mapping continuous activations to thermal mean-field averages.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy (e.g., 94.9% on CIFAR-10), inference cost, and the integrated autocorrelation time ($	au_{	ext{int}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The temperature gradient parameter $\delta$, the number of Gibbs sweeps $G$, and regularization weights ($\lambda_{FP}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the thermodynamic inference process can be accurately controlled by tuning parameters like $\delta$ and $G$ to approach the deterministic feed-forward limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the architecture (Encoder $	o$ Thermodynamic Blocks $	o$ Decoder) for a CIFAR-10 classifier, detailing how a single block is mapped onto an Ising system with input ($s_0$), hidden ($s_1$), and output ($s_2$) spins.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical correspondence between high-temperature Gibbs sampling and feed-forward inference to developing specific training algorithms (Gibbs regularization) that stabilize this mapping, culminating in rigorous analysis of error sources like autocorrelation time.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermodynamic computing devices based on the Ising model show great promise for low-power AI inference and edge computing, but scalable methods for training large models for such hardware remain limited. Prior theory shows that the time-averaged behavior of high-temperature Gibbs-sampled Ising systems can implement feed-forward neural inference. We turn this theoretical correspondence into a scalable and purely backpropagation-based algorithm for training deep convolutional networks for thermodynamic inference on Ising machine hardware. Our image classification models achieve accuracies of 94.9% on CIFAR-10 and 76.0% on CIFAR-100 under binary Gibbs sampling. We then develop and experimentally validate a mathematical theory relating inference cost to accuracy and controlling autocorrelation times. Subsequently, we calculate asymptotic results showing that inference cost is bounded by a well-controlled tradeoff with performance and exhibit algorithms for computing optimal inference schedules. Finally, we discuss implications for hardware development and the future of high-temperature thermodynamic AI models.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a unified tensor-network methodology to solve tight-binding Hamiltonians on ultra-large systems, enabling simulations up to billions of sites. By mapping real-space problems onto MPO representations, it efficiently calculates key observables like spectral functions and topological invariants without storing massive matrices. This breakthrough allows for the study of complex quantum matter in regimes previously inaccessible due to computational cost.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful computational toolkit that extends beyond standard DMRG or exact diagonalization limits, making it highly relevant for studying complex quantum materials where system sizes are the primary bottleneck.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing scalable numerical techniques to simulate quantum systems described by tight-binding Hamiltonians on ultra-large lattices (billions of sites).

**Main result.** The methodology enables routine simulation of systems with billions of sites by keeping the memory footprint logarithmic in system size, overcoming limitations of explicit matrix methods.

**Method.** It employs a unified tensor-network approach, mapping large real-space Hamiltonians onto Matrix Product Operators (MPOs) defined on an L-pseudospin chain. Key techniques include using quantics tensor cross interpolation and applying the Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM).

**Model / system.** The models are general tight-binding Hamiltonians applicable to moiré superlattices, quasicrystals, and systems requiring non-Hermitian or correlated descriptions (e.g., Hubbard model, excitons). The system size $N$ can reach $10^{12}$ sites.

**Key observables.** Spectral functions ($A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$), Local Density of States (LDOS), real-space topological invariants (Chern markers), and time evolution dynamics ($\langle x|ho(t)|xangle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$ up to billions of sites; bond dimension remaining modest (order of tens); energy $\omega \in (-1, 1)$ or complex energies for non-Hermitian analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the tensor network compressibility of real-space structures and assumes that physical observables can be efficiently represented as MPOs/MPSs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mapping from real-space lattices to pseudospin chains, comparing memory scaling (MPO vs. full matrix), and showing the construction of spectral operators and topological markers using tensor contractions.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the mathematical framework by first establishing the MPO representation for Hamiltonians on various lattices (1D/2D) via shift tensors. It then outlines algorithms for calculating observables like spectral functions (via KPM), topological invariants, and time evolution (via von Neumann equation solvers).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding quantum materials at meso and even macroscopic scales requires tight-binding calculations on system sizes where explicit matrix representations become prohibitively costly. This represents a major bottleneck to rationalize phenomena in moiré and super-moiré heterostructures and quasicrystals. Here, we present a unified tensor-network methodology to solve tight-binding problems at exceptionally large scales, by mapping a system of $N = 2^L$ sites onto a many-body problem of $L$ pseudospin sites, which is subsequently solved with tensor network algorithms. For Hamiltonians with compressible real-space structure, the tensor network bond dimension remains modest, typically of order a few tens, independent of $N$.Tensor network representations of arbitrary hopping functions including long-range, spatially modulated, and twisted-layer couplings are built with quantics tensor cross interpolation, and all physical observables are evaluated entirely with tensor network algebra without explicit matrix storage or diagonalization. We demonstrate applications to spectral functions, momentum-space spectra via the tensor-network quantum Fourier transform, real-space topological invariants, real-time dynamics, correlation induced symmetry breaking with self-consistent mean-field calculations, non-Hermitian phenomena, and excitonic many-body physics. Our methodology enables routinely solving systems with billions of sites, by leveraging the tensor network compressibility of real-space structures, and establishing a flexible framework to study quantum matter at ultra-large length scales. The methodology is implemented in the open-source Julia package TensorBinding.jl.

</details>
