**Summary.** This paper analytically connects the exact solutions for a periodically-driven two-level system when subjected to different driving fields, such as linear versus rotating-wave approximations. By mapping the problem onto Heun differential equations, the authors establish a unified mathematical framework that correctly reproduces known physical effects like the Bloch-Siegert shifts in various limiting regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep analytical insight into open quantum systems dynamics by connecting exact solutions to highly specialized mathematical functions (Heun equations), which is crucial for understanding driven quantum optics phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing a rigorous, analytical connection between the exact solutions for a periodically-driven two-level system (TLS) under different driving conditions, specifically linear vs. rotating-wave approximation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully derive this analytic link by mapping the dynamics onto Heun differential equations and utilizing path-multiplicative Floquet solutions to recover known physical limits like the Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts.

**Method.** The methodology involves transforming the time-dependent Schrödinger equation into a second-order ODE, which is then solved by relating it to the general forms of Heun or Confluent Heun equations via hypergeometric function expansions.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a two-level atom (TLS) subjected to periodic external driving fields. The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian involving Pauli matrices, leading to differential equations solvable in terms of special functions.

**Key observables.** The population dynamics and coherence decay rates of the TLS; specific shifts like the Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts are key derived quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving strength ($f$), system frequency ($\omega_0$), driving frequency ($\omega$), and detuning parameters ($\Delta$). The analysis explores weak-driving and high-frequency limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core mathematical machinery relies on expressing local solutions via hypergeometric functions derived from path-multiplicative Floquet solutions, which are subject to limitations regarding periodicity and resonance conditions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; the focus is on deriving analytical connections between solution forms (e.g., Eq. 32, Eq. 41, Eq. 85).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the physical problem and mapping it to Heun/Confluent Heun equations (Sections II & III), then detailing the mathematical machinery using Floquet solutions and hypergeometric functions, establishing the connection through perturbative analysis of limiting cases (Sections IV & V).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate and establish an analytic connection between the exact solutions describing the dynamics of a two-level system driven by periodic external fields, focusing on the cases of linear driving and the so-called rotating-wave approximation, or circular driving. In both cases, the exact solutions can be obtained by mapping the Schrodinger equation onto Heun equations: the confluent Heun equation for linear driving and the Heun equation for the rotating-wave case. In particular, we demonstrate a direct analytic connection between the exact solutions for linear driving and those for the rotating-wave case. This result is obtained by analyzing local solutions expressed in terms of hypergeometric functions, which, in the case of the confluent Heun equation, can be derived by considering path-multiplicative Floquet solutions involving a bilateral series. This series leads to two continued-fraction expansions that can be perturbatively solved by imposing a suitable consistency condition. The connection between the linear-driving and rotating-wave solutions is established through a perturbative procedure that allows us to recover not only the rotating-wave approximation itself, but also the correct Stark and Bloch-Siegert shifts, as well as the so-called high-frequency approximation.

</details>

The Levin–Wen regions A1, . . . , A4 are built from these quadrants (see text).</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of identifying topological phases at finite temperature without relying on symmetry breaking. By analyzing the 3D $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code, the authors introduce a novel, robust order parameter called $f_W$. This invariant successfully distinguishes between topologically ordered and trivial phases across complex field-temperature regimes where traditional measures fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a powerful, non-symmetry-breaking diagnostic tool ($f_W$) for characterizing topological order in realistic thermal settings, which is crucial for understanding quantum phase transitions in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining if sharp phase distinctions can be identified for topological order at non-zero temperature ($T>0$) without relying on spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB). The authors seek robust, quasi-local-channel invariants to classify these mixed states.

**Main result.** A new diagnostic, the decoded Wilson-loop correlation $f_W$, is introduced and shown to be a robust invariant that quantizes to 1 in the topological phase and 0 in the trivial phase at finite temperature. This distinguishes phases where traditional measures like entanglement entropy ($\gamma$) fail.

**Method.** The study combines perturbative calculations, large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo simulations on the thermal Gibbs state, and theoretical analysis of various correlation functions (Wilson loops, string order parameters).

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the three-dimensional ($	ext{3D}$) $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code subjected to generic magnetic fields and finite temperature. The Hamiltonian/Gibbs measure describes the thermal state $ho_eta = e^{-eta \hat{H}/Z}$.

**Key observables.** Topological Entanglement Entropy ($\gamma$), Decoded Wilson-loop correlation ($f_W$), Fredenhagen–Marcu string order parameter ($ho_{	ext{FM}}$), and various thermodynamic responses like specific heat ($C_v$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), external magnetic fields $(h_x, h_z)$, and the lattice size $L$. The critical point is often compared to the 3D Ising universality class.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors must contend with the ambiguity of phase equivalence relations for Gibbs states at $T>0$, sometimes assuming quasi-static traversal through Lindbladian channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how different observables (Wilson loops, $ho_{	ext{FM}}$) show differential sensitivity to magnetic, electric, and thermal fields across the $(h_x, h_z, T)$ phase diagram. Table I summarizes diagnostic values for various models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares multiple diagnostics ($\gamma$, Wilson loops, etc.) against known transitions (thermal $T_c$, magnetic $h_x^c$, electric $h_z^c$). It culminates in the proposal and rigorous demonstration of $f_W$ as the universal invariant resolving all three boundaries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the finite-temperature topological order of the three-dimensional $\mathbb{Z}_2$ toric code in a generic magnetic field, where every higher-form symmetry is explicitly broken and can at most be emergent. We show perturbatively, and confirm by large-scale quantum Monte Carlo, that the topological entanglement entropy stays quantized at $γ= \ln 2$ throughout the topological phase -- at finite temperature and under the symmetry-breaking field alike -- and collapses to $0$ across the thermal transition, a quantization protected geometrically by the Bianchi identity rather than by any exact symmetry of the system. The plateau $γ= \ln 2$ is, however, not invariant under quasi-local channels: a constant-depth channel can generate this identical quantized value from a trivial product state. We therefore introduce the decoded Wilson-loop correlation $f_W$, which quantizes to $1$ in the topological phase and $0$ in the trivial phase as $L\to\infty$ and, unlike $γ$, is a quasi-local-channel invariant -- a robust topological invariant of the mixed state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the Twisted Gaussian Schell state, a quantum analogue of structured light beams. It demonstrates that the 'twist' parameter acts as a resource, inducing measurable nonclassical correlations and activating entanglement in multipartite systems even when the state is otherwise separable. The findings establish a deep connection between classical beam properties and fundamental quantum information measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges classical beam engineering concepts (Schell model) with quantum information theory in the context of continuous-variable quantum optics, providing concrete signatures for nonclassicality and entanglement induced by geometric phase terms like the twist.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces and analyzes the Twisted Gaussian Schell (TGS) state, a quantum-optical analog of a classical beam model, to characterize its nonclassicality and entanglement properties.

**Main result.** The TGS state exhibits nonclassicality when the twist parameter is non-zero, and increasing this twist parameter can activate or deepen entanglement in select bipartitions even if the natural bipartition remains separable.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum optics formalism, calculating observables like quadrature variances, logarithmic negativity (entanglement), and joint photon number distributions in both Fock and covariance matrix representations.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-mode TGS state ($\hat{ho}_{	ext{TGS}}$), a mixed Gaussian state generated via metaplectic evolution from an initial thermal state. It is characterized by parameters including local squeezing ($\Omega$), thermal noise ($\kappa$), and the twist phase ($v$).

**Key observables.** Nonclassicality (squeezing below shot-noise limit), Entanglement (Logarithmic Negativity, $	ext{EN}$), Mutual Information ($I$, $I_G$), and Joint Photon Number Distribution $P(n_1, n_2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist parameter ($v$), local squeezing ($\Omega$), thermal noise ($\kappa$); the analysis explores regimes where these parameters dictate separability or entanglement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the formal equivalence between paraxial classical optics and continuous-variable quantum systems, and uses criteria like PPT for Gaussian state separability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the generation scheme (Fig. 1), density plots of mutual information (Fig. 2), joint photon distributions $P(n_1, n_2)$ (Fig. 3), and maps showing entanglement regions in the $(\kappa, v)$ plane (Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the TGS state by first establishing its generation scheme, then characterizing nonclassicality via quadrature squeezing, followed by detailed investigations into separability and entanglement across various bipartitions using both photon number statistics and covariance matrix methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the Twisted Gaussian Schell (TGS) state, a two-mode mixed Gaussian state defined as the quantum-optical analog of the Twisted Gaussian Schell-model beam of classical paraxial optics, characterized by the so-called twist phase. In the TGS state, the twist parameter arises when an asymmetric two-mode thermal state is subject to local squeezing after the action of phase shifters and a beam splitter. Its defining quantum feature is nonclassicality: although the state is separable in its natural bipartition, when the twist parameter is nonzero there are global quadratures that can be squeezed below the shot-noise limit. The nonclassicality has also a direct signature in the joint photon-number distribution, which we obtain in closed form. Moreover, coupling each mode to an ancillary vacuum at a balanced beam splitter yields a four-mode state with entanglement in select $2\times2$ bipartitions, with local description given by two TGS states, and all $1\times3$ bipartitions.   For fixed input squeezing, increasing the twist parameter activates entanglement where the state is otherwise separable and deepens it where already present. The classical physicality bound on the twist parameter coincides with the quantum physicality condition. These results advance the two-way bridge between classical beam engineering and quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work examines how scalar chirality modifies two-magnon excitations in a triangular lattice magnet. The key finding is that chirality acts as an effective interaction, selectively strengthening the binding of one specific two-magnon state. This mechanism provides a microscopic route to inducing spin-nematic instabilities without affecting the fundamental single-particle magnetic gap.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the methodology—using symmetry analysis and solving coupled excitation equations (gap equations)—is highly relevant for understanding collective excitations in strongly correlated quantum systems beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how a uniform scalar-chirality interaction affects two-magnon bound states in a spin-1/2 triangular-lattice magnet.

**Main result.** The chirality term selectively enhances the binding of one specific two-magnon channel by splitting opposite relative-motion chiralities, without altering the single-magnon spectrum.

**Method.** The analysis uses symmetry-adapted partial-wave decomposition to derive finite-dimensional gap equations for the two-magnon sector, confirmed by exact diagonalization.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 triangular-lattice Heisenberg model with $J_1, J_2, J_3$ exchange interactions, augmented by a scalar-chirality term ($\hat{H}_{	ext{Chir}}$). The analysis focuses on excitations above the fully polarized state.

**Key observables.** Two-magnon binding energy ($\Delta$), one-magnon dispersion relation, and the resulting spin-nematic instability signatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange couplings ($J_1, J_2, J_3$) and the chirality coupling strength ($J_\chi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the high-field limit above the fully polarized state; it focuses on leading pair channels (two-magnon states).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate parameter space loci for various magnetic ordering manifolds and show gap contours derived from the $K=0$ gap equation, demonstrating enhanced binding at specific coupling values.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the one-magnon sector to show chirality cancels out there. It then tackles the two-magnon sector by deriving a partial-wave decomposition of the interaction kernel, leading to solvable gap equations that reveal chiral enhancement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study one- and two-magnon excitations above the fully polarized state of the spin-$1/2$ triangular-lattice $J_1$-$J_2$-$J_3$ Heisenberg model with an additional uniform scalar-chirality interaction. In the one-magnon sector of the Heisenberg model, we identify two special minimum manifolds by rewriting the dispersion in complete-square form. The scalar-chirality term cancels exactly in this sector, leaving the one-magnon dispersion and the single-magnon instability unchanged. In contrast, it survives in the two-magnon sector as an oriented interaction between neighboring flipped spins. Using symmetry-adapted triangular-lattice harmonics, we derive finite-dimensional gap equations at the $Γ$ point in the symmetry-resolved $\mathsf{A_1}$ and $\mathsf{E_2}$-type partial-wave channels. The chirality coupling splits the two opposite relative-motion chiralities in the $\mathsf{E_2}$-type sector, thereby selectively enhancing one two-magnon bound-state channel. Exact diagonalization confirms this mechanism and reveals enhanced binding, as well as additional bound states at $M$ and at incommensurate total momenta. Our results identify scalar chirality as an efficient microscopic mechanism for strengthening two-magnon binding without shifting the one-magnon spectrum, and provide a route toward high-field spin-nematic and multipolar instabilities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the difficult task of non-perturbatively simulating Maxwell-Chern-Simons theory by analyzing its constant mode sector on a torus lattice. By mapping the problem onto a Hofstadter model, the authors demonstrate that the finite lattice spectrum correctly reproduces the system's fundamental topological degeneracy in the continuum limit. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding how global topological invariants manifest in discretized quantum Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of topological gauge theories like MCS theory provides an excellent model for understanding emergent quantum symmetries and ground state degeneracies in strongly interacting lattice systems, relevant to fractional quantum Hall physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a non-perturbative Hamiltonian approach for Maxwell-Chern-Simons (MCS) theory in 2+1 dimensions, which is challenging due to the sign problem.

**Main result.** The study shows that the lattice spectrum exhibits quasi-degeneracies whose vanishing spread in the continuum limit correctly recovers the global topological degeneracy predicted by the CS level $k$.

**Method.** The authors analyze the zero-mode sector on a spatial torus, discretizing it to map the problem onto a generalized Harper-Hofstadter model with twisted boundary conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is the MCS theory defined on a lattice torus. The Hamiltonian is analyzed by focusing on the flat (zero-mode) sector, which governs the topological properties of the gauge field.

**Key observables.** Topological degeneracy ($k$-fold), energy spectrum structure, and the convergence rate of quasi-degenerate energy clusters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The CS level $k$ (determining flux $\Phi_{tot} = 2\pi k$), lattice size ($N_x, N_y$), and the ratio of flux to area ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the constant mode sector (flat connections) as a solvable benchmark. The continuum limit requires the flux per plaquette $\alpha$ to be small.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the convergence of energy level spacing towards harmonic oscillator behavior and show the emergence of $k$-fold quasi-degenerate multiplets in finite lattices, particularly when commensurability conditions are met.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical motivation (Hamiltonian approach to MCS theory) to analyzing the zero-mode sector on a torus. It details the lattice discretization, derives the effective Harper-type Hamiltonian, and systematically analyzes spectral properties under different flux/lattice commensurability regimes ($	ext{gcd}(k, N)$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maxwell-Chern-Simons (MCS) theory in $2+1$ dimensions provides a paradigmatic example of a topological gauge theory with both dynamical and topological degrees of freedom. Its Euclidean formulation suffers from a sign problem, making Hamiltonian numerical approaches particularly attractive. As a first step toward the non-perturbative Hamiltonian study of MCS theory, we investigate the constant mode sector on a spatial torus. Being analytically solvable in the continuum, it provides an ideal benchmark for understanding how the topological properties of the theory are encoded in a finite-dimensional lattice Hilbert space. We construct a finite-dimensional discretization of the torus of flat connections and show that the resulting lattice problem maps onto a generalized Harper-Hofstadter model with twisted boundary conditions. We identify the commensurability conditions under which the finite lattice exactly reproduces the magnetic translation algebra and the topological degeneracy of the continuum theory. A systematic analysis of gauge field truncation and its convergence toward the continuum limit is then presented.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously determines the conditions under which long-range quantum spin chains and ladders possess an exact dimer ground state. By analyzing Hamiltonian structure using semi-positivity criteria and extending these methods to anisotropic interactions, the authors map out complex phase diagrams. The findings provide both theoretical boundaries and numerical confirmations for understanding highly correlated magnetic phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework used—defining physical phases through convex cones and spectral positivity—provides powerful tools applicable to understanding stability in complex, interacting many-body systems beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is determining precise, explicit conditions on coupling constants that guarantee an exact dimer state is the ground state for long-range interacting spin chains and ladders.

**Main result.** The authors derived sufficient conditions ($\alpha_n \ge 0$, $eta_{2n} \ge 0$, and $eta_{2n-1} = 0$) that define regions in the phase diagram where the dimer state is guaranteed, extending these results to anisotropic interactions.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical derivation of necessary/sufficient conditions (e.g., semi-positivity arguments) with validation using exact diagonalization on finite systems and geometric interpretations via convex cones.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on interacting quantum spin chains and ladders described by long-range Hamiltonians, which are generalized to include anisotropic interactions ($\gamma_\mu$). The system is analyzed in terms of pseudospin operators and related linear exchange models.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_{	ext{GS}}$), Dimer entanglement entropy ($\Delta S_{	ext{dimer}}$), and the coefficients $\alpha_n$ and $eta_n$ derived from the Hamiltonian structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants $\{J_n\}$ and $\{K_n\}$, anisotropy parameters ($\gamma_x, \gamma_y, \gamma_z$), and system length $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an even length $L$ for simplicity; while sufficient conditions are derived, numerical results suggest the actual dimer phase can extend beyond these theoretical boundaries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing predicted vs. numerically found regions for the dimer state in both isotropic and anisotropic chains/ladders, using energy and entanglement entropy as diagnostics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing general Hamiltonian forms (chains/ladders) to deriving necessary conditions via semi-positivity arguments ($\alpha_n, eta_n$). It then extends this analysis to include anisotropy by analyzing the spectrum of an asymmetric quantum top and finally validates results using ED on specific models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interacting spin chains and ladders are known to support a plethora of quantum phases with complex ground-state phase diagrams. In this work, we study a large family of such models and determine precise, explicit conditions under which an exact dimer state is guaranteed to be the ground state. These general conditions are validated for various generalizations of the Majumdar-Ghosh model using exact diagonalization. Our results provide exact reference points in the phase diagrams of a wide class of spin chains and ladders, including those with anisotropic and arbitrary-range interactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes slow heat conduction in hard disk gases by coupling hydrodynamic theory with molecular dynamics simulations. The authors successfully extend the theoretical framework beyond the ideal gas limit to account for finite density effects using an appropriate equation of state. The strong agreement between theory and simulation validates the use of isobaric hydrodynamics for strongly inhomogeneous, low-Mach number cooling flows.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on classical fluid dynamics, the rigorous comparison between continuum hydrodynamics (derived from statistical principles) and microscopic particle simulations provides a powerful benchmark for developing non-equilibrium transport theories applicable to quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates slow, heat-driven non-equilibrium flow in a gas of hard disks confined to a channel under nearly isobaric conditions.

**Main result.** The hydrodynamic theory, extended to finite densities using a hard-disk equation of state, shows excellent agreement with event-driven molecular dynamics simulations across both dilute and dense regimes.

**Method.** The authors solve coupled one-dimensional partial differential equations derived from the low-Mach number isobaric heat-conduction formalism and compare these solutions against particle-level Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a gas of elastically colliding hard disks confined to a long channel, modeled using hydrodynamic equations that incorporate non-ideal equation of state corrections for finite densities.

**Key observables.** Temperature T(x, t), Density $\rho(x, t)$, Velocity $v(x, t)$, and Pressure $p(x, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low Mach number regime; initial conditions featuring large temperature/density contrasts but nearly equal pressures ($p \approx 	ext{const}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The flow is assumed to be low-Mach number (neglecting acoustic dynamics), and the theory extends from an ideal gas limit to finite densities using a specific equation of state.

**Figures summary.** Figures show self-similar scaling collapse of density, velocity, and temperature profiles when comparing MD data to hydrodynamic solutions; pressure plots confirm the system remains nearly isobaric over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ideal gas limit theory, then extends it by incorporating a non-ideal equation of state for finite densities. The resulting 1D equations are solved numerically and validated against event-driven molecular dynamics simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a slow heat-driven flow in a gas of elastically colliding hard disks confined to a long channel. The initial state consists of two regions with large temperature and density contrasts but nearly equal pressures, leading to a low-Mach-number, nearly isobaric evolution. In the dilute limit, the corresponding isobaric hydrodynamic theory reduces to a previously known ideal-gas description. We extend this theory to finite densities by incorporating a non-ideal equation of state of a hard-disk fluid, and solve the resulting one-dimensional equations numerically. Finite-density effects produce appreciable deviations from the ideal-gas prediction. We then test the theory directly against event-driven molecular dynamics simulations of hard disks and find very good agreement in both the dilute and finite-density regimes. The results provide, to our knowledge, the first particle-level test of isobaric gas dynamics of a strongly inhomogeneous cooling flow.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors resolve a conflict between experimental measurements and Bohmian mechanics concerning particle speeds in classically forbidden regions. They show that naive estimates based on tunneling time or simple dwell times are incorrect because they neglect entanglement effects between spatial dimensions. By correctly applying the Büttiker dwell time formula, they demonstrate quantitative agreement with Bohmian predictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental conceptual issues regarding quantum measurement, non-equilibrium dynamics, and the interpretation of wave function properties (like speed) in regimes where classical intuition breaks down, which is central to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves discrepancies between particle speeds inferred from experiments (Sharoglazova et al.) and predictions from Bohmian mechanics when a quantum particle tunnels through a classically forbidden region.

**Main result.** The discrepancy arises because naive speed estimates fail to account for entanglement between longitudinal and transverse degrees of freedom in the evanescent regime. A corrected application of the Büttiker dwell time formula yields results that perfectly agree with Bohmian predictions.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving detailed calculations of Bohmian trajectories, average speeds, and dwell times using a two-dimensional waveguide model derived from the Schrödinger equation.

**Model / system.** A non-relativistic quantum particle modeled in coupled waveguides realizing motion in a 2D potential landscape with a barrier/potential step. The dynamics are governed by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation.

**Key observables.** Bohmian velocity, average speed ($ar{v}_B$), tunneling time ($T_{tun}$), and dwell time ($	au_d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evanescent regime (exponential decay), coupling constants ($\mu, ar{\mu}$), energy splitting ($J_0$), and wave packet width ($\sigma_k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption challenged is that transverse tunneling time is independent of the longitudinal degree of freedom's entanglement effects in the evanescent regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Bohmian trajectories (parabolic paths) and compare density distributions, showing differences between propagative and evanescent regimes, particularly regarding wave function factorization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the conflict with experimental results, then developing theoretical tools (Bohmian mechanics, dwell time corrections), and finally resolving the puzzle by analyzing how entanglement invalidates simple tunneling time assumptions in the forbidden region.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, Sharoglazova et al. [Nature 643, 67 (2025)] proposed a procedure for determining the speed of a quantum particle in the classically forbidden region of a potential step, and implemented it in a beautiful experiment. The inferred speeds disagree significantly with the Bohmian velocities, which the authors presented as an experimental challenge to Bohmian mechanics. This is puzzling because the speeds are inferred from particle populations in coupled waveguides for which Bohmian mechanics and standard quantum mechanics make identical predictions. We resolve the puzzle by a detailed theoretical analysis of the experimental setup. We show that the speed inference rests on an assumption that fails in the relevant (evanescent) regime according to both Bohmian mechanics and standard quantum mechanics -- namely that the inter-waveguide tunneling time is set by the transverse coupling and is not affected by entanglement with the longitudinal degree of freedom. We also consider a second speed estimate suggested by Sharoglazova et al., which is based on the Büttiker dwell time formula for particles in the forbidden region. We show that the authors applied the formula incorrectly, and that a correct application yields exact agreement with the predictions of Bohmian mechanics. Our analysis includes explicit calculations of Bohmian trajectories, dwell times, and longitudinal speeds in the two-dimensional waveguide model of the experiment.

</details>

### [Leggett-Garg inequality in the massive scalar vacuum: No violation under spacelike-separated measurements](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00314v1)

**Authors:** Yang Xiang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00314v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5**

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. K3(τ, L) for the massive scalar ﬁeld with spacelike-separated measurements (L = τ +2+ǫ, ǫ = 10−3) for various masses m. The thin horizontal line marks the classical bound K3 = 1; for all masses considered, K3 remains strictly below 1. For each mass, the maximum occurs at the smallest accessible τ (τ = 0.001), and Kmax 3 decreases monotonically as m increases, from Kmax 3 ≈0.716 at m = 0.001 down to 0.403 at m = 0.4. For τ ≥2, the two time windows no longer overlap; the curves decay exponentially with τ, as expected from the spacelike decay of the massive Hadamard function.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates whether the vacuum state of a massive scalar field violates the Leggett-Garg inequality (LGI) when measurements are strictly spacelike separated. By employing a protocol that rigorously satisfies noninvasive measurability, the authors calculate the LGI parameter $K_3$ and find it remains below the classical bound of 1. This result highlights a fundamental distinction between spatial entanglement violations (Bell tests) and temporal macrorealism in relativistic quantum fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous methodological benchmark by successfully adapting Bell/LGI tests to QFT while satisfying causality constraints. The contrast between violating spatial Bell inequalities and preserving temporal macrorealism is highly relevant for understanding quantum foundations in relativistic settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to test for macrorealism using the Leggett-Garg inequality (LGI) in a relativistic quantum field theory setting, specifically by ensuring noninvasive measurability (NIM) through spacelike separation.

**Main result.** Under strict spacelike separation conditions, the vacuum state of a free massive scalar field does not violate the LGI ($K_3 < 1$), establishing that macrorealism is preserved for Gaussian fields in this context.

**Method.** The authors use a protocol involving three independent ensembles measured at spacelike-separated events to calculate the LGI parameter $K_3$ from two-time correlation functions derived using the Heisenberg picture.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a free massive scalar field ($\phi$) in 1+1 dimensions. The test utilizes dichotomic observables defined by the sign of smeared field values, $	ext{sign}(\phi(f))$.

**Key observables.** The primary diagnostic is the LGI parameter $K_3 = 2C(	au, L) - C(2	au, 2L)$, derived from two-time correlation functions $C(	au, L)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mass ($m$), time separation ($	au$), and spatial separation ($L$). The condition $L > 	au + 2	au_0$ ensures spacelike separation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol strictly satisfies the Noninvasive Measurability (NIM) condition by ensuring all measurement events are spacelike separated. Calculations rely on the Gaussian nature of the vacuum state for free fields.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots $K_3(	au, L)$ versus $	au$, showing that $K_3 < 1$ and demonstrating exponential decay for large time separations ($	au \ge 2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by defining the LGI test in QFT with NIM. It then calculates the necessary two-time correlation functions using techniques appropriate for spacelike separation, culminating in a numerical demonstration that $K_3$ remains below the classical bound of 1.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We overcome the long-standing noninvasive measurability (NIM) challenge in Leggett-Garg tests by exploiting the causal structure of quantum field theory (QFT). Our protocol uses three independent ensembles of the vacuum state, each measured by a different pair of observers at spacelike-separated events, yielding the three two-time correlators. By placing these events at positions $(0,0)$, $(τ,L)$, and $(2τ,2L)$ with $L>τ+2τ_0$, we rigorously ensure that no measurement can influence another. We investigate the vacuum state of a free massive scalar field in 1+1 dimensions, employing the dichotomic observable $Q(f)=\operatorname{sign}(φ(f))$ where $φ(f)$ is the smeared field. In the Heisenberg picture, the time evolution is absorbed into a translation of the time-window function, allowing us to derive the two-time correlation function $C(τ,L)$ and the Leggett-Garg parameter $K_3=2C(τ,L)-C(2τ,2L)$. For non-overlapping time windows, we find that the correlation function decays exponentially with $τ$ for a massive field. For overlapping windows, our numerical computation for a rectangular time window yields $K_3<1$ across the entire mass range, firmly establishing that the vacuum does not violate the LGI. Thus, under strict noninvasive conditions, the vacuum shows no violation of macrorealism, in stark contrast to its well-known violation of spatial Bell inequalities. Our spacelike-separated protocol provides the first LGI test in QFT with rigorously satisfied NIM, setting a methodological benchmark for future studies and highlighting the fundamental distinction between spacelike entanglement and temporal macrorealism in relativistic quantum fields.

</details>

**Authors:** F. X. Liu, Z. Song

The triplet pair presented in this work is formed across two nearest-neighbor sites on two layers (labeled by a red dashed line surrounding the two sites), while the singlet pair is an on-site pair (labeled by a red dashed...</figcaption>

**Summary.** The authors propose and derive exact interlayer triplet-pairing eigenstates for the multi-layer extended Hubbard model. These unique states only exist in bilayer and trilayer systems due to specific constraints on the Hamiltonian's algebra. The study confirms these states via time evolution simulations, highlighting a coexistence of singlet and triplet pairing mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous analytical framework for finding exotic, long-range ordered states in strongly correlated lattice models, which is highly relevant for understanding high-$T_c$ superconductivity mechanisms beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates the existence and properties of exact interlayer triplet-pairing eigenstates within the multi-layer extended Hubbard model, extending prior knowledge on $\eta$-pairing states.

**Main result.** Exact condensate-pair eigenstates exhibiting off-diagonal long-range order are found for bilayer and trilayer systems when interlayer interactions are present. These states demonstrate a unique pairing mechanism distinct from the standard $\eta$-pairing symmetry.

**Method.** The analysis relies on constructing these exact eigenstates by analyzing commutation relations involving the Hamiltonian and specialized triplet-pairing operators, utilizing a Restricted Spectrum Generating Algebra (RSGA).

**Model / system.** The system is the multi-layer extended Hubbard model defined on a 2D square lattice with parameters including intralayer hopping ($t$), interlayer hopping ($t_\perp$), on-site repulsion ($U$), and interlayer interaction ($V$). The Hamiltonian incorporates both singlet ($\eta$) and triplet ($\zeta$) pairing channels.

**Key observables.** Off-diagonal long-range order (ODLRO), fidelity $F(t)$ under time evolution, and correlation functions for singlet ($C_S$) and triplet ($C_T$) pairs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The existence of exact states is critically dependent on the number of layers ($\lambda=2$ or $\lambda=3$) and the presence of interlayer interactions ($V \neq 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation requires restricting Hamiltonian parameters to satisfy the conditions necessary for applying the Restricted Spectrum Generating Algebra (RSGA).

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates that the fidelity $F(t)$ remains unity for $\lambda=2, 3$ when $V \neq 0$, while decaying for $\lambda=4$. Figure 3 shows correlation functions decaying when interlayer interactions are present.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the model and operators, derives the conditions for exact eigenstates using RSGA, proves their existence only for bilayer/trilayer systems, and finally uses quench dynamics simulations (fidelity analysis) to confirm these theoretical findings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

$η$-pairing symmetry generalizes the pairing mechanisms in superconductivity but is broken in the presence of interlayer interactions. In this work, we extend this approach to triplet pairs. We propose interlayer triplet-pairing operators for the multi-layer extended Hubbard model. We find that a set of exact condensate-pair eigenstates can be constructed, which exhibit off-diagonal long-range order. In contrast to the $η$-pairing mechanism, this originates from restricted spectrum generating algebra and is only available for bilayer and trilayer systems in the presence of interlayer Hubbard interactions. Nevertheless, the system also retains the original on-site $η$-pairing symmetry in the absence of interlayer interactions. Consequently, both singlet and triplet pairs coexist in the eigenstates of the multi-layer Hubbard model. We employ quench dynamics to demonstrate the results through numerical simulations. Our findings open avenues for the study of exact condensate-pair states in strongly correlated systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive and mathematically rigorous classification of three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes, demonstrating that these paradoxical structures are far richer than previously thought. Using graph theory and combinatorial techniques, the authors map out the precise conditions under which quantum correlations violate classical expectations. This advances our understanding of fundamental quantum advantages beyond simple state examples.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum information theory, the rigorous mathematical classification of fundamental resource measures like nonlocality provides deep insights into the structure of quantum correlations that underpin many-body physics models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To completely classify all three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes established via a biconditional parity proof, extending beyond previously known examples like the GHZ paradox.

**Main result.** The authors provide a complete structural classification for these paradoxes, showing that they are abundant and violate prior regularity assumptions. They also present explicit examples of paradoxes arising from states outside the interpolant family.

**Method.** The work employs advanced techniques combining graph theory (2-CNF formulae, implication graphs) with combinatorial analysis to characterize logical obstructions in quantum correlations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on three-qubit quantum systems and the concept of 'nonlocality paradoxes,' which are logical contradictions arising when assuming classical probabilistic models for quantum measurement outcomes.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal fraction, unsatisfiable sets of constraints derived from impossible measurement contexts (e.g., $\Omega(z)$ or $\Psi(z)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n=3$), the structure of the measurements (equatorial settings), and parameters defining the state $|	ext{B}(\lambda, \Phi)angle$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial classification focused on paradoxes admitting a biconditional parity proof; later work challenges assumptions about interpolant states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of balanced quantum states and schematic representations of the $eta$-equation relating angles to impossible measurement outcomes for various qubit assignments.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining strong nonlocality via contextuality, establishing initial classifications using graph theory (2-SAT), developing structural tools like 'Charlie clocks,' and culminating in classifying paradoxes both within and outside the interpolant state family.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum nonlocality paradoxes, such as that of GHZ, provide maximally sharp logical obstructions to classical probabilistic models of quantum correlations. They are key resources in a broad variety of information-theoretic tasks that exhibit unconditional quantum advantage. For example, in nonlocal games, which are communication tasks that serve as core technical tools in recent landmark results in quantum computational complexity theory.   Their role in establishing quantum advantage motivated their study by Abramsky et al. who introduced an infinite family of three-qubit paradoxes exhibiting novel conditional structure. This was later extended by de Silva et al. into a full classification program.   In this work, we completely classify all three-qubit nonlocality paradoxes established via a biconditional parity proof; this is a very large class of paradoxes that encompasses all earlier-known examples. We do this by introducing a suite of new structural and combinatorial techniques. We find that the landscape of nonlocality paradoxes is far richer than previously understood, violating regularity conditions underlying all prior constructions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the challenge of imposing symmetry in Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) by showing that symmetry must be enforced across all computational interfaces—encoding, dynamics, measurement, and readout. By introducing 'observable-orbit completion,' the authors demonstrate that aligning these four components yields substantial performance gains on forecasting tasks using both simulated and real-world data.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, actionable blueprint for how to incorporate physical symmetries into complex quantum machine learning models, moving beyond simple Hamiltonian symmetry constraints to address measurement limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** In Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC), simply making the quantum reservoir symmetric is insufficient; the relevant symmetry must be explicitly visible in the final measured feature map for successful training.

**Main result.** Achieving optimal performance requires enforcing cyclic equivariance across all four architectural interfaces—encoding, dynamics, measurement, and readout—a concept termed 'observable-orbit completion'.

**Method.** The authors developed an 'observable-orbit completion' protocol that systematically measures symmetry-related observable channels across the entire system architecture to ensure consistency.

**Model / system.** The study uses QRC applied to cyclic time-series forecasting tasks (e.g., weather data on a latitude circle) implemented via quantum circuits, tested on spin-ring models and real-world datasets like WeatherBench 2.

**Key observables.** Normalized Root Mean Square Error (NRMSE) reduction; quantitative performance gains (%) when comparing fully aligned vs. baseline configurations across the four interfaces (E/H/O/R).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The feature set size is fixed at 61 features per site, comprising various Pauli channels and context features.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes a candidate symmetry group derived from task geometry or domain knowledge; the gains are shown to be robust across different physical implementations (spin-ring, real weather data).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate performance comparisons showing significant gains when all four interfaces are aligned compared to scrambled baselines. Table III quantifies these partial and full alignment gains.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical necessity of symmetry reaching the measurement layer, details the 'four-interface audit' framework (E/H/O/R), presents quantitative results across multiple forecasting tasks (L96, Advection, WeatherBench 2), and validates findings on both simulated spin-rings and actual hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Symmetry is a powerful inductive bias, but in quantum reservoir computing (QRC) it cannot be imposed only by making the reservoir symmetric. QRC maps inputs through fixed quantum dynamics into nonlinear expectation-value features and trains only a classical readout, so the relevant symmetry must be visible in the measured feature map. We study cyclic forecasting tasks, such as sensors around a turbine or weather stations along a latitude circle, where the same local pattern should be forecast by the same rule wherever it appears on the ring. Thus, rotating the input by one site should rotate, not change, the predicted field. We show that a symmetric Hamiltonian is not enough: even large Pauli measurement sets can fail if their channels do not match the data symmetry, since optimization cannot recover channels that were never measured. We address this through observable-orbit completion, which measures symmetry-related observable channels and aligns encoding, dynamics, measurement, and readout. The strongest gains arise from aligning all four interfaces together, with matched spin-ring, real-weather, and IBM hardware checks supporting the same measured-span mechanism.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that common classical approximations for simulating noise in quantum networks—such as Pauli twirling or using reset channels—introduce severe errors when predicting protocol performance. By analyzing entanglement purification and swapping, the authors show that neglecting coherent error terms leads to qualitative failures, proving that rigorous validation of complete noise architectures is essential for reliable quantum technology predictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** For a theoretical physicist working on open quantum systems, this work provides concrete examples of how the choice of error model in simulation can fundamentally alter predictions about achievable quantum communication thresholds, emphasizing the necessity of retaining full system coherence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simplified error models used for simulating large-scale quantum networks can lead to severe quantitative and qualitative errors in predicting protocol performance.

**Main result.** Neglecting coherent error contributions, such as off-diagonal Pauli terms or systematic over-rotations, causes approximations (like Pauli Twirling) to fail dramatically compared to exact simulations. These failures manifest as outcome-dependent fidelity discrepancies across iterative protocols.

**Method.** The authors compare the performance of quantum network protocols (e.g., entanglement purification, swapping) simulated using full error models against those using simplified noise approximations like Pauli twirling or reset channels.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on simulating quantum networks and communication protocols such as entanglement purification (DEJMPS protocol), entanglement swapping, and repeater chains. The underlying physics involves noisy operations modeled by various types of noise, including amplitude damping and coherent over-rotations.

**Key observables.** Fidelity and Negativity are the primary diagnostics used to quantify protocol performance across different measurement outcomes (e.g., 00 vs 11).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters such as depolarizing noise strength ($p_{	ext{dep}}$), amplitude damping rates ($\gamma$), and over-rotation angles ($\phi$) are varied.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core limitation explored is the assumption that error channels can be accurately mapped onto diagonal Pauli channels or simple reset approximations, which fundamentally ignores coherent off-diagonal noise correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare fidelity and negativity for entanglement purification (Fig. 3), show the divergence between amplitude damping and reset channel models (Fig. 5), and track performance degradation across repeater chains using both exact and approximated error channels (Fig. 8, 11).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes protocols like EPP and swapping, contrasting results derived from tracking individual measurement outcomes (retaining coherence) versus averaging or applying simplified diagonal noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient classical simulation of large-scale quantum networks frequently relies on noise approximations, which consider a restricted set of operators to describe noisy channels and operations. In this work, we demonstrate how such simplified error models, such as Pauli twirling or reset channels, can lead to severe quantitative and qualitative discrepancies in protocol performance predictions. We analyze, in particular, how small differences can accumulate in iterative and sequential protocols such as entanglement purification, entanglement swapping, and repeater chains. Our results reveal that neglected error contributions can lead to important performance under- and over-estimations, measurement-outcome dependency, and oscillations in the fidelity, which are entirely overlooked by the simplified error model approximations. These results show that rigorous validation of complete noise architectures is indispensable for accurately predicting operational thresholds in future quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses advanced statistical methods to analyze single-cell measurements from bacterial growth. By examining probability distributions and correlations among size and time metrics, the authors demonstrate that one can uniquely determine whether cell division follows a Timer, Sizer, or Adder rule, and whether the underlying growth is linear or exponential. This provides a powerful diagnostic tool for understanding fundamental biophysical processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is biology, the rigorous application of stochastic processes, moment analysis, and distribution fitting to distinguish underlying physical laws mirrors techniques used in analyzing complex non-equilibrium systems in statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to differentiate between various underlying cell-division (e.g., Sizer, Adder) and single-cell growth models (linear vs. exponential) in microbial cells.

**Main result.** Analysis of probability distributions, statistical relationships (especially involving standard deviations), and correlations among single-cell quantities can uniquely distinguish between different division and growth mechanisms.

**Method.** The authors derive analytical probability distributions and statistical moments for key observables by assuming various underlying mechanistic models. These theoretical predictions are then compared against published experimental data.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bacterial cell growth and division, modeled using concepts like Timer, Sizer, and Adder mechanisms. The analysis extends to stochastic single-cell growth and asymmetric partitioning.

**Key observables.** Single-cell quantities: birth-size ($s_b$), division-size ($s_d$), division-time ($	au_d$), and division-added-size ($\Delta d$). Statistical measures include probability distributions, means, standard deviations, and correlation coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Growth rate autocorrelation parameters ($\alpha$, $eta$); temperature variations (e.g., $25^\circ	ext{C}, 37^\circ	ext{C}$); partitioning fraction ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes independence between certain variables (like growth rate and size parameters) or relies on approximations like assuming sharply spiked distributions for moment calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare experimental single-cell distributions ($s_b, s_d, \Delta d, 	au_d$) against analytical predictions from various model combinations. Plots of correlations (PCC) are used to test the consistency of different models across multiple temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing that simple correlations are insufficient, then showing how full probability distributions and statistical relationships (means vs. standard deviations) provide stronger discriminative power between division/growth models, culminating in comparisons with experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Complex interactions among regulatory molecules determine the rules underlying cell growth and division in microbial cells. While the governing molecular network may not always be obvious, it is well known that correlations among certain physiological quantities measured in experiments, such as birth-size, division-size, division-time, and division-added-size, can differentiate among various cell-division models, such as Timer, Sizer, and Adder. Here we show that, apart from these correlations, which we extend for the case of stochastic single-cell growth and stochastic asymmetric partitioning, probability distributions of these quantities and statistical relationships between them can also be used to differentiate between these division models. Interestingly, we show that these quantities can not only differentiate the division models, but also distinguish among the single-cell growth paradigms, such as linear and exponential growth. We then demonstrate this differentiability among various division and growth models by comparing our analytical results with published experimental data. We further show that these results remain valid even when the growth rate of a cell is correlated with the growth rate of cells from previous generations in the lineage.

</details>

### [Demographic senescence as multi-level selection in miniature](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00262v1)

**Authors:** Ananda Shikhara Bhat, Hanna Kokko  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00262v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

(A) The model consists of M organisms, each of which have N intra-organismal sub-systems capable of failure. (B) An organism with f ∈{0, 1</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper mathematically proves that demographic senescence can be modeled as a multi-level selection process. By developing a two-level stochastic model and applying advanced scaling limits, the authors derive an SPDE that governs how physiological damage accumulation (low level) drives differential survival rates (high level). This unifies concepts from evolutionary biology and aging theory into a single mathematical framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical mapping between biological aging (senescence) and fundamental evolutionary dynamics like MLS provides a powerful, general framework applicable across different complex systems exhibiting trait-dependent mortality.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To formally demonstrate that the emergent mortality patterns observed during demographic senescence can be mathematically understood as a consequence of multi-level selection.

**Main result.** The authors establish a formal equivalence between senescence and MLS by deriving a limiting stochastic partial differential equation (SPDE) governing the population composition.

**Method.** The methodology involves formulating a two-level Moran-type process, recasting it into a ball-and-urn scheme, and applying system-size expansion/diffusion approximations to derive continuum limits.

**Model / system.** The model tracks the state of $M$ systems (organisms), each containing $N$ sub-systems. Dynamics occur at two levels: within-system changes (warming/cooling) and between-system selection (differential mortality risk).

**Key observables.** Emergent mortality patterns, failure accumulation curves, and the limiting probability density function $\zeta_\lambda(f, t)$ describing population composition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include $M$ (number of systems), $N$ (sub-systems per system), and the scaling ratio $\lambda = N/M$. The replacement probability $\varrho$ is also crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on diffusion approximations, functional central limit theorems, and taking specific limits ($M 	o \infty$, $N 	o \infty$) to simplify the discrete stochastic process into continuous PDEs/SPDEs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the two-level dynamics: (A) showing $M$ organisms with $N$ sub-systems; (B) depicting failure accumulation and death; and (C) characterizing the system via a ball-and-urn scheme tracking failure proportions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the discrete stochastic process using a Master Equation, deriving its functional Fokker-Planck equation via diffusion approximation, and finally taking multiple scaling limits ($M 	o \infty$, $N 	o \infty$) to arrive at the final limiting SPDE governing the superprocess $\zeta_\lambda$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-level selection and senescence do not at first sight have much in common. Here, we demonstrate that the emergent mortality patterns generated by demographic senescence can be understood as the product of multi-level selection. We formulate a two-level Moran type process and use its scaling limits to illustrate that a simple mathematical framework that models multi-level selection in group-structured populations also models damage accumulation patterns and resultant mortality curves in ageing organisms. To verbally make the connection, observe that defectors spread within a group consisting of cooperators and defectors; when groups compete against each other, defector-rich groups suffer, and between-group selection causes such groups to be systematically under-represented. Exactly analogously, senescing individuals accumulate damage to physiological sub-systems, and `damage begets damage'; individuals who are more damaged are more likely to die, hence damage-rich individuals are systematically under-represented in later age classes. Thus, emergent senescence patterns in complex, integrated organisms are formally equivalent to the patterns generated by a within-generation multi-level selection process in which intra-organismal sub-systems play the role of particles, organisms play the role of collectives, and selective disappearance plays the role of group selection.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates a double-occupancy cell fluid model interacting via Curie-Weiss forces. By analyzing its grand canonical partition function, the authors map out a complex phase diagram showing multiple coexistence regimes. The results demonstrate how simple competition between local repulsion and long-range attraction can generate rich physics, including liquid-liquid transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the mapping to the Blume-Capel model connects this work to quantum spin systems, making it relevant for understanding phase transitions in lattice models that share mathematical structure with quantum Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the complex phase diagram of a fluid modeled by a double-occupancy cell model interacting via Curie-Weiss type long-range forces.

**Main result.** The competition between local repulsion and global attraction generates rich thermodynamic behavior, including regimes with single or two critical points, tricritical points, and triple points, leading to gas-liquid and liquid-liquid coexistence.

**Method.** The analysis employs the grand canonical ensemble formalism, utilizing analytical techniques combined with numerical methods, notably applying Laplace's method to evaluate integrals in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a double-occupancy cell fluid model where each site can hold up to two particles. This model is shown to be isomorphic to the Blume-Capel model on a complete graph.

**Key observables.** Phase diagram structure, critical points $(T^*_c; ho^*_c)$, tricritical points, triple points, and the pair distribution function $g(2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase behavior is critically dependent on the ratio $a = \Delta/J$, which controls the balance between local repulsion ($\Delta$) and global attraction ($J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations in the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$) and uses mean-field descriptions, meaning it neglects local structural ordering effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing lines of critical points for varying $a$, meeting at a tricritical point. Other figures show coexistence lines and spatial dependencies of pair distribution functions across different phases (I, II, III).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model's isomorphism to the Blume-Capel model. It then derives the partition function in the grand canonical ensemble, evaluates it using Laplace's method, and finally maps out the phase diagram by analyzing the conditions for critical points and coexistence lines as a function of the interaction ratio $a$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A double-occupancy cell model of a fluid with Curie-Weiss interaction is studied. First, we show that the model is isomorphic to the Blume-Capel model on a complete graph through a simple transformation from spin to occupancy variables. We then investigate its phase behavior within the grand-canonical ensemble using a combination of analytical and numerical methods. Despite its simplicity, the model exhibits a remarkably rich thermodynamic behavior depending on the ratio between the local repulsive and global attractive interactions. We identify regimes characterized by a single critical point, two distinct critical points, tricritical behavior, and triple-point formation. For sufficiently strong repulsion, the system possesses three fluid phases of different densities, leading to both gas-liquid and liquid-liquid coexistence. The locations of the critical, tricritical, and triple points are determined, and the corresponding phase diagrams are constructed. These results demonstrate that the competition between double-occupancy repulsion and long-range attraction is sufficient to generate complex phase behavior in a minimal multiple-occupancy lattice-gas model.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the dynamical Lie algebra governing QAOA applied to MaxCut on complete graphs. By proving that the loss function variance scales linearly with system size, it definitively shows that this specific algorithm avoids barren plateaus. The methodology is highly technical, relying on advanced group theory and representation theory (Schur-Weyl duality).

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical insight into the convergence properties of quantum algorithms by analyzing their underlying symmetry structure, which is highly relevant for understanding noise and optimization landscapes in quantum computing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the dynamical Lie algebra (DLA) associated with running QAOA on MaxCut problems defined on complete graphs ($K_n$) and proves that this system avoids barren plateaus.

**Main result.** It establishes that the variance of the loss function scales linearly ($\Theta(n)$) with the number of qubits, confirming the absence of barren plateaus for this specific quantum optimization problem.

**Method.** The analysis heavily utilizes the mathematical framework of Dynamical Lie Algebras (DLAs), employing Schur-Weyl duality to decompose operators into irreducible representations of $su(2)$ and $S_n$.

**Model / system.** The model is QAOA applied to the MaxCut problem on a complete graph ($K_n$) using an $n$-qubit quantum system. The analysis focuses on the structure and generators of the associated DLA.

**Key observables.** Loss function variance ($	ext{Var}_	heta[\ell(ho, O; 	heta)]$), the structure of the commutator subalgebra $[\mathfrak{g}_{K_n}, \mathfrak{g}_{K_n}]$, and $s$-purities ($	ext{Ps}(ho)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$); parameters $\alpha, eta$ used in linear independence checks; indices $j$ related to irreducible representations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on establishing specific inclusion relations for the commutator subalgebra $[\mathfrak{g}, \mathfrak{g}]$ and assumes the circuit is deep enough to form a unitary 2-design for variance calculations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure suggests figures illustrating the decomposition of operators or eigenvalue spectra related to $W^{(j)}_{	ext{even}}$ subspaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first defining the DLA generators and analyzing their commutator structure using Schur-Weyl duality (Chunk 1). It then provides a self-contained proof for the structure of $[\mathfrak{g}, \mathfrak{g}]$ via Lemma 3.9 (Chunk 2). Finally, it analyzes specific spectral properties, such as eigenvalues of $(\hat{J}(j)_z)^2$, on subspaces $W^{(j)}_{	ext{even}}$ to complete the structural proofs (Chunk 3).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an analytical expression for the dynamical Lie algebra corresponding to the QAOA-MaxCut problem on complete graphs, and show that the variance of the associated loss function scales linearly in the number of qubits. This solves an open problem from [ASYZ26] and confirms that such systems do not exhibit barren plateaus. The proof is based on projecting the dynamical Lie algebra generators onto subspaces given by the Schur-Weyl duality between irreducible representations of the unitary and symmetric groups.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses dHvA measurements on Sr2RuO4 under uniaxial stress to map out the evolution of its electronic structure. By observing changes in Fermi surface areas and effective masses, the authors propose that charge transfer is the key mechanism driving the system toward a Lifshitz transition. The findings establish quantum oscillation measurements as a powerful tool for probing correlated materials under external strain.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter physics, the precise measurement of electronic structure evolution under external fields (stress) provides an excellent model for studying how external perturbations tune many-body ground states, which has parallels in controlling quantum phases relevant to AMO systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To study how applying uniaxial stress affects the electronic structure and Fermi surface of the unconventional superconductor Sr2RuO4, particularly to reconcile discrepancies between predicted density of states changes and observed superconducting temperature suppression.

**Main result.** The application of c-axis uniaxial stress causes measurable changes in the cross-sectional areas of different Fermi sheets ($\alpha$ increases, $eta$ decreases, $\gamma$ increases), suggesting charge transfer is driving the system toward a Lifshitz transition. Effective masses on all measured sheets are found to be slightly enhanced upon compression.

**Method.** The study combines experimental De Haas-van Alphen (dHvA) quantum oscillation measurements under uniaxial stress with theoretical Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations and magnetostriction measurements for comparison.

**Model / system.** The system is the unconventional superconductor Sr2RuO4. The analysis focuses on how applying controlled c-axis uniaxial strain/stress ($\sigma_{zz}$) tunes the electronic band structure, leading to changes in Fermi surface topology and carrier distribution among different orbital character sheets ($\alpha, eta, \gamma$).

**Key observables.** dHvA frequencies (for $\alpha, eta, \gamma$ sheets), cross-sectional areas of Fermi sheets, effective masses ($m^*$), superconducting critical temperature $T_c(\sigma)$, and strain/stress dependence ($\Delta F$ vs. $\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial stress up to $-1.8$ GPa (experimentally) or higher (theoretically); the approach to a Lifshitz transition; effective mass enhancement factor ($\sim 10\%$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that relative changes in effective masses are intrinsic and can be compared across different stress states, and that strain inhomogeneity issues can be controlled.

**Figures summary.** Figures show raw/normalized quantum oscillations vs. magnetic field under various stresses; comparisons between dHvA frequency shifts ($\Delta F$) derived from experiment and DFT calculations; and plots detailing the evolution of Fermi surface areas and $T_c$ dependence on stress.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the experimental feasibility of measuring dHvA effects under uniaxial stress. It then details how the measured changes in Fermi surface topology (sheet area changes) point to charge transfer mechanisms approaching a Van Hove singularity, contrasting this with other stress directions. Finally, it confirms quantitative agreement between these quantum oscillation results and DFT predictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Uniaxial stress has now been widely used to study correlated electron materials. However, Fermi surface-resolved experimental data on the evolution of the electronic structure under piezoelectrically applied stress are sparse, with no reports of de Haas-van Alphen (dHvA) effects under uniaxial stress. Here we present dHvA measurements under $c$-axis uniaxial stress on the unconventional superconductor $\mathrm{Sr}_2\mathrm{RuO}_4$. This allows us to study the evolution of the electronic structure directly and to gain insight into the contradicting behavior of the predicted enhancement of the electronic density of states and the observed suppression of $T_\text{c}$. We are able to follow all Fermi surfaces for stress up to $-1.8$~GPa and find that the cross-sectional areas of the hole-like $α$ sheet increase and electron-like $β$ sheet decrease. At the same time, the area of the electron-like $γ$ sheet increases. Therefore, in contrast to in-plane uniaxial stress, charge transfer is the mechanism for approaching the electron-to-hole Lifshitz transition and the associated Van Hove singularity. Additionally, we find that the effective masses on all three Fermi sheets are slightly enhanced as the Lifshitz transition is approached. We compare the dHvA results with quantum oscillations in the magnetostriction and band structure calculations, and find good agreement. At a more general level, our findings show that quantum oscillation measurements under uniaxial stress, combined with band-structure calculations, offer a promising new route for studying quantum materials.

</details>
