**Summary.** This theoretical work classifies complex two-qudit Clifford circuits over finite fields and derives precise formulas for local masking distances. It proves that short quantum messages can be perfectly hidden from partial output measurements while remaining fully recoverable from the whole system. This advances the understanding of information security in quantum computation architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework connecting finite field algebra ($	ext{Sp}(4, q)$) to fundamental quantum information tasks like perfect data hiding provides deep insights into the structure of quantum gates.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper classifies two-qudit finite-field Clifford dual-unitary gates and applies this classification to determine exact local masking and operator transport properties in homogeneous brickwork circuits.

**Main result.** It establishes specific, quantifiable masking laws ($d_1(t), d_2(t)$) showing that short quantum messages can be perfectly hidden from small output subsystems while remaining fully recoverable from the total output.

**Method.** The analysis uses algebraic techniques involving symplectic groups $	ext{Sp}(4, q)$, classifying gates based on determinants ($\delta$) and relating them to geometric structures like MDS configurations and cross-ratios.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by Clifford dual-unitary circuits operating over finite fields $\mathbb{F}_q$ on qudits. The dynamics are analyzed using symplectic matrix representations within the group $	ext{Sp}(4, q)$.

**Key observables.** Local masking distances ($d_1(t), d_2(t)$), entanglement entropy profiles, and algebraic codimensions of transport phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The finite field size $q$, the circuit depth $t$, and the determinant parameter $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes specific structures for homogeneous repetition and focuses on exact local masking (state-independent reduction), distinguishing it from approximate indistinguishability.

**Figures summary.** Figures/tables summarize phase counts, leakage vs. perturbation strength ($\epsilon$), and recovery fidelity versus Pauli noise probability ($p$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by classifying the dual-unitary locus into $q$ core types (perfect, rank-one, SWAP), then analyzing how homogeneous repetition separates these cores into five distinct transport phases with varying algebraic codimensions. Finally, it derives explicit masking laws and tests them numerically for small $q$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We classify two-qudit finite-field Clifford dual-unitary gates and apply the classification to exact local masking and operator transport in homogeneous brickwork circuits. Under ordered one-qudit Clifford equivalence, the dual-unitary locus contains $q-2$ perfect-tensor cores, one rank-one core, and one SWAP core. The perfect cores are indexed by \[ δ=\det B=\det C, \] and are related to the ordered cross-ratio $λ$ of the associated $[4,2,3]_q$ MDS configuration through \[ λ=\fracδ{δ-1}. \] Homogeneous repetition separates these cores into five transport phases whose algebraic codimensions in $\operatorname{Sp}(4,q)$ are $0,1,2,3,4$.   The one-site Weyl edge channels determine exact local-masking distances. Perfect-tensor circuits attain \[ d_1(t)=4t, \qquad d_2(t)=4t-2, \] whereas delayed erasers satisfy \[ d_1(t)=4t-2, \qquad d_2(t)=4t-4 \] for $t\geq 2$. Consequently, sufficiently short quantum messages are completely hidden from every one- or two-qudit output subsystem, even when the input is entangled with a reference, while remaining exactly recoverable from the full output.   For $q=3$, we construct an explicit perfect-tensor Clifford gate from two inverse SUM gates. Exhaustive Weyl-support searches for $t=1,2,3$ reproduce the predicted masking distances, and a one-period Choi-channel calculation for a four-qutrit periodic circuit gives numerical residual leakage below $2\times 10^{-16}$. Under the coherent perturbation considered here, local leakage is linear in the perturbation strength, whereas the infidelity of recovery using the ideal inverse is quadratic near the perfect point.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a powerful Monte Carlo technique to calculate free energy differences between distinct global sectors in lattice field theories. Applied to the 3D Ising model, it yields an independent measurement of the symmetry-twisted Casimir difference ($\Delta Z_2$). This method provides a robust tool for probing universal CFT data by analyzing finite-size effects across different geometric limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the use of advanced Monte Carlo techniques (flat histograms) and connections to CFT/spectral analysis are highly relevant for understanding quantum many-body systems where finite-size effects and boundary conditions dictate low-energy physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to estimate partition function ratios between distinct global sectors of a lattice theory, specifically focusing on symmetry-twisted observables.

**Main result.** The authors directly calculate the symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference ($\Delta Z_2 = 0.327(2)$) for the critical 3D Ising model without needing lattice derivatives or bulk subtractions.

**Method.** A Monte Carlo strategy is employed by enlarging the configuration space to sample an interpolating family between the desired sectors, using flat histogram methods to reconstruct the free energy difference.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the three-dimensional Ising model simulated on a slab geometry ($R^2 	imes S^1_{L_z}$) at its bulk critical point. The comparison is made between the standard periodic sector and the $\mathbb{Z}_2$-twisted antiperiodic sector.

**Key observables.** Symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference ($\Delta Z_2$), sector free energy difference ($\Delta F_{Z_2}$), and finite-size gap parameters ($\kappa_\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The aspect ratio $q = L/L_z$, the lattice size ($L, L_z$), and the coupling variable $J$ used for interpolation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on large-volume and aspect ratio extrapolations to extract universal continuum limits. The method assumes that sector differences allow access to CFT compactification data.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate benchmarks (e.g., 2D case approaching $\pi/4$) and the convergence of observables ($\Delta Z_2$ and gap parameters) as system sizes are taken to their thermodynamic limits via various extrapolation ansätze.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general Monte Carlo method for sector ratio estimation, applies it specifically to the 3D Ising model on a slab geometry, analyzes the large-$q$ limit to find $\Delta Z_2$, and then examines the small-$q$ channel to determine spectral gaps ($\kappa_\sigma$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a Monte Carlo strategy for directly estimating partition function ratios between distinct global sectors of a lattice theory. It enlarges the configuration space to sample an interpolating family whose endpoints are the desired sectors, and uses flat histogram methods to reconstruct the corresponding free energy difference. Although the construction is more general, we focus here on the three-dimensional Ising model on the slab $\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}_{L_{z}}$ at the bulk critical point, comparing the untwisted periodic sector with the $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-twisted antiperiodic sector. A large-volume and aspect ratio extrapolation gives the symmetry-twisted thermodynamic Casimir difference $Δ_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=0.327(2)$ directly, without lattice derivatives or bulk subtractions. This provides an independent twisted sector probe of tensions observed in periodic sector thermodynamic Casimir observables. More generally, the method gives direct but selective numerical access to CFT compactification data, including estimates of the effective thermal screening scale and the $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-odd sector energy gap on $T^{2}$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a unified theory for spontaneous Raman scattering by bridging quantum mechanics and nonlinear optics. It shows that calculating the process using advanced quantum field methods yields perfect analytical agreement with traditional susceptibility approaches, while also predicting a previously unrecognized 'spontaneous Raman loss' mechanism.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it rigorously unifies two major theoretical descriptions of light-matter interaction—the microscopic quantum picture (KHD) and the macroscopic response theory ($\chi^{(3)}$)—while predicting new vacuum-seeded phenomena like sRL.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a theoretical framework that fundamentally bridges quantum mechanical and nonlinear optical approaches for spontaneous Raman scattering.

**Main result.** The authors achieve perfect analytical agreement between the quantum mechanical description (including negative-frequency terms) and the macroscopic nonlinear susceptibility approach, predicting a novel phenomenon called 'spontaneous Raman loss' (sRL).

**Method.** The study involves deriving the spontaneous Raman cross-section by calculating third-order nonlinear susceptibilities ($\chi^{(3)}$) using both traditional Kramers-Heisenberg-Dirac theory and an advanced quantum mechanical formalism incorporating vacuum field expectation values.

**Model / system.** The system is molecular scattering undergoing Raman processes, involving vibrational quanta ($\omega_{vib}$), pump fields ($\omega_i$), and scattered photons ($\omega_s$). The analysis uses concepts from nonlinear optics and quantum electrodynamics applied to light-matter interaction.

**Key observables.** Spontaneous Raman cross-section ($d\sigma/d\Omega$), spontaneous Raman gain (sRG) and loss (sRL) intensities, and the third-order nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi^{(3)}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Molecular density ($N$), vacuum permittivity ($\epsilon_o$), and various frequency differences ($\omega_{eg}, \omega_{ev}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that unobservable terms (related to negative-frequency processes beyond the observable range) are physically forbidden in spontaneous scattering, allowing for a direct mapping between formalisms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the rotating and counter-rotating terms in KHD theory. Figure 2 details eight Feynman diagrams used to calculate $\chi_{	ext{sRL}}^{(3)}$ and $\chi_{	ext{sRG}}^{(3)}$. Table 1 maps observable/unobservable quantum mechanical terms to nonlinear susceptibility components.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for a unified theory, then calculating sRG and sRL using vacuum expectation values derived from $\chi^{(3)}$ (via Feynman diagrams), and finally demonstrating that an advanced negative-frequency-assisted quantum mechanical approach yields exact analytical agreement with the established nonlinear optical formalism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical framework for spontaneous Raman scattering that fundamentally bridges quantum-mechanical and nonlinear-optical approaches. By conceptualizing spontaneous Raman scattering as a stimulated Raman gain or loss event seeded by the quantum vacuum field, we rigorously derive the spontaneous Raman cross-section directly from the third-order nonlinear susceptibility. Crucially, this framework predicts the existence of a hitherto unrecognized phenomenon: "spontaneous Raman loss" (sRL), which acts as the vacuum-seeded counterpart to stimulated Raman loss, complementing traditional spontaneous Raman scattering (spontaneous Raman gain, sRG). Furthermore, we establish a rigorous connection to the traditional Kramers-Heisenberg-Dirac (KHD) theory, revealing that the spontaneous process is governed by interference before a detector between the signal field emitted from molecules and the vacuum field itself that stimulates the molecules. This insight uncovers a direct correspondence between the sRG susceptibility and the rotating/counter-rotating interference terms in the KHD formula. Ultimately, we extend the foundational KHD theory by incorporating previously unrecognized essential terms, achieving perfect analytical agreement between the quantum mechanical and nonlinear optical descriptions of Raman scattering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the landscape of quantum control for four-level systems, proving that the zero-control point acts as a high-order trap. By comparing V-V and chain interaction models using GRAPE/GPM simulations, the authors reveal that the optimization efficiency varies drastically depending on the system's underlying Hamiltonian structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of optimal control theory by quantifying how the underlying structure of the Hamiltonian dictates the difficulty and efficiency of finding controls, which is crucial for designing robust quantum protocols in AMO settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes the quantum control landscape for optimizing a specific observable by finding optimal time-dependent controls $f(t)$ in four-level quantum systems.

**Main result.** The null control ($f \equiv 0$) is proven to be a five-order trap, but the optimization efficiency shows diverse behavior: a sharp increase for V-V systems versus a slower increase for chain interaction systems.

**Method.** The analysis combines analytical investigation of the objective functional's Taylor expansion at the null control with numerical simulations using GRAPE (unconstrained) and GPM (constrained).

**Model / system.** The system involves four-level quantum dynamics governed by a Hamiltonian $H = H_0 + f(t)V$. Two specific models are compared: a V-V system and an anharmonic/chain interaction system.

**Key observables.** Optimization efficiency (fraction of successful runs), the objective functional value, and the order of the trap determined by Taylor expansion coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distance parameter $l$ from the null control; time duration $T=20$; number of discretization points $M=100$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes controllability of the system and relies on structural assumptions regarding the Hamiltonian for proving trap orders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy level structures (V-V vs. chain), and subsequent figures show optimization efficiency (fraction of failed runs) versus distance $l$ for both V-V and chain systems, comparing GRAPE and GPM results.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the quantum control problem and characterizing traps analytically to performing numerical comparisons between unconstrained (GRAPE) and constrained (GPM) optimization methods on two distinct physical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we perform an analytical and numerical analysis of quantum landscapes for controlling special four-level quantum systems for which we prove that the null control is a five-order trap: a $V-V$ system and an anharmonic system. As a control goal, an observable optimization is considered. The rigorous theoretical analysis is followed by the numerical experiments based on the GRadient Ascent Pulse Engineering (GRAPE) algorithm and Gradient Projection Method (GPM), performed to investigate the behavior of the efficiency of optimization for unconstrained (using GRAPE) and constrained (using GPM) controls. As the main result, we observe an interesting phenomenon with a diverse behavior of the optimization efficiency depending on the system Hamiltonian -- sharp increase of the optimization efficiency up to 100% at certain distance from the null control for a V-V system, while much slower and less significant increase (and even small decrease) for a system with the chain interaction. This sharp difference might be related with the fine structure of the subspace of controls where second derivative of the objective functional is zero.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors investigate the structural phase transition in $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$ by constructing a total free-energy functional that couples DFT results with an extended Hubbard model. They demonstrate that this transition is governed by a delicate balance between phonon entropy (favoring the high-T $\alpha$ phase) and spin entropy/electronic energy gains (favoring the low-T $eta$ phase). The framework successfully predicts how external pressure can suppress this transition, potentially stabilizing the QSL candidate regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a comprehensive theoretical framework for understanding structural transitions in Mott insulators where lattice dynamics, electronic correlations, and magnetic ordering are intrinsically coupled, which is highly relevant to correlated materials physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the microscopic origin of a first-order structural phase transition in $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$ that suppresses its potential quantum spin liquid (QSL) state.

**Main result.** The transition is jointly driven by phonon and spin entropy, with high-temperature stability favoring the $\alpha$ phase due to entropic gains, while low-temperature favorability of the $eta$ phase arises from interlayer dimerization and spin singlet formation.

**Method.** A unified free-energy framework combining first-principles DFT calculations with an extended Hubbard model and phonon theory is employed to analyze lattice, electronic, and magnetic contributions to the total free energy.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Nb}_3	ext{Cl}_8$, modeled using an effective Hamiltonian derived from a cluster-centered $2a_1$ orbital basis. The analysis incorporates terms for electron correlation (Hubbard model) and lattice vibrations (phonon free energy).

**Key observables.** Helmholtz free energies ($	ext{F}_{	ext{tot}}$), phonon frequencies, spin entropy changes ($S_{	ext{spin}}$), and generalized enthalpy under uniaxial stress ($\Delta H$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transition temperature ($T_c \approx 113 	ext{ K}$), critical pressure ($P_c \approx 2.6 	ext{ GPa}$), and Hubbard parameters ($U, V, t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The phonon calculation uses the harmonic approximation; the model requires a double-counting correction ($	ext{EDC}$) to reconcile DFT and correlated electron treatments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phonon dispersions comparing $\alpha$ and $eta$ phases, and plots show the decomposition of free energy and enthalpy differences as functions of temperature and pressure.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically constructs a total free-energy functional by adding DFT energies, phonon contributions, and Hubbard model spin/correlation terms. It analyzes how entropy (phonon vs. spin) dictates phase stability across varying temperatures and external pressures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As a prototypical flat-band cluster Mott insulator on an effective triangular lattice, Nb$_3$Cl$_8$ is a potential candidate for hosting a quantum spin liquid (QSL) state. Nevertheless, a first-order structural phase transition around 90K transforms the high-temperature paramagnetic $α$ phase into the low-temperature nonmagnetic $β$ phase, suppressing the candidate QSL regime of the $α$ phase. To clarify the microscopic origin of this transition, we combine first-principles calculations with an extended Hubbard model to construct a unified free-energy framework. This framework reveals that the transition is jointly driven by phonon and spin entropy: the $α$ phase is stabilized by softer phonons and larger paramagnetic spin entropy, whereas the $β$ phase is favored by interlayer dimerization, which hardens the phonons and quenches the spin entropy through singlet formation. Furthermore, by evaluating the pressure-dependent generalized enthalpy, we provide a thermodynamic explanation for the suppression of the transition under c-axis uniaxial pressure, where stabilizing the $α$ phase may allow the candidate QSL regime of the $α$ phase to be explored at low temperatures.

</details>

(56), open symbols] versus the semiclassical thermodynamic-limit form (solid), for 4He (m = 6.64 × 10−27 kg) in the harmonic trap (ωx, ωy, ωz) = 2π × (1000, 1000, 100) Hz (¯ω = 2π × 464 Hz), at N = 105 (Tc = 0.97 µK) and N = 106 (Tc = 2.10 µK). The two agree to &lt; 0.1% for T ≥Tc; the exact sum rounds the cusp over δT/Tc ∼N −1/3 and the below-Tc excess decreases with N.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The authors theoretically predict that Bose-Einstein condensation imprints a universal cusp onto the time-of-arrival statistics of a trapped gas released into free fall. This characteristic ratio is shown to equal the specific-heat ratio of the ideal gas, providing a highly robust and measurable signature for BEC in cold atom experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean, measurable dynamical signature of BEC that is robust against minor perturbations (like weak interactions) and depends only on fundamental thermodynamic ratios, making it an excellent diagnostic tool for ultracold atomic experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) generates a universal, measurable signature in the time-of-arrival (TOA) statistics of a trapped gas released into free fall.

**Main result.** The one-sided slope ratio ($R_\infty$) characterizing the cusp in the TOA distribution is predicted to be universal ($\approx 2.556$), matching the specific-heat ratio of the ideal trapped gas, independent of trap parameters.

**Method.** Semiclassical analysis of the time-of-arrival flux, involving expansions in a small parameter $\epsilon$ and separating contributions from the condensate and thermal cloud.

**Model / system.** An ideal Bose gas confined in an anisotropic harmonic trap, released to free fall. The model is analyzed in the long time-of-flight (far-field) semiclassical limit.

**Key observables.** The TOA distribution $\Pi(t)$, mean arrival time shift $\langle T angle - t_c$, standard deviation $\Delta T$, and the universal one-sided slope ratio $R_\infty$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ideal gas limit, far-field regime ($\omega z t_c \gg 1$), finite atom number ($N$), and weak interaction strength ($\chi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ideal gas model initially; interactions are treated perturbatively (Hartree-Fock) or neglected to establish the universal limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the development of $\langle T angle - t_c$ and $\Delta T$ showing a cusp, confirming $R_\infty \approx 2.556$, and demonstrate that finite-$N$ effects round this cusp while weak interactions cause only minor shifts.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the TOA distribution semiclassically, derives model-independent relations for $\langle T angle$ and $\Delta T$, calculates the universal ratio $R_\infty$ by comparing it to the specific heat ratio, and finally tests its robustness against finite atom numbers and weak interactions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that Bose--Einstein condensation produces a cusp in the time-of-arrival (TOA) statistics of a harmonically trapped gas released into free fall. In the semiclassical long time-of-flight regime, with $ε=σ_V/\sqrt{2gH}\ll1$, both the mean and standard deviation of the arrival time distribution, which are governed by the longitudinal velocity variance, remain continuous, but acquire a cusp whose one-sided slope ratio is universal within the ideal-gas far-field limit, $\mathcal{R}_\infty=2.5556\ldots$, and equals the trapped-gas specific-heat ratio $C(Tc^-)/C(Tc^+)$. Finite atom number rounds the cusp and weak interactions perturb it only weakly, leaving a measurable time-domain signature of condensation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates how structural distortions, specifically the 'breathing mode,' induce unconventional odd-frequency Cooper pairs in Kagome superconductors. By classifying superconducting symmetries, the authors show that this structural modulation drives a dynamical pairing mechanism even when conventional $s$-wave superconductivity is present. This establishes breathing Kagome lattices as a platform for realizing exotic, time-nonlocal quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state superconductivity, the concept of dynamical pairing driven by structural fluctuations (a form of coupling between lattice degrees of freedom and electronic correlations) is relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To classify superconducting symmetries in Kagome superconductors and demonstrate the emergence of odd-frequency dynamical Cooper pairs driven by structural distortions.

**Main result.** The breathing mode induces a specific type of odd-frequency, spin-singlet pairing ($	ext{OSEO}$ class) that can emerge even when the parent superconductivity is conventional $s$-wave.

**Method.** Theoretical calculation involving the full classification of superconducting symmetries using anomalous Green's functions and solving the Bogoliubov de Gennes Hamiltonian self-consistently.

**Model / system.** The system is based on Kagome materials, modeled with a normal state Hamiltonian that includes a structural modulation called the 'breathing mode,' which breaks inversion symmetry ($t_a 
eq t_b$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting pair amplitudes symmetry (even/odd under frequency/spin), odd-frequency pairing signatures, and quasiparticle interference ($\deltaho_a(q, \omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structural distortion parameter ($t_a - t_b$) is crucial; superconductivity exists in specific chemical potential ranges ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially considers the simplest onsite spin-singlet superconductivity using $\Delta_{AA}, \Delta_{BB}, \Delta_{CC}$ as independent order parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures comparing pristine vs. distorted lattices, and show self-consistent superconducting order parameters ($\Delta_{\alpha\alpha'}$) dependence on chemical potential.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by classifying allowed pair symmetries based on symmetry constraints, demonstrating how the breathing mode opens gaps at Dirac points, leading to the emergence of odd-frequency pairing mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The breathing mode in Kagome materials is a structural modulation that breaks inversion symmetry and has been shown to be a crucial source for intriguing phases in the normal state. In this work, we carry out a full classification of superconducting symmetries in kagome superconductors and demonstrate the emergence of odd-frequency dynamical Cooper pairs entirely driven by the breathing mode. We then show that odd-frequency spin-singlet Cooper pairs can be realized by controlling the breathing mode in kagome lattices with conventional spin-singlet $s$-wave superconductivity. Since odd-frequency pairing is intrinsically nonlocal in time, our results put forward the breathing mode for designing dynamical Cooper pairs in kagome materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how long-range correlated disorder fundamentally changes the nature of topological Anderson insulators, causing them to become non-self-averaging. The authors demonstrate this by showing that the relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent remains finite in the thermodynamic limit, indicating a breakdown of standard averaging theorems for these complex quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The breakdown of self-averaging is a critical concept in disordered systems theory, suggesting that standard ensemble averaging techniques fail to capture the true physics near topological phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates topological Anderson insulators under long-range correlated disorder, a regime poorly understood compared to standard uncorrelated disorder.

**Main result.** Long-range correlated disorder can induce non-self-averaging behavior in the topological states, meaning single sample properties deviate significantly from ensemble averages.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating and analyzing the relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent ($\gamma$) across multiple disorder realizations to diagnose self-averaging versus non-self-averaging regimes.

**Model / system.** The model used is a disordered Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain, incorporating long-range correlated disorder defined by a power-law spectral density $S(k) \sim 1/k^\alpha$.

**Key observables.** Topological Quantum Number ($Q$), Lyapunov Exponent ($\gamma$), and the Relative Variance of the Lyapunov Exponent ($R_\gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correlation parameter $\alpha$ (quantifying disorder range), the system size $N$, and the number of disorder realizations $N_r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed at half-filling using large system sizes ($N = 5 	imes 10^4$) and many disorder realizations ($N_r = 10^5$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows how $R_\gamma$ behaves for different $\alpha$, indicating non-self-averaging when $R_\gamma$ remains finite in the thermodynamic limit. Figure 2 plots average and relative variance of $\gamma$ vs. $N$, confirming fixed constant values for non-self-averaging cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by contrasting self-averaging (short-range disorder) with the complex effects of long-range correlated disorder, demonstrating mathematically that this correlation leads to a finite relative variance in the thermodynamic limit, defining the 'non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator'.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Current research on the disordered topological quantum phases primarily focuses on the uncorrelated and short-range correlated disorder regime. These topological Anderson insulators are typically self-averaging. However, topological quantum systems with long-range correlated disorder have received limited attention due to the absence of tractable analytical methods. In fact, the long-range correlated disorder introduces more complex effects on topological quantum states. Here, we demonstrate that the long-range correlated disorder could induce anomalously statistical feature where the topological Anderson states become non-self-averaging, and the phase diagram is strongly dependent on individual disorder configurations. We term this statistical phase the non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator. The non-self-averaging property is identified by the non-vanishing finite values of the relative variance of the Lyapunov exponent in the thermodynamic limit, alongside the non-Gaussian distributions of the Lyapunov exponent. Consequently, the topological properties of a single disordered sample deviate from the ensemble average, causing a breakdown of the central limit theorem. The non-self-averaging topological Anderson insulator provides insights into the interplay between correlated disorder and topology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports experimental evidence of unexpected flat energy bands in the Weyl semimetal TaRhTe4 using ARPES. The findings highlight a significant discrepancy between theory and experiment, suggesting that topological materials can host emergent electronic states not captured by current computational models. This combination of topology and flat-band physics makes it an interesting platform for condensed matter research.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on topology/band structure, the observation of emergent, unpredicted electronic features like flat bands provides a platform for investigating strong correlation effects or novel many-body physics that standard DFT might miss.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to observe flat electronic bands near the Fermi level in a noncentrosymmetric bulk Weyl semimetal, as such features are rare and unexpected based on initial theoretical calculations.

**Main result.** ARPES measurements reveal the presence of distinct, momentum-independent flat bands localized at specific energies below the Fermi level, particularly evident on one crystal surface termination (Termination A).

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) experiments with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe the electronic structure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is bulk TaRhTe4, classified as a noncentrosymmetric van-der Waals type-II Weyl semimetal. The analysis compares experimental measurements on different crystal terminations against theoretical predictions.

**Key observables.** Flat bands near the chemical potential, Fermi surfaces (FSs), and the location/existence of Weyl points (WPs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (5–5.5 K) for ARPES; energy scales around 4 meV below E_F.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The flat bands observed were not predicted by initial DFT calculations, suggesting physics beyond the standard theory or surface effects are at play.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare calculated and measured FSs for two terminations (A and B); Figure 4 specifically highlights the momentum-independent flat bands on Termination A via EDCs/cuts.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the material's topological nature using DFT, presents ARPES data comparing different surface terminations, identifies general agreement with theory, and then focuses heavily on the discrepancy—the unexpected appearance of flat bands in experiment versus calculation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flat bands have been theoretically predicted for decades but have only recently been realized in quantum materials such as magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene, kagome and Lieb lattices, and rare-earth metal compounds. To date, only twisted layered materials have enabled tuning of flat-band energies near the electronic chemical potential, thereby influencing transport and thermodynamic properties. Here, we report the presence of flat bands near the chemical potential in bulk TaRhTe$_{4}$, a noncentrosymmetric van-der Waals type-II Weyl semimetal. Flat bands are rarely observed in Weyl semimetals, particularly in nonmagnetic bulk systems, and the observed flat bands were not predicted by density functional theory calculations. TaRhTe$_{4}$ therefore provides a platform in which nontrivial topology coexists with flat bands near the Fermi level, as evidenced by our angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes the weak ferromagnetism in NiS2 nanocrystals by measuring their magnetization across temperature ranges relative to antiferromagnetic transitions. The key finding is that the strong magnetic hysteresis below the lower transition is attributed to uncompensated surface moments, which successfully explains the observed field-cooling effects while simultaneously constraining theoretical models of bulk magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study's rigorous comparison of bulk vs. nanoscale magnetic responses, coupled with detailed characterization of phase transitions via heat capacity, provides a model for how dimensionality and interfaces fundamentally alter many-body quantum ground states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin of weak ferromagnetism observed in NiS2, specifically determining whether this magnetism arises from homogeneous canting throughout the bulk or is localized at structural features like domain walls.

**Main result.** The pronounced hysteretic ferromagnetic response below the lower antiferromagnetic transition temperature is best explained by uncompensated surface moments generated where the low-temperature magnetic order terminates at the nanocrystal surfaces.

**Method.** Magnetization (M-H) and specific heat measurements were performed on synthesized NiS2 nanocrystals, comparing results above and below the critical transition temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is structurally well-ordered NiS2 nanocrystals ($\sim 27$ nm). The analysis contrasts two theoretical models for weak ferromagnetism: homogeneous canting (volume property) versus localization at domain walls (interface property).

**Key observables.** Hysteretic ferromagnetic response, large coercivity, exchange bias, and vertical loop shift observed below $T_{N2}$; nearly linear M-H response above $T_{N2}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanocrystal diameter ($\sim 27$ nm); Antiferromagnetic transition temperatures ($T_{N1} \simeq 39$ K, $T_{N2} \simeq 30$ K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetic features are highly sensitive to nanoscale confinement effects, allowing differentiation between bulk and surface contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of magnetization (ZFC/FC) confirming two transitions; hysteresis loops demonstrate strong magnetic signatures only below $T_{N2}$, which are analyzed for exchange bias and coercivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the bulk properties, synthesizes nanocrystals, measures magnetic responses across different temperatures, and then uses the temperature-dependent contrast between hysteretic (low T) and linear (high T) behavior to rule out competing theoretical models in favor of surface moment generation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structurally well-ordered NiS$_2$ nanocrystals with an average diameter of $27.0 \pm 6.5$ nm retain the bulk-like two-step antiferromagnetic transitions, as shown by magnetization and heat-capacity measurements. Below the lower transition, the nanocrystals exhibit a hysteretic ferromagnetic response with large coercivity, exchange bias, and a vertical loop shift after field cooling, whereas the $M$-$H$ response just above the transition is nearly linear. These features are best explained by uncompensated surface moments generated where the low-temperature antiferromagnetic order terminates at the nanocrystal surface. The absence of a clear additional bulk-like weak-ferromagnetic component constrains homogeneous-canting models and indirectly favors a domain-wall scenario for the weak ferromagnetism of bulk NiS$_2$.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors use Quantum Monte Carlo simulations on a fractionalized lattice model to study the transition between an antiferromagnetic insulator and a d-wave superconductor. They find evidence for a deconfined quantum critical point where both orders vanish continuously, characterized by changes in the electronic dispersion from gapless Dirac cones to gapped states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced numerical evidence for deconfined quantum criticality in models relevant to high-Tc superconductivity, utilizing sophisticated gauge field theories within a QMC framework that is highly valuable for understanding emergent quantum phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum phase transition (QPT) between an antiferromagnetic insulator (Néel state) and a nodal d-wave superconductor in strongly correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** Evidence is found for a second-order deconfined quantum phase transition (DQCP) where both Néel and d-wave superconducting orders vanish continuously. The electronic dispersion changes from gapless Dirac to gapped as the system moves into the AFM state.

**Method.** Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations are employed using a fractionalized representation of electrons into fermionic spinons and bosonic chargons coupled to an SU(2) lattice gauge field.

**Model / system.** The model is defined on the square lattice at half-filling, utilizing an SU(2) lattice gauge theory framework. The electron degrees of freedom are decomposed into spinon and chargon components whose dynamics govern the competition between magnetic and superconducting orders.

**Key observables.** Spin structure factor ($\chi_S$), pairing structure factor ($\chi_{el, d}$), electronic spectral function ($A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$), and correlation ratios for AFM, dSC, and VBS order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transition is mapped by varying coupling constants like $\kappa$ and $\kappa_	au$, which tune the relative strengths of magnetic and superconducting interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on successfully fractionalizing electrons into spinons and chargons to avoid the sign problem in QMC simulations. The analysis also assumes that specific gauge-invariant composite operators correctly capture physical observables.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing three distinct regimes (dSC, AFM, deconfined) separated by transition lines, alongside spectral plots demonstrating gapless Dirac dispersion in the dSC phase and gapped behavior in the AFM phase across the critical point.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian decomposition into spinon and chargon parts. It then applies QMC simulations to calculate various correlation functions and spectra across different parameter regimes, culminating in a detailed comparison of spectral features near the proposed DQCP.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a quantum Monte Carlo study of the transition between the insulating Néel state and the nodal $d$-wave superconductor on the square lattice at half-filling. We access a regime of frustrated magnetic order without a sign problem using a parton representation of the electron in terms of fermionic spinons and bosonic chargons. Both partons move in a background $π$-flux (so the electron experiences no net flux) and are coupled to a quantum fluctuating SU(2) lattice gauge field. In contrast to earlier studies directly on the electronic degrees of freedom, we find evidence for a second-order deconfined quantum phase transition at which both the Néel and $d$-wave superconductivity orders vanish continuously. We compute correlators of the spinon-chargon composite with the same quantum numbers as the electron: we find a gapless Dirac dispersion inside the $d$-wave superconductor, turning into a gapped dispersion in the antiferromagnet.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces GsOQDC, an open-source GUI framework designed to simulate Optical Quantum Data Centers (OQDCs) for Distributed Quantum Computation. It unifies the entire workflow—from circuit design through network scheduling and execution simulation—into one platform. This allows researchers to evaluate complex cross-layer performance metrics like entanglement dynamics and system fidelity in a practical, graphical environment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the practical engineering challenge of simulating large-scale quantum networking, which underpins future distributed quantum computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The existing software ecosystem for Quantum Data Centers (QDCs) is fragmented, lacking an integrated environment to evaluate end-to-end behavior across quantum applications and optical network resources.

**Main result.** GsOQDC provides the first graphical platform enabling end-to-end design-space exploration of distributed quantum applications while jointly considering optical-network behavior and entanglement-resource dynamics.

**Method.** The framework integrates circuit modeling, optically switched interconnection, network-aware compilation, scheduling, and a Discrete-Event Simulation (DES) engine for cross-layer evaluation.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an Optical Quantum Data Center (OQDC), comprising multiple interconnected Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) linked by optical networks to perform Distributed Quantum Computation (DQC).

**Key observables.** Entanglement-resource dynamics, system-level performance, Fidelity, Communication overhead, Entanglement-resource utilization, and Job Execution Time (JET).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate errors, coherence times, network architecture parameters, and scheduling constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the need to bridge gaps between tools that either lack compilation/scheduling or assume abstract communication models.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the unified GsOQDC workflow (Circuit $ightarrow$ Network $ightarrow$ Compile $ightarrow$ Schedule $ightarrow$ Run). Figure 2 shows the interactive Network Designer for OQDCs. Figure 3 visualizes distributed workload scheduling using Gantt charts.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces GsOQDC, detailing its architecture (Fig. 1), providing an interactive GUI for network design and parameter setting (Fig. 2), and demonstrating the simulation of complex workloads via scheduling visualizations (Fig. 3).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present GsOQDC, an open-source graphical framework integrating optical-network design, distributed quantum-circuit compilation, scheduling, and DES-based remote-gate simulation, enabling end-to-end cross-layer evaluation of entanglement-resource dynamics and system-level performance in Optical Quantum Data Centers.

</details>

### [Non-signaling assistance in prepare-and-measure scenarios with classical communication](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01193v1)

**Authors:** José Nogueira, Carlos Vieira, Lucas E. A. Porto, Lucas Pollyceno, Rafael Rabelo, Otfried Gühne  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.01193v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2607.01193_figures/2607.01193_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Schematic of a prepare-and-measure scenario. Alice en- codes her input x into a preparation device, which emits either a classical or quantum system sent to Bob. Bob receives the system, chooses an input y for his measurement device, and produces an output b. The devices may share correlations, which can be clas- sical (shared randomness λ) or quantum (arising from a shared entangled state ρAB).</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.01193_figures/2607.01193_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Schematic representation of a prepare-and-measure sce- nario with non-adaptive non-signaling assistance, according to the definition in Eq. (6). Alice and Bob share an arbitrary non- signaling box in the Bell scenario (nX, nA; nY , nβ). Alice inputs x into her share of the box, obtains outcome a, and communicates it to Bob. Bob independently inputs y into his share of the box and obtains β. Finally, he post-processes a,β to produce the output b.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.01193_figures/2607.01193_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. Schematic representation of a prepare-and-measure sce- nario with adaptive non-signaling assistance, according to Eq. (8). Alice and Bob share an arbitrary non-signaling box in the Bell sce- nario (nX, nA; nY nA, nB). Alice inputs x into her share of the box, obtains outcome a, and communicates it to Bob. Bob then inputs the composite setting (y, a) into his share of the box and obtains the output b. The protocol is adaptive because Bob’s input to the shared box depends on the message received from Alice.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares the power of adaptive versus non-adaptive Non-Signaling (NS) assistance in Prepare-and-Measure quantum communication models. It establishes that while NS assistance can simulate full quantum communication, adaptivity only yields a strict advantage over non-adaptivity under specific structural conditions related to input discrimination tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical bounds on what can be achieved using correlations weaker than entanglement but stronger than classical communication, offering insights into resource theories beyond standard quantum channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the operational advantage of using adaptive versus non-adaptive Non-Signaling (NS) assistance in Prepare-and-Measure (PM) quantum communication scenarios.

**Main result.** Adaptive NS assistance provides a strict advantage over non-adaptive assistance only in specific PM scenarios characterized by input-discrimination inequalities. Furthermore, non-adaptive NS assistance is shown to be powerful enough to simulate quantum communication for the same message dimension.

**Method.** The authors characterize achievable probability distributions using linear programming techniques and structural reductions, comparing sets defined by adaptive vs. non-adaptive correlation models.

**Model / system.** The model is a Prepare-and-Measure (PM) scenario $(n_X; n_Y, n_B)_{nA}$, where Alice prepares a message $a$ based on input $x$, and Bob measures given input $y$ and the message $a$. The resource analyzed is Non-Signaling (NS) correlation assistance.

**Key observables.** The achievable probability distributions $q(b|x y)$ characterizing the communication behavior, and derived constraints like $\max_{x \in [nX]} q(b|x, y) \le nA$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Message dimension ($n_A$), input dimensions ($n_X, n_Y$), and output dimension ($n_B$). The comparison hinges on the relationship between $n_A$ and the system sizes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to classical communication channels assisted by NS correlations. Key results rely on reducing general scenarios to single-input subscenarios ($n_Y=1$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics for non-adaptive and adaptive NS assistance, contrasting the mathematical definitions of the achievable sets.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically defines the PM model, characterizes behaviors under classical randomness, then analyzes the constraints imposed by non-adaptive NS assistance (showing it simulates quantum communication). Finally, it rigorously compares this to adaptive NS assistance, identifying the precise conditions for a strict advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extracting the full power of non-local correlations in prepare-and-measure (PM) scenarios requires precise control over the timing and structure of the receiver's measurements. Indeed, recent developments in entanglement-assisted classical communication scenarios have shown that adaptive strategies-where the receiver uses the transmitted message to guide their measurement choice-can outperform standard non-adaptive protocols. Moving beyond quantum theory, however, the ultimate limits of such advantages remain largely unexplored. In this work, we thoroughly study adaptive and non-adaptive non-signaling (NS) assistance in PM scenarios with classical communication. We provide simple characterizations of the sets of behaviors that can be realized using both non-adaptive and adaptive NS assistance in arbitrary PM scenarios. As a consequence, we show that non-adaptive NS assistance is already strong enough to reproduce quantum communication with the same message dimension: the transmission of a qudit can be simulated by a classical dit assisted non-adaptively by NS correlations. We then compare adaptive and non-adaptive NS assistance. We prove that any adaptive NS advantage can be traced back to scenarios in which the receiver has no measurement choice, ruling out the genuinely multi-setting advantages found in entanglement-assisted quantum protocols. Finally, we identify all PM scenarios where adaptive NS strategies provide a strict advantage over non-adaptive ones.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the critical challenge of deploying Quantum Illumination through turbulent air by developing a sophisticated, data-driven framework. It merges macroscopic meteorological measurements with microscopic quantum channel dynamics using Kolmogorov-Arnold networks. This allows for the dynamic prediction and quantification of quantum advantage across diverse, real-world atmospheric conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges classical atmospheric physics, advanced machine learning, and quantum optics by providing a predictive tool for maintaining quantum coherence in harsh real-world environments. The use of KAN for physical parameterization is particularly novel.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is quantifying how atmospheric turbulence degrades the quantum advantage offered by Quantum Illumination (QI) for target detection in free-space channels.

**Main result.** The authors propose a physics-driven framework using Kolmogorov-Arnold networks to map standard meteorological variables directly to the temporal evolution of the quantum advantage, enabling all-weather operation.

**Method.** They integrate advanced machine learning (KAN) with established atmospheric models (MOST theory) to create an end-to-end system that predicts turbulence effects on QI performance.

**Model / system.** The physical system is free-space quantum illumination using a Two-Mode Squeezed Vacuum state. The model links macroscopic weather data (T, P, GHI, etc.) through boundary layer physics to the microscopic degradation of quantum correlations.

**Key observables.** Quantum advantage ($\log_{10} R$), average error probability ($ar{P}_{	ext{err}}$), and the refractive index structure constant ($C_n^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Meteorological parameters include Global Horizontal Irradiance (GHI), longwave radiation ($L_{\downarrow}$), dew depression, and wind speed ($u$). Key regimes are defined by GHI levels (day vs. night).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on coupling the MOST theory to deep learning models; initial simplifications may assume near-ground horizontal channels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between different turbulence prediction models (KAN vs. AGA-BP) and show how QI advantage peaks under specific, narrow atmospheric conditions (e.g., twilight transitions or zero GHI with strong $L_{\downarrow}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical problem (QI in turbulence), detailing the necessary meteorological modeling (MOST/Energy Balance), introducing the advanced ML framework (KAN), and finally applying this integrated system to predict optimal operational windows for quantum sensing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum illumination offers a significant theoretical advantage for target detection in high background noise environments. However, its practical deployment in free-space channels is hindered by atmospheric turbulence. Stochastic fluctuations in atmospheric turbulence inevitably degrade the quantum signature, rendering the real-time evaluation of quantum advantage under such dynamic conditions a critical yet unresolved challenge. To circumvent the reliance on costly direct turbulence measurements, we propose a physics-driven framework that integrates Kolmogorov-Arnold networks directly bridge macroscopic meteorological observations with microscopic quantum channel dynamics. Trained on 105,120 samples from 12 climatically diverse sites and validated on 26,280 unseen samples from three extreme boundary conditions (arid continental, tropical maritime, high-altitude plateau), our approach establishes a physically consistent mapping from standard meteorological variables to the temporal evolution of the quantum advantage. This end-to-end system dynamically quantifies the degradation of quantum advantage across diverse turbulence conditions. Our results provide a rigorous theoretical and data-driven pathway for environmental adaptation, facilitating the transition of quantum radar networks from proof-of-principle demonstrations to all-weather operational systems.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00266v1>

</figure>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a rigorous framework to calculate the 'quantum complexity resource' from mixed Gaussian states of light. By formulating this as a convex optimization problem, the authors derive that while simple cases yield closed-form solutions, the general two-mode case requires finding the root of a high-degree polynomial, proving that no simple analytical solution exists.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides deep mathematical insight into the limitations of finding closed-form solutions for fundamental physical quantities, connecting quantum resource theory to advanced algebraic geometry (Galois theory).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the problem of quantifying a 'quantum complexity resource' inherent in mixed Gaussian states of multimode light.

**Main result.** For the two-mode case, the optimal quantum resource cannot be expressed in a closed form using elementary functions; instead, it is determined as the least real root of a degree-12 polynomial.

**Method.** The problem is formulated and solved via convex optimization (SDP), utilizing dual maximization techniques and analyzing necessary conditions derived from KKT multipliers.

**Model / system.** The system models multimode light using continuous variables, characterized by the quadrature covariance matrix V. The analysis focuses specifically on the two-mode case where modes are genuinely coupled.

**Key observables.** Quantum Advantage Resource ($N_q(V)$), $	ext{Tr}(V_{\star q})$, and the degree-12 polynomial $P(x; V)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of nonclassical directions ($\kappa$), which dictates whether a closed-form solution exists.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on Slater's condition (V must be strictly mixed) and proceeds by analyzing structured regimes ($\kappa=0, 1, 2$) before tackling the generic coupled case.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the optimization problem for the quantum resource to solving it in specific low-dimensional sectors (e.g., $\kappa=1$), culminating in the derivation of a general polynomial equation whose root yields the solution for generic coupled states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the problem of extracting a quantum complexity resource from a mixed Gaussian state of the multimode light. We present the first complete, certificate-checked solution to this problem in a genuinely coupled sector. We carry this out for the two-mode case, the smallest case in which modes are genuinely coupled. Even in this case the solution is highly nontrivial, and we rigorously prove that it cannot be given in a closed form.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes Type-IIB axion-dilaton wormholes, distinguishing between stable BPS instantons and unstable non-BPS wormhole deformations. The core mathematical achievement is proving that the fluctuation spectrum around the BPS endpoint factorizes into a simple quadratic form of an operator $Q^\dagger Q$. This provides strong structural constraints on quantum gravity solutions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly specialized in quantum gravity, the rigorous mathematical techniques used—such as deriving physical operators by eliminating constrained degrees of freedom (Schur complement) and proving spectral properties via operator factorization—are analogous to methods used in effective field theories or open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits Type-IIB axion--dilaton Euclidean saddles, comparing the BPS instanton ($E=0$) to non-BPS wormholes ($E>0$). It aims to provide a rigorous understanding of the spectrum and stability of these gravitational solutions.

**Main result.** The physical Hessian for the $E=0$ BPS instanton factorizes as $\mathcal{H}_
u = Q^\dagger_
u Q_
u$, establishing an endpoint theorem for the BPS sector. The analysis separates the wormhole throat structure from its long-distance multipole operator term.

**Method.** The methodology involves variational analysis of the Euclidean action, deriving radial equations, and applying constraint solving (Hamiltonian/gauge fixing) to reduce the quadratic fluctuation action to a physical Hessian operator.

**Model / system.** The system is Type IIB string theory compactified on geometries supporting axion-dilaton fields. The analysis covers both asymptotically flat 4D and AdS$_5$ backgrounds, treating wormholes as deformations of BPS instantons.

**Key observables.** The spectrum of the physical Hessian ($\mathcal{H}_
u$), which determines stability; the energy parameter $E$; and the charge sector invariants ($q_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Energy parameter $E$ (determining BPS vs. non-BPS); neck radius $a_c$; and background curvature scales (e.g., AdS length scale).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Hessian factorization theorem is strictly proven only for the physical domain corresponding to the $E=0$ BPS instanton, not necessarily for the full $E>0$ wormhole stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the general Euclidean action and radial equations. It then focuses on deriving the physical Hessian for the BPS endpoint ($E=0$) via constraint imposition, demonstrating its factorization. Finally, it compares this result to non-BPS wormholes and discusses dimensional reductions (4D vs AdS$_5$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

I revisit Type-IIB axion--dilaton Euclidean saddles in a specified axion charge sector. In that sector, the solution with $E=0$ is the BPS instanton, while $E>0$ gives non-BPS wormholes with a smooth throat. The two cases solve the same radial equations but define different fluctuation problems. For the $E=0$ instanton, the Hamiltonian constraint, gauge quotient, charge-sector boundary condition, and removal of collective zero modes reduce the quadratic action to a physical Hessian. This Hessian factorizes, $ {\cal H}_ν={\mathcal Q}_ν^\dagger{\mathcal Q}_ν$. I interpret this as an endpoint theorem, beyond a stability theorem for the full $E>0$ wormhole. This puts Type IIB wormhole spectra on firmer grounds. I also separate the connected two-ended wormhole throat from its long-distance two-end multipole operator term. Once the coefficient matrix $C^{ij}$ is derived, the different-component and same-component placements of the two end insertions are terms in the same quadratic expression. Removing either term requires a genuine projection or cancellation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the critical challenge of circuit scheduling on multi-core quantum processors by proposing a dependency-aware greedy algorithm. This method allows for fine-grained parallelism, significantly improving execution speed compared to simpler layer-based approaches. The results demonstrate that intelligent scheduling can drastically reduce makespan and better utilize hardware resources in these complex quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical scheduling algorithms for quantum hardware, the analysis of communication bottlenecks (teleportation latency) and resource management in a distributed quantum setting is highly relevant to understanding physical constraints in large-scale quantum computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing effective circuit scheduling algorithms for multi-core quantum systems to minimize the overall execution time (makespan).

**Main result.** Dependency-aware greedy scheduling achieves substantial performance gains, showing an average 40% reduction in makespan compared to baseline layered scheduling on real benchmarks.

**Method.** The authors propose a dependency-aware greedy scheduling strategy that schedules each gate at the earliest possible time based on resource and data dependencies, contrasting it with rigid layer-by-layer execution.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a multi-core quantum computing architecture utilizing an n x n 2D mesh topology. Inter-core operations rely on quantum teleportation over a Network-on-Chip (NoC).

**Key observables.** Makespan ($	ext{T}_{	ext{total}}$), Core Utilization, and Communication Overlap Ratio ($	ext{R}_{	ext{comm}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The communication delay is proportional to the number of hops ($H 	imes \lambda_{	ext{hop}}$), which is a critical bottleneck.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption addressed is that fine-grained parallelism can be exploited by overlapping computation and communication latency, overcoming limitations imposed by strict layered scheduling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the multi-core architecture (NoC/Quantum Channel), compare makespans between layered vs. greedy scheduling using case studies, and show performance metrics across synthetic and real quantum circuits (QAOA, QFT).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical model and the limitations of baseline layered scheduling. It then details the proposed dependency-aware greedy algorithm, followed by extensive evaluation on both synthetic random circuits and established real benchmarks to validate performance gains.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-core quantum computing architectures have emerged as a promising solution to the qubit scalability limitations of monolithic NISQ devices. Quantum algorithms are expressed as quantum circuits composed of single- and two-qubit gates. However, circuit scheduling in multi-core quantum systems remains largely unexplored. Reducing overall execution time (makespan), increasing core utilization, and hiding communication latency behind computation depends on effective scheduling. In this paper, we first introduce a layered scheduling approach as a baseline where quantum gates within the same layer are executed in parallel, while layers themselves are executed sequentially. We then propose a greedy scheduling strategy which schedules each gate as soon as all its dependencies and required resources are available. This allows fine-grained parallelism across cores. Our evaluation shows that on real benchmarks, greedy scheduling achieves an average 40% reduction in makespan and improvement in core utilization. The results suggest that the use of intelligent circuit scheduling to exploit parallelism can greatly enhance the speed of circuit execution in multi-core quantum architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QPipe, an advanced LLM-based multi-agent system designed to automate the complex process of turning natural language requirements into working quantum applications for test optimization. By coordinating specialized agents—such as those handling encoding, code generation, and review—QPipe achieves high success rates in generating executable circuits that solve real-world problems better than classical benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on SE optimization rather than fundamental physics models, the reliance on QUBO/Ising formulations directly connects to quantum computational chemistry and materials science problems often modeled by Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is autonomously translating high-level, natural-language (NL) task requirements for Software Engineering optimization into fully executable quantum application workflows.

**Main result.** The proposed QPipe agentic system successfully generates executable quantum applications that solve test optimization problems, with generated solutions outperforming a classical genetic algorithm baseline in most tested cases.

**Method.** A multi-agent LLM architecture (QPipe) is employed to guide the process through specialized agents responsible for parsing requirements, formulating QUBO/Ising models, generating code, reviewing logic, and executing the quantum workflow.

**Model / system.** The system operates on quantum computing principles, encoding optimization problems into QUBO or Ising Hamiltonians ($\min x^	op Qx$). The execution targets ideal simulators using frameworks like Qiskit to run algorithms such as QAOA.

**Key observables.** Success rates for compilation (100%) and application execution (96.7%); solution quality measured by the QUBO-value reduction ratio (QRR) compared to a GA baseline; generation cost in seconds and tokens.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system manages resource constraints like qubit budgets, often decomposing large problems into smaller, manageable subproblems for sequential processing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The evaluation assumes ideal quantum simulators are used, ignoring real-world noise or device constraints. The success relies on the stability of formulation patterns (QUBO/Ising) and LLM reasoning capabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the end-to-end workflow from NL input to executable output via specialized agents; pattern analysis shows successful runs consistently use QUBO formulations and QAOA algorithms, often decomposing problems into 4-6 subproblems.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the QPipe architecture, which involves sequential stages: requirement parsing $ightarrow$ quantum suitability analysis $ightarrow$ workflow planning/encoding $ightarrow$ code generation $ightarrow$ multi-agent review $ightarrow$ execution sandbox $ightarrow$ result combination and verification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing is increasingly explored for software engineering (SE) optimization, but translating natural-language (NL) task-level requirements into executable quantum applications still demands substantial quantum and programming expertise. We present QPipe, a large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent architecture that autonomously turns NL requirements into traceable quantum-application workflows through specialized agents for requirement parsing, formulation, code generation, review, execution, and verification. We evaluate QPipe on 20 NL requirements, each associated with a real-world benchmark and a test-optimization problem. QPipe successfully completes the key stages of quantum-application generation across requirements, achieving average rates of 100% for code compilation and 96.7% for application execution and final-result combination, with average generation costs of 260.1 seconds and 1.89M tokens per requirement. Among the generated quantum applications that execute successfully, the returned solutions outperform the offline genetic algorithm baseline in most cases. Ablation results further show that QPipe's advantage depends on retaining code-generation skills, task knowledge, review feedback, and multi-agent decomposition. These results indicate that agentic coordination can support generation of executable quantum applications for tackling test optimization problems from real-world benchmarks.

</details>

**Summary.** The research investigates the robust superconductivity in the topological semimetal YPtBi. By measuring transport properties across samples with varying levels of disorder and carrier concentration, the authors find that $T_c$ remains remarkably stable. This resilience suggests that the superconducting transition is governed by phase stiffness rather than conventional pairing strength, highlighting a new form of protection in high-spin superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental evidence for superconductivity whose critical temperature is insensitive to disorder and doping, challenging standard BCS predictions and pointing towards exotic pairing mechanisms relevant to strongly correlated topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the resilience of $j=3/2$ superconductivity in the topological semimetal YPtBi against various perturbations, including magnetic and nonmagnetic disorder and changes in carrier density.

**Main result.** The superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) exhibits remarkable robustness across large variations in disorder and doping levels, suggesting that the transition is governed by phase stiffness rather than conventional pair formation amplitude.

**Method.** A combination of electrical transport measurements (resistivity, Hall effect) and quantum oscillation measurements (Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations) were performed on various sample types.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the topological semimetal YPtBi, which possesses strong spin-orbit coupling leading to a $j=3/2$ quasiparticle manifold near the Fermi level. The system is tested by introducing controlled disorder (Au) and varying carrier concentration via doping ($	ext{Ca}, 	ext{Nd}$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), residual resistivity ratio, quantum oscillation frequency ($F$) and effective mass ($m^*$), and Hall coefficient derived carrier density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder levels (varying $ho_0$), carrier densities ($n$ spanning orders of magnitude), and magnetic fields up to 41.5 T.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The robustness suggests the pairing mechanism is protected, potentially limited by superfluid phase stiffness rather than pair formation energy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate transport behavior changes across different samples (Fig. 1); quantum oscillation analysis confirms periodicity and allows extraction of $m^*$ and $F$ (Fig. 2); angular measurements confirm the $j=3/2$ Fermi surface structure (Fig. 3); and a summary figure shows $T_c$'s insensitivity to disorder and doping (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes multiple sample types (pure, disordered, doped) using transport measurements, analyzes quantum oscillations to map the Fermi surface and electronic structure changes, and culminates in arguing that the observed $T_c$ robustness points toward a phase stiffness-limited superconducting mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cooper pairing in most of the known fermionic superfluids occurs via spin-1/2 quasiparticle interactions that lead to spin-singlet or spin-triplet pairing. In the topological semimetal YPtBi, strong spin-orbit coupling results in a band inversion between highly symmetric $s$- and $p$-like electronic bands and a degeneracy at the $Γ$ point that ensures the manifold of $j$=3/2 quasiparticle states thrive near the Fermi level, where superconducting pairing occurs. Here we study the effects of magnetic and nonmagnetic disorder and carrier density on this exotic superconducting pairing state. By varying levels of disorder and carrier densities by nearly two and three orders of magnitude, respectively, we show that the superconducting critical temperature of YPtBi has a remarkable robustness, with little variation across this span. Our results suggest that superconductivity in YPtBi may reside in a regime where phase stiffness, rather than pair formation, governs the transition temperature. The insensitivity of Cooper pairing to dramatic changes in quasiparticle environment in a $j$=3/2 superconductor highlights a new form of protection realized in topological high-spin superconductors.

</details>
