**Summary.** This experimental study investigates how modifying the structure of Vanadium superconducting films using buffer and capping layers affects their microwave loss in resonators. By correlating structural data with $Q_{	ext{int}}$ measurements, the authors pinpoint non-TLS losses originating from surface oxides or hydrides as a key limitation for quantum circuit development.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the practical challenge of material engineering for quantum devices by linking microscopic defects (oxides, grain boundaries) to macroscopic performance metrics ($Q_{int}$), which is crucial for designing reliable superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how material properties, particularly surface chemistry and structural quality, influence microwave losses in superconducting Vanadium (V) films used in quantum circuits.

**Main result.** While buffer layers improve crystal structure, the presence of V oxides or hydrides at grain boundaries appears to be a dominant source of non-TLS loss, which can be mitigated by capping layers like Tantalum (Ta).

**Method.** The research combines structural characterization (XRD, AFM) with electrical and microwave measurements ($Q_{int}$ vs. $\langle n_{	ext{ph}} angle$) and surface analysis (XPS, EDS).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting resonator built from V films on Si wafers, modified by Nb buffer layers and Ta capping layers. Loss mechanisms are analyzed using the photon-number dependence of the internal quality factor ($Q_{int}$).

**Key observables.** Internal quality factor ($Q_{	ext{int}}$), non-TLS loss ($\delta_{	ext{other}}$), lattice orientation uniformity (XRD FWHM), and surface oxidation states (XPS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon number ($\langle n_{	ext{ph}} angle$), temperature (down to 10 mK), film thickness, and buffer/capping layer materials (Nb, Ta).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The microwave loss can be modeled by separating contributions into Two-Level System (TLS) losses and photon-number-independent non-TLS losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD profiles confirming lattice coherence enhancement with Nb buffers; AFM images detail surface roughness changes; $Q_{int}$ vs. $\langle n_{	ext{ph}} angle$ plots analyze loss mechanisms, and XPS spectra map out various V and Ta oxide states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from structural characterization (Nb buffer effect) to electrical property analysis ($	ext{Tc}$, $	ext{RRR}$), then focuses on microwave loss modeling ($Q_{int}$ vs. $\langle n_{	ext{ph}} angle$), and finally correlates these losses with detailed surface chemistry findings (oxides/hydrides).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the correlation between material properties and microwave losses in superconducting films is a crucial subject for developing low-loss materials for quantum circuits. We focus on vanadium (V) as a novel material for superconducting quantum devices and discuss loss in V films in relation to their structural properties. Using a sputtering method, we grow four V-film structures on (001)-oriented Si wafers, employing Nb and Ta as the buffer and capping layer materials, respectively: Nb/V/Ta, Nb/V, V/Ta, and V. X-ray diffraction and atomic force microscopy reveal that the V films grown on the Nb buffer layers have higher uniformity of lattice orientation and smaller grain size than that directly grown on the Si wafer. Coplanar waveguide resonators are fabricated from the four V-film structures, and averaged photon number ($\langle n_{\rm ph} \rangle$) dependences of internal quality factor ($Q_{\rm int}$) are obtained by performing microwave measurements. By analyzing the obtained $Q_{\rm int}$ vs $\langle n_{\rm ph} \rangle$, it is found that loss at the V surface is dominated by $\langle n_{\rm ph} \rangle$-independent non-two-level-system (non-TLS) losses, which can be mitigated by introducing the Ta capping layer. Furthermore, the V films on the Nb buffer layers exhibit lower $Q_{\rm int}$ in the $\langle n_{\rm ph} \rangle$ range from 10$^{0}$ to 10$^{6}$ and higher non-TLS loss than that directly grown on Si wafers, even though the former has higher lattice-orientation uniformity than the latter. Origins of these trends might be relevant to V oxides, of which presence at surfaces and grain boundaries in bulk regions in the V resonators is suggested by energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy and X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy, and/or V hydrides.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses the framework of the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator to provide a novel derivation for the Wallis product and its reciprocal. By analyzing how the variational energy ratio behaves for odd versus even effective angular parameters, the authors show that quantum mechanics unifies these two distinct mathematical constants. This connects fundamental number theory results to radial quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO systems like cold atoms or trapped ions, the use of quantum mechanics (variational methods on radial equations) to derive fundamental mathematical constants is a powerful demonstration of physical modeling in pure mathematics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives the mathematical identities of the Wallis product and its reciprocal using a quantum mechanical variational approach.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the singular harmonic oscillator provides a unified framework from which both the standard and reciprocal Wallis products can be derived by examining odd and even sequences of an effective angular parameter $\nu$.

**Method.** The core method is a variational calculation applied to the radial Schrödinger equation for the 4D singular harmonic oscillator, utilizing specific trial wave functions.

**Model / system.** The model is the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator with an inverse-square interaction term. The analysis leverages the Kustaanheimo--Stiefel relation connecting it to the 3D Coulomb problem.

**Key observables.** The accuracy ratio $R_{4d}(\nu)$ derived from the variational energy, which approaches unity in the large-$\nu$ limit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective angular parameter $\nu$, and its specific sequences: odd ($\nu=2n-1$) and even ($\nu=2n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the validity of the variational upper bound and asymptotic approximations for Gamma functions in the large-$\nu$ limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the Hamiltonian, applies the variational method using a quartic trial family, calculates the energy ratio $R_{4d}(\nu)$, and then analyzes the limits for odd and even $\nu$ to recover the known infinite products.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a variational derivation of the Wallis product and its reciprocal from the four-dimensional singular harmonic oscillator. The inverse-square interaction is absorbed into an effective angular parameter $ν$, so that the lowest exact energy in a fixed sector is $E_{4d,\mathrm{exact}}=\hbarω(ν+2)$. Motivated by the radial Kustaanheimo--Stiefel relation $r=ρ^2$ between the four-dimensional oscillator and the three-dimensional Coulomb problem, we use the quartic trial family $R_a(ρ)=Nρ^νe^{-aρ^4}$. The minimized variational energy yields an accuracy ratio governed by adjacent Gamma functions. In the large-$ν$ semiclassical limit, this ratio approaches unity. Restricting $ν$ to the odd sequence $ν=2n-1$ gives the standard Wallis product, whereas the even sequence $ν=2n$ gives its reciprocal form. The Coulomb-dual interpretation further relates the two branches to integer and half-integer effective angular sectors in the dual Coulomb/MICZ description. The result shows that Wallis-type infinite products persist under an inverse-square deformation of the oscillator and arise from a common Gamma-function structure in radial variational dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, purely differential derivation of the Obara-Saika recurrence relation used to calculate electron repulsion integrals (ERIs) in quantum chemistry. By deriving these relations solely from the derivatives of Gaussian basis functions, the authors provide a self-contained and pedagogically valuable method. This new formulation is designed to expose independent primitive derivative terms, making the calculation highly suitable for modern parallel hardware like GPUs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum chemistry integrals rather than AMO systems, the rigorous development of a computationally efficient, parallelizable mathematical framework for calculating complex multi-electron interactions is highly relevant to any field involving many-body electronic structure calculations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a novel, rigorous derivation for calculating complex two-electron repulsion integrals (ERIs) required in quantum chemistry calculations.

**Main result.** The authors successfully derive the Obara-Saika recurrence relation using only differential relations between Gaussian basis functions, yielding a hierarchical formulation of the integral calculation.

**Method.** The method involves applying repeated differentiation operators ($\partial/\partial A_i$) to the ERI expressions and utilizing established mathematical identities (like Lemma 1) to build up higher-order integrals from lower ones.

**Model / system.** The calculations are performed for electronic structure problems using Gaussian basis sets, specifically focusing on two-center four-orbital electron repulsion integrals ($\langle ss|ss angle$, etc.).

**Key observables.** Electron Repulsion Integrals (ERIs), the Obara-Saika recurrence relation (OS-VRR), and independent primitive derivative quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gaussian coefficients, nuclear coordinates ($A, B, C, D$), and angular momentum indices.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the mathematical validity of applying differential operators to Gaussian basis functions; numerical instability is noted as a practical limitation when using recursion for Boys functions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes focus heavily on equations and derivations rather than figure descriptions).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for ERI calculation, introducing the differential approach based on Gaussian derivatives, deriving recurrence relations step-by-step using operators, and finally comparing the resulting relation to the established OS method.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Obara-Saika (OS) method is one of the most widely used techniques in quantum chemistry for evaluating electron repulsion integrals (ERIs) via a set of recurrence relations that build higher angular momentum integrals from lower-order ones. The original derivation by Obara and Saika proceeded by directly relating integrals of differing angular momentum. In this work, we present a compact novel derivation of the OS vertical recurrence relation based solely on differential relations between Gaussian basis functions, expanding on a method suggested in earlier work. By explicitly deriving the required derivative expressions we identify all non-zero primitive terms contributing to the full ERI to develop a hierarchical formulation of the OS recursion relations. This approach has pedagogical value as a rigorous and self-contained derivation. Additionally, the resulting organization exposes independent primitive derivative quantities and may be useful for code generation and parallel implementations on modern GPU architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work compares CV-QNNs against DV-QNNs for classifying semiconductor wafer defects using a controlled hybrid architecture. The results strongly indicate that CV approaches offer superior performance, especially when distinguishing subtle spatial defect patterns. This guides the choice of quantum paradigm for industrial AI acceleration.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML application, the rigorous comparison between continuous vs. discrete encoding methods provides insights into the fundamental representational power limits of different quantum information processing models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares Continuous-Variable (CV) versus Discrete-Variable (DV) quantum neural network paradigms to determine which is best suited for high-stakes wafer-map defect classification in semiconductor manufacturing.

**Main result.** The CV head consistently outperforms the DV head, showing a significant advantage on spatially localized defects like Edge-Loc. This suggests structured continuous encoding is superior for this task.

**Method.** A controlled evaluation protocol was used where only the quantum classification head (CV-QNN or DV-QNN) was varied while keeping the feature extraction CNN backbone identical.

**Model / system.** The system involves classifying eight defect classes from a wafer map dataset (WM-811K). The models are hybrid, combining classical CNN features with quantum circuits operating on either continuous variables (CV) or discrete qubits (DV).

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy and recall, particularly for the spatially structured 'Edge-Loc' class.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qumodes/qubits tested (3, 4, 8); input noise level ($\sigma$); circuit depth; feature vector dimension (512).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The CV advantage is attributed to structured design and continuous phase-space encoding, not necessarily genuine non-Gaussianity at the tested cutoff ($d=2$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show performance comparisons across different defect classes, noise robustness sweeps (CV vs DV), and depth-resolved hardware accuracy degradation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem context in semiconductor yield, details the controlled comparison setup (shared CNN backbone + interchangeable quantum heads), presents quantitative results showing CV superiority, diagnoses the physical limitations of DV circuits, and discusses implications for future quantum hardware design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Realizing quantum neural networks (QNNs) in industry requires knowing which quantum computing paradigm suits which task. Motivated by AI accelerators and high-bandwidth memory, where die stacking makes wafer-level defect screening central to yield, we study WM-811K wafer-map defect classification (eight classes), comparing the dominant paradigms, continuous-variable (CV) and discrete-variable (DV), under controlled conditions. To isolate the quantum circuit as the sole variable, a shared convolutional backbone (~4.3M parameters) feeds interchangeable heads (classical dense, CV-QNN, or DV-QNN) as the only structural difference; each quantum head is scaled over three sizes (3, 4, 8 qumodes/qubits). The CV head consistently outperforms the DV head: at four qumodes/qubits it reaches 79.7 +/- 1.8% accuracy versus 61.6 +/- 1.4%, a non-overlapping 18-point gap. The advantage is sharpest on the spatially localized Edge-Loc class, easily confused with Scratch, which CV recovers with recall 0.66 +/- 0.06 while DV fails at every size (<=0.05), showing the structured CV layer better captures fine spatial distinctions between defect types. Training curves show the DV limitation is a representational-capacity ceiling, not an optimization failure; at the Fock cutoff used here (d = 2) the CV advantage reflects two intrinsic properties, a structured, neural-network-analogue layer and continuous phase-space encoding, not Hilbert-space dimensionality. On IBM hardware, DV accuracy holds at shallow depth, degrading only at the deepest circuit. Both quantum heads remain below the classical baseline (85.0%), but the controlled setting isolates where a structured head already helps and, as noise and scale improve, which paradigm can deliver practical advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a comprehensive mathematical framework—a 'toolbox'—for designing Geometric Quantum Machine Learning (GQML) models tailored for graph structures. By rigorously enforcing symmetry constraints derived from the permutation group $S_n$, it provides systematic methods to construct, characterize, and improve the expressivity of quantum circuits used in quantum machine learning tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical grounding in symmetry principles (group representation theory) applied directly to quantum computation models for structured data like graphs, which is highly relevant for designing physically constrained quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to develop a rigorous framework for Geometric Quantum Machine Learning (GQML) models applied to graph problems, addressing the lack of a unifying design perspective compared to classical GML.

**Main result.** A comprehensive 'toolbox' is provided to systematically characterize and construct equivariant quantum graph models, demonstrating benefits like natural integration with classical methods and enabling classical pre-training strategies.

**Method.** The authors employ group theory (specifically the permutation group $S_n$) to define equivariance/invariance for quantum operators and maps, leading to a systematic basis construction via symmetrization (twirling).

**Model / system.** The model involves encoding $n$-node graphs into $n$-qubit states ($ho_\alpha(x)$) and utilizing general QML templates involving an encoding, unitary evolution ($U_	heta$), and measurement ($O$). The symmetry group acting on the system is $S_n$.

**Key observables.** Equivariant observables (symmetric or equivariant tensors of Pauli strings), expectation values $	ext{Tr} [O_	heta ho_\alpha(x)]$, and classification outputs derived from these expectations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $n$ (number of nodes/qubits), circuit depth $L$, and the set of parameters $	heta$ governing the unitary evolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining equivariance with respect to group actions ($S_n$) and assumes that model expressivity can be systematically increased by incorporating all compatible equivariant observables.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the proposed toolbox for GQML construction, showing its use across encoding, measurement identification, and classical integration. Figure 2 summarizes $S_n$ actions on quantum spaces, while later figures show performance comparisons between pre-trained vs. random models.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from background theory (defining equivariance for QML) to building the toolbox by characterizing equivariant linear maps ($	ext{Hom}_{	ext{S}_n}(V, W)$), and finally applying this toolbox to construct and test specific GQML architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Geometric Machine Learning (GML) successes have been achieved through the thorough study and design of new equivariant neural networks. In comparison, geometric quantum machine learning (GQML) models lack such a detailed understanding and, despite already several proposals, a unifying perspective on their design remains elusive. In this work, we focus on GQML models for graph problems that showcase a lot of structure and still remain frontier in machine learning. For the case when n-node graphs are encoded in n-qubit states, we provide a comprehensive characterization of their constituents. Taken together, these furnish us with a toolbox for the design of quantum graph models, and we further probe its benefits including the natural integration with classical models, generalization of known GQML models (sometimes extending their expressivity at virtually no cost), and straightforward classical pre-training strategies. The latter two features are demonstrated in dedicated numerical experiments.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00575v1>

**Summary.** The paper presents an advanced hybrid quantum algorithm combining operator elimination with a one-body downfolding framework (OBDF) to enhance ADAPT-VQE for electronic structure calculations. By pruning redundant operators and incorporating external correlation effects, the method achieves faster convergence and higher accuracy compared to standard approaches on molecular models like H6 and N2.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of applying quantum algorithms (like VQE) to complex many-body Hamiltonians, providing concrete algorithmic improvements for resource efficiency and systematic error reduction in correlated electron simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to reduce quantum resources and improve convergence stability for ADAPT-VQE, a method used for electronic structure calculations on near-term quantum devices.

**Main result.** Operator elimination significantly reduces circuit depth and iteration count, while combining this technique with the one-body downfolding framework (OBDF) yields energies closer to the FCI reference than standard methods.

**Method.** The work introduces plateau-operator elimination into ADAPT-VQE to prune redundant operators during ansatz construction. This is combined with OBDF, which replaces the bare Hamiltonian in the active space with a correlated effective Hamiltonian incorporating external correlation effects.

**Model / system.** The methodology is applied to quantum chemistry problems, benchmarked on molecular systems including linear H6 chains, H6 lattices, H6 rings, and the N2 molecule. The underlying physics involves solving electronic structure Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Energy expectation value (compared to FCI), CNOT gate counts (circuit depth), and convergence rate of energy gradients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The elimination threshold ($\delta$) for vanishing parameters, the active space definition, and the correlation effects captured by OBDF.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Permanent operator elimination is preferred over pool restoration due to observed instabilities; the assumption is that eliminating non-contributing operators reduces overhead without sacrificing accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how plateau-operator elimination stabilizes convergence (e.g., H6 chain), shows significant reduction in CNOT gate counts across various systems, and compares energy surfaces (PES) of H6/N2 using different levels of theory including OBDF-ADAPT-VQE.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the limitations of standard ADAPT-VQE. It then details the operator elimination strategy, showing its benefits in convergence speed and resource reduction. Finally, it integrates this with the OBDF framework to systematically improve accuracy by incorporating external correlation effects, benchmarking on multiple molecular systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Adaptive Derivative-Assembled Problem-Tailored variational quantum eigensolvers (ADAPT-VQE) represent one of the most promising approaches for quantum chemistry on near-term quantum devices. However, their optimization is slow and may stall due to vanishing parameters and redundant operators in the ansatz. In this work, we propose a simple strategy of operator elimination that removes non-contributing operators from the pool once they are detected, enabling the optimization to continue progressing toward convergence. We examine two variants, with and without pool restoration after elimination, and find that the former converges more smoothly and faster than the latter and the standard ADAPT-VQE. To capture dynamical correlations between the active space and its environment, we combine ADAPT-VQE with our recently developed downfolding approach, the one-body downfolding framework (OBDF). In OBDF, the bare molecular Hamiltonian in the active space is replaced by a correlated effective Hamiltonian that incorporates dynamical correlation effects outside the active space. We benchmark our implementation on a linear \ce{H_6} chain, an \ce{H_6} lattice, an \ce{H_6} ring, and the \ce{N_2} molecule using the OpenFermion simulator. Our results show that operator elimination significantly reduces circuit depth and iteration count, and that OBDF-ADAPT-VQE yields energies closer to the full configuration interaction (FCI) reference than the standard approach within the same active space.

</details>

<a id="paper-2607.00507"></a>

**Authors:** Shiroman Prakash  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.00507v1>

**Summary.** The paper proves an unconditional exponential memory advantage of Quantum Finite Automata over classical automata by exploiting graph-theoretic contextuality. By analyzing the Kochen-Specker Problem on specific graphs, it shows that the required quantum memory scales linearly with system size ($\xi(G)$), while the classical requirement scales exponentially ($\chi(G)$). This advantage is robust to common types of noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, quantifiable bounds showing how structural quantum resources (contextuality) translate into computational advantages that are robust against realistic physical noise models, which is crucial for building fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to demonstrate an exponential, noise-resilient memory advantage for quantum finite automata (QFAs) over classical deterministic finite automata (DFAs) by leveraging the structural resource of quantum contextuality.

**Main result.** An exponential separation is shown between the classical state requirement ($\chi(G)$) and the quantum memory dimension ($\xi(G)+1$) needed to solve a promise problem derived from the Kochen-Specker Problem (KSP).

**Method.** The analysis uses graph theory invariants—the chromatic number $\chi(G)$ and orthogonal rank $\xi(G)$—to establish bounds on classical vs. quantum memory resources for recognizing specific logical structures.

**Model / system.** The core model is the comparison between DFAs and QFAs solving a promise problem defined on an exclusivity graph $G$. The advantage is demonstrated using Boolean-orthogonality graphs ($\Omega_n$) or Kochen-Specker graphs derived from high-dimensional polytopes.

**Key observables.** Classical memory cost ($N = \chi(G)$) versus Quantum memory dimension ($d = \xi(G)+1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponential scaling difference between $\chi(G)$ and $\xi(G)$, and the maintenance of an $O(1)$ noise threshold against depolarizing/coherent noise.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The advantage relies on 'representational contextuality,' defined by the integer gap $\chi(G) > \xi(G)$, which is a stronger condition than statistical contextuality ($\chi_f(G)$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the graph structures (e.g., $\Omega_4$, 18-vertex graph, 60-ray orthogonality graph) and compare the exponential scaling of classical memory cost versus the linear scaling of quantum memory cost.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem using KSP on a graph $G$. It then proves the lower bound for DFAs ($\chi(G)$). Subsequently, it constructs the QFA solution using $\xi(G)+1$ dimensions and rigorously demonstrates the exponential separation and noise resilience.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum contextuality is widely recognized as an essential non-classical resource underlying quantum technology, yet illuminating the precise mechanisms through which it translates into unconditional computational advantages remains an ongoing challenge. We demonstrate an exponential, noise-resilient memory advantage for quantum finite automata arising from graph-theoretic approaches to contextuality. We define a promise problem on an exclusivity graph $G$ for which any classical deterministic automaton acts as a non-contextual hidden variable model requiring at least $N=χ(G)$ states, where $χ(G)$ is the graph's chromatic number. In contrast, by exploiting a structural phenomenon we term \textit{representational contextuality}, a QFA solves this task using a memory of dimension at most $d=ξ(G)+1$, where $ξ(G)$ is the graph's orthogonal rank. This separation scales exponentially ($d=\mathcal O(n)$ versus $N=2^{Ω(n)}$) for Boolean-orthogonality graphs. Crucially, this memory advantage maintains an $\mathcal{O}(1)$ threshold against both depolarizing and coherent noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This work unifies the study of scattering and bound states for the 1D Dirac equation by mapping it onto a solvable Schrödinger-like problem using symmetry constraints. By applying Supersymmetric Quantum Mechanics to potentials like the Pöschl–Teller, the authors derive closed-form expressions for transmission probabilities. This framework allows them to systematically locate both discrete bound states and continuous resonances via pole analysis in the complex momentum plane.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of SUSYQM and its connection to spectral properties (bound vs. resonance) provides powerful, exact analytical tools for studying relativistic quantum systems, which is highly relevant for understanding particle confinement or wave propagation in structured media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified theoretical framework to analyze both scattering phenomena (continuum spectrum) and bound states/resonances (discrete spectrum) for the one-dimensional Dirac equation.

**Main result.** It successfully maps the relativistic problem onto an effective Schrödinger-like equation, allowing the use of supersymmetric quantum mechanics techniques to derive closed-form expressions for transmission probabilities and characterize resonance poles in the complex momentum plane.

**Method.** The core methods involve symmetry reduction (spin/pseudospin), utilizing Supersymmetric Quantum Mechanics (SUSYQM) with shape invariance, and employing analytic continuation to extract bound states from scattering amplitudes.

**Model / system.** The system is a fermion described by the one-dimensional Dirac equation subjected to combined scalar and vector potentials. The analysis focuses on specific solvable models like the hyperbolic Pöschl–Teller potential.

**Key observables.** Transmission probability $T(k)$, reflection amplitude, bound-state energies (poles of $T(k)$), and resonance frequencies/quasi-normal modes (QNMs) found as poles in the complex momentum plane.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The sign of the potential parameter $V_0$ determines whether the system behaves like an attractive well (bound states) or a repulsive barrier (resonances).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the existence of spin-symmetry ($\Delta=0$) or pseudospin-symmetry ($\Sigma=0$) to reduce the coupled Dirac system, and assuming short-range interactions that vanish at infinity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy dependence of scattering quantities, showing how $T(k)$ approaches unity in certain limits, and depict the pole structure in the complex momentum plane for resonance analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the unified mapping from the Dirac equation to a Schrödinger-like problem. It then applies SUSYQM techniques using shape invariance to solve the Pöschl–Teller potential case, deriving scattering amplitudes, and finally analyzing the pole structure in the complex plane to distinguish between bound states and resonances.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a unified treatment of scattering and discrete spectra for the one-dimensional Dirac equation with scalar and vector interactions. Under the spin-symmetry condition, the coupled first-order Dirac system maps exactly onto an effective Sturm--Liouville (Schrö\-din\-ger-like) problem for a single spinor component. This mapping provides a convenient framework for analyzing transmission, reflection, and analytic continuation. As an explicit application, we consider effective interactions of hyperbolic Pöschl--Teller type and exploit supersymmetric quantum mechanics and shape invariance to obtain a closed-form expression for the transmission probability. The bound-state spectrum is then recovered from the poles of the analytically continued transmission amplitude, reproducing known results and offering a unified description of scattering and bound states. For the barrier configuration, we briefly comment on the resulting pole pattern in the complex momentum plane and its connection with resonance and quasi-normal-mode behavior. Moreover, we use the chiral transformation to relate the spin- and pseudospin-symmetry sectors and translate results between them without repeating the full derivation.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors experimentally study charge and spin dynamics in a double quantum dot subjected to nonequilibrium phonon irradiation. They observe that the system exhibits resonant tunneling at triplet resonance, and crucially, they demonstrate strong nonreciprocal electron transport cycles. This work establishes an accessible method for investigating directed current flow driven by energy gradients in nanoscale solid-state devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean experimental platform for studying non-equilibrium quantum transport and rectification effects driven by coupling to an external bath (phonons), which is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates real-time charge and spin dynamics in excited states of a double quantum dot (DQD) driven by nonequilibrium phonons to understand emergent functionalities beyond equilibrium.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that phonon irradiation induces resonant inter-dot tunneling at triplet resonance, leading to strong unidirectional electron transport cycles with high rectification ($\eta \approx 1$) when coupling is weak.

**Method.** Time-resolved charge sensing and waiting-time statistics are used to measure transition rates between different spin/charge states in the DQD under controlled phonon excitation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a semiconductor Double Quantum Dot (DQD) fabricated on an AlGaAs/GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure, which acts as the quantum device. Phonons are generated by applying a DC bias to a nearby source QD, creating a nonequilibrium phonon density gradient.

**Key observables.** Charge state transition time traces, inter-dot tunneling rates ($\Gamma_{ij}$), waiting-time histograms (single/double exponential), and the unidirectional transfer asymmetry ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phonon bias voltage ($V_{PS}$), detuning voltage ($V_R$), and the relative strength of inter-dot coupling versus dot-reservoir rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on rate-equation models for DQD spin states, and some fitting procedures assume specific functional forms for waiting-time distributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show charge stability diagrams revealing triplet resonance lines (Fig. 1c), time traces of transitions at both singlet and triplet resonances (Figs. 2a/b), histograms confirming double exponential behavior at ES, and finally, current measurements demonstrating unidirectional cycles with high rectification ($\eta$) (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the basic physics by observing resonant tunneling at GS vs. ES; it then analyzes detailed rate dynamics using waiting-time statistics to distinguish spin-conserving versus spin-flip processes; finally, it focuses on the nonreciprocal transport regime induced by phonon gradients.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven nonequilibrium systems can host emergent functionalities beyond equilibrium, but real-time access to excited-state dynamics remains limited. Here we report real-time measurements of phonon-driven charge and spin dynamics in excited states of a double quantum dot. Under phonon irradiation, resonant inter-dot tunneling emerges at triplet resonance. Time-resolved charge sensing reveals that the resonant inter-dot tunneling is strongly modified by spin blockade. For weaker inter-dot coupling, the nonequilibrium phonon environment generates a unidirectional transport cycle along the phonon density gradient.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a unified theory for the Spin Hall effect in Dirac electrons by calculating spin and magnetic moment accumulation coefficients ($	ext{SAC}$ and $	ext{MMAC}$). Using the T-matrix approximation, the authors predict three distinct scaling regimes based on impurity scattering strength. The key finding is a novel scaling exponent (0.6) in the dirty regime, providing a comprehensive picture of spin transport beyond simple current definitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport rather than quantum information directly, the rigorous theoretical framework involving non-equilibrium Green's functions, symmetry analysis (time-reversal/inversion), and scaling laws in disordered systems is highly relevant to understanding decoherence and topological effects in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on developing a unified theory for the Spin Hall (SH) effect in Dirac electrons, specifically addressing ambiguities related to defining spin current.

**Main result.** The authors found three distinct scaling regimes for the spin and magnetic-moment accumulation coefficients ($	ext{SAC}$ and $	ext{MMAC}$) as impurity scattering changes, with a novel exponent of 0.6 in the dirty regime that differs from the anomalous Hall effect's scaling.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations utilizing the self-consistent T-matrix approximation within linear response theory framework applied to Green's functions for Dirac electrons.

**Model / system.** The model uses a Dirac Hamiltonian describing low-energy physics, such as in bismuth, and incorporates short-range nonmagnetic impurities via the T-matrix formalism. The analysis distinguishes between spin accumulation ($	ext{SAC}$) and magnetic moment accumulation ($	ext{MMAC}$).

**Key observables.** Spin Accumulation Coefficient ($	ext{SAC}$), Magnetic-Moment Accumulation Coefficient ($	ext{MMAC}$), Spin Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}^{sz}$), and $	ext{MMH}$ conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}^{mz}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric conductivity ($\sigma^{yy}$), Fermi energy ($\epsilon_F$), impurity concentration ($n_i$), and the ratio of band gap to Fermi energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the self-consistent T-matrix approximation, assuming short-range impurities, and often neglect vertex corrections (VCs) or assume a scalar self-energy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the density of states, the dependence of $	ext{SAC}$ and $	ext{MMAC}$ on Fermi energy (comparing T-matrix vs. Born approximations), and the scaling relationship between conductivity and $	ext{SAC}/	ext{MMAC}$ across different impurity regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives transport coefficients ($\sigma_{jj}, \sigma_{ij}^{sa}, g_{ij}^{sa}, \sigma_{xy}^{mz}$) using Keldysh Green's functions in the T-matrix approximation, analyzes the resulting scaling laws based on conductivity, and concludes by proposing $	ext{SAC}$ as a robust measure for the SH effect.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spin Hall (SH) effect for the Dirac electrons in terms of the spin and magnetic-moment accumulation coefficients $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy}$. We take short-range nonmagnetic impurities into account within the self-consistent $T$-matrix approximation. Similarly to the universal scaling for the anomalous Hall (AH) effect, we find three disctinct regimes by changing the electric conductivity $σ^{yy}$; the superclean regime with $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy} \propto σ^{yy}$ owing to the skew scattering, moderately dirty regime with almost constant $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy}$, and dirty regime with a new scaling relation $-Γ^{00} g_{s(m)z}^{\phantom{s(m)z} xy} \propto (σ^{yy})^{0.6}$ whose exponent differs from that of the AH conductivity. Our results construct a unified theory of the SH effect without any ambiguity of spin current.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a functional universal logic circuit built from superconducting nanowires controlled by side-gate electrodes. By exploiting the Gate-Controlled Supercurrent effect, the authors successfully implemented all classical logic gates (AND, OR, NOT, etc.) using only three components. This establishes a promising, low-power building block for future hybrid superconducting supercomputers.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical digital logic, the underlying physics—controlling quantum states (superconductivity) with external fields to achieve robust switching—is highly relevant to superconducting qubits and quantum circuit design.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop superconducting-based computing architectures to overcome scaling and power limitations of conventional CMOS electronics.

**Main result.** A universal logic circuit capable of implementing all classical logic gates (AND, OR, NOT, etc.) and combinational circuits like the half-adder was realized using only three superconducting nanowires.

**Method.** The functionality is demonstrated by controlling the supercurrent in superconducting nanowires via an applied gate voltage, switching the device between a zero-voltage superconducting state and a finite-voltage normal state.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes superconductor-semiconductor hybrid structures, specifically $	ext{NbTiN}$ superconducting nanowires patterned on Si substrates. The operation relies critically on the Gate-Controlled Supercurrent (GCS) effect.

**Key observables.** Switching current ($I_{sw}$), critical current ($I_c$) as a function of gate voltage ($V_{sg}$), and the resulting output voltage ($\approx 2 	ext{ V}$) corresponding to logical '1' or '0'.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operation is performed at liquid helium temperatures ($4	ext{ K}$). Key electrical parameters include $I_{sw} \approx 332\ \mu	ext{A}$ and gate voltage ranges for suppression ($\approx 30	ext{ V}$ to $40	ext{ V}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is the requirement for large gate voltages to achieve supercurrent suppression, and thermal fluctuations can cause small scattering on plateaus.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device configuration using three nanowires; $V-I$ curves demonstrate switching capability; schematics show time-domain realizations of AND, OR, NOT, XOR, and half-adder logic gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the GCS effect as a superconducting switch. It then systematically demonstrates the realization of basic gates (AND, OR, NOT) using minimal components, culminating in the proof of universality for all classical logic functions and combinational circuits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The observation of the gate-controlled supercurrent (GCS) effect in superconducting nanostructures initiated major research efforts toward the realisation of superconducting-based computing architectures. Here we introduce a universal logic circuit that can be a promising superconducting building block of classical hybrid supercomputers. We demonstrate a functionally complete set of logic gates by realising the AND, OR, NOT and COPY gates. The general layout and scalability of our device, combined with recent experiments demonstrating fast switching and small voltage signals, make it a functional candidate in superconducting electronics. Our device enables the realization of all classical logic gates and the half-adder combinational logic circuit using at most three nanowires, each uniquely configured with two side-gate electrodes.

</details>

**Summary.** This research presents a robust method to passivate the native oxide interfaces on Niobium superconductors using Platinum alloying. By combining advanced spectroscopy and DFT simulations, the authors prove that annealing Pt overlayers forms a stable $	ext{Nb-Pt}$ alloy, effectively mitigating dielectric loss mechanisms critical for high-coherence superconducting qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses a critical material science bottleneck for superconducting quantum circuits by engineering interface chemistry, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems where environmental coupling (loss) dictates coherence time.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Dielectric loss from two-level systems (TLS) at surfaces and interfaces limits coherence in superconducting transmon qubits built using Niobium (Nb).

**Main result.** Depositing a thin Pt layer followed by annealing transforms the surface into a chemically stable, passivating Nb–Pt alloy that suppresses further oxide growth.

**Method.** The study combines advanced experimental techniques like Variable Energy XPS (VEXPS) and Extended X-ray Absorption Fine Structure (EXAFS) with first-principles DFT calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting Nb films on sapphire, focusing specifically on the native oxide interface ($	ext{NbO}_x/	ext{Nb}$) and its modification by Pt alloying ($	ext{Pt}/	ext{NbO}_x/	ext{Nb}$).

**Key observables.** Superconducting Transition Temperature ($T_c$), Residual Resistance Ratio (RRR), chemical oxidation states ($	ext{Nb}^{5+}$ vs $	ext{Nb}^0$), and local atomic structure derived from EXAFS fitting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_c = 9.30 \pm 0.10$ K; Native oxide thickness of $2.6 \pm 0.2$ nm; Alloying structures identified as $	ext{NbPt}_3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** VEXPS is necessary due to overlapping spectral signatures, and the analysis relies on fitting experimental data against theoretical structural models.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail cross-sections showing oxide removal (EDX), VEXPS spectra confirming chemical state changes, and EXAFS fits establishing the $	ext{NbPt}_3$ surface alloy structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the TLS loss problem in Nb qubits to demonstrating Pt encapsulation. It then uses advanced spectroscopy (VEXPS/EXAFS) to characterize the resulting stable $	ext{Nb-Pt}$ alloy, supported by DFT calculations elucidating charge transfer and structural formation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dielectric loss arising from two-level systems (TLS) at surfaces and interfaces remains a primary limitation to coherence in superconducting transmon qubits. Niobium (Nb), a widely used material in superconducting quantum circuits, readily forms native oxides under ambient conditions, leading to lossy dielectric interfaces that degrade device performance. Here, a robust and scalable fabrication strategy is demonstrated for chemically stabilizing Nb surfaces and mitigating further oxidation, including protection of both surface and sidewall regions. High-purity Nb films were fabricated with bulk-like superconducting transition temperatures ($T_c = 9.30\pm0.10$) K. We demonstrate that a thin Pt encapsulation layer, deposited after native oxide formation, can be transformed via thermal annealing into a Nb-Pt alloy at the surface. Spectroscopic and microscopic analyses confirm the formation of a chemically stable metallic alloy layer and its ability to suppress further oxide growth. Ab initio simulations elucidate the atomic-scale rearrangement and electronic structure evolution associated with Pt incorporation on native niobium oxide, providing insight into the stabilization mechanism of the alloyed surface. This approach offers a materials pathway for engineering chemically robust Nb interfaces, including sidewalls, toward higher-coherence superconducting qubit architectures."

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental study characterizes the formation of hydroxyl defects within the surface oxides of superconducting Tantalum. Using SIMS and ARXPS, the authors prove that these hydroxyl groups are major contributors to decoherence sources in quantum circuits. They demonstrate that modifying the oxide layer via CMP or plasma nitridization can effectively suppress this defect concentration.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical material science bottleneck for superconducting qubits and quantum devices by identifying a specific molecular defect source (hydroxyls) and providing actionable surface engineering solutions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Surface oxides on superconducting Tantalum (Ta) host Two-Level System (TLS) defects, which are a primary source of microwave loss and decoherence in quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The accumulation of hydroxyls ([OH]) in the suboxide region is confirmed, and surface engineering techniques like Chemical Mechanical Planarization (CMP) or plasma nitridization can suppress this hydroxyl loading to improve coherence.

**Method.** The study employs advanced characterization techniques including Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (SIMS), Angle-resolved X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (ARXPS), and Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM).

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting Tantalum ($\alpha$-Ta) films coated with native or engineered surface oxides ($	ext{Ta}_2	ext{O}_5$, $	ext{TaO}_x$). The focus is on the molecular defects, specifically hydroxyls, within this oxide layer.

**Key observables.** Depth profiles of oxide species ($	ext{Ta}_2	ext{O}_5$, $	ext{TaOH}$), total maximum $	ext{TaOH}^-/	ext{Total}$ signal intensity across different treatments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting temperature regime, surface treatment type (native vs. CMP vs. nitridized), and oxide thickness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The TLS defects are molecularly linked to the presence of hydroxyl groups within the non-uniform oxide structure; mitigation via surface chemistry is feasible.

**Figures summary.** Figures show ARXPS analysis confirming stratified oxidation states, SIMS depth profiles quantifying $	ext{TaOH}$ accumulation in suboxides, and comparative plots demonstrating that CMP or nitridization reduces the maximum hydroxyl signal intensity.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the oxide structure using ARXPS/TEM, then uses SIMS to map out the chemical species ($	ext{Ta}_2	ext{O}_5$, $	ext{TaOH}$) across depth. It culminates by comparing native vs. engineered oxides (CMP, nitridization) to quantify hydroxyl suppression.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, tantalum (Ta) has gained attention in superconducting quantum circuits due to the longer coherence times achieved when replacing niobium (Nb) in capacitor pads. Previous literature shows that surface oxides that form upon ambient exposure on superconducting metals such as Ta, Al, and Nb host two-level system (TLS) defects, which are a leading source of microwave loss and decoherence. While the surface oxides of Nb and Al have been extensively studied, Ta oxides remain less well understood. Using secondary ion mass spectrometry of alpha-Ta films deposited at 300 mm wafer scale, we show for the first time that hydroxyls accumulate in the Ta suboxide region above the underlying Ta. Angle-resolved X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy shows that the surface region is dominated by Ta2O5, with sub-stoichiometric TaOx present in between the Ta2O5 and underlying Ta. The thickness of the tantalum oxide is confirmed by transmission electron microscopy. We demonstrate that [OH] incorporation can be suppressed by replacing the native oxide with an oxide formed during chemical mechanical planarization of alpha-Ta films. Our findings support the hypothesis that TLS defects are non-uniform within the oxide thickness and suggest hydroxyls as a probable molecular origin of these loss channels. Furthermore, we show the feasibility of plasma nitridization as a method to decrease hydroxyl loading on alpha-Ta surfaces. The modulation of hydroxyl content through surface engineering of alpha-Ta can enable the fabrication of more robust, high-coherence superconducting quantum circuits by addressing a potential TLS source.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces and validates a novel technique for measuring the optical absorption of 2D TMD materials integrated into $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ waveguides. By using the waveguide's own photoluminescence as an internal probe source, they achieve source-free, on-chip spectroscopy. This method directly quantifies how guided light interacts with the TMDs, providing critical data for developing next-generation integrated quantum optoelectronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful experimental tool for characterizing quantum material properties (excitonic absorption) under realistic device operating conditions, which is crucial input data for designing quantum photonic circuits or sensing elements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a method to accurately measure the optical absorption of two-dimensional transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) while they are integrated into an operational photonic waveguide environment.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated source-free, on-chip evanescent absorption spectroscopy, showing that guided photoluminescence spectral reshaping quantitatively reflects the energy-dependent absorption response of the TMD layer.

**Method.** They use the broadband defect-related photoluminescence generated within the $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ waveguide core as an internal light source, which then propagates and is attenuated by evanescent coupling with the overlying TMD flake.

**Model / system.** The system involves transferring TMD flakes ($	ext{MoS}_2$, $	ext{WS}_2$) onto $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ photonic waveguides. Measurements compare this guided PL attenuation to local micro-photoluminescence ($\mu$-PL) and Raman characterization.

**Key observables.** Energy-dependent guided absorption spectrum ($	ext{dB cm}^{-1}$), spectral reshaping of photoluminescence, and comparison between uniform vs. non-uniform flake measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Excitonic resonance energies (e.g., $\sim 1.83 	ext{ eV}$ for $	ext{MoS}_2$), coverage lengths ($L = 680 \mu	ext{m}$ to $1.2 	ext{ mm}$), and attenuation coefficients ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes the PL generated in the core accurately represents the light interacting with the TMDs, and near resonance, reported losses are conservative lower-bound estimates due to saturation effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate sample sketches, comparison plots of transmitted vs. reference spectra, and detailed spectral measurements showing excitonic resonances and attenuation profiles for $	ext{MoS}_2$ and $	ext{WS}_2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of in-situ characterization, details the source-free PL measurement technique using $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ waveguides, presents comparative results against local measurements ($\mu$-PL/Raman), and concludes by validating the platform for integrated optoelectronics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

On-chip integration of two-dimensional transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) with photonic waveguides underpins a growing class of compact optoelectronic and sensing devices, whose performance depends on the optical absorption of the 2D material in this integrated environment. We demonstrate source-free, on-chip evanescent absorption spectroscopy of MoS$_2$ and WS$_2$ flakes integrated on Si$_3$N$_4$ waveguides, in which the broadband defect-related photoluminescence of the Si$_3$N$_4$ core itself acts as the internal probe. Light generated inside the core propagates under the TMD flake and is spectrally attenuated by evanescent interaction with the 2D material, and normalizing the transmitted spectrum to a reference waveguide without TMD yields the energy-dependent absorption response without any external illumination. The extracted guided absorption agrees with Raman and local micro-photoluminescence characterization and resolves differences between spatially uniform and non-uniform flakes. A complementary top-pumping scheme recovers guided WS$_2$ photoluminescence, whose spectral reshaping during propagation is quantitatively reproduced by the measured absorption. This source-free platform qualifies 2D materials in their integrated photonic environment and provides a direct route toward waveguide-integrated TMD photodetectors and sensors.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how electrons scatter from magnetic skyrmion textures in the strong-coupling regime, moving beyond simple weak-interaction approximations. By employing the Lippmann-Schwinger equation, the authors predict new scattering resonances that critically determine the material's topological and spin Hall conductivities. This provides a detailed quantum mechanical understanding of charge transport in complex chiral magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the use of advanced Green's function techniques to model quantum scattering off structured potentials (like skyrmions) shares mathematical rigor with open quantum system dynamics and non-equilibrium many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses electron scattering from chiral spin textures, like skyrmions, specifically overcoming the limitations of weak-coupling approximations used in previous research.

**Main result.** The full formalism reveals novel scattering features—including Ramsauer-Townsend minima and Landau-level resonances—that strongly influence the topological and spin Hall conductivities.

**Method.** The problem is solved using the Lippmann-Schwinger equation and Green's function formalism, which remains valid across all coupling strengths.

**Model / system.** The system models itinerant electrons scattering off a 2D skyrmion potential derived from an exchange interaction Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on calculating spin-dependent scattering cross sections ($\sigma_{\alphaeta}$) and resulting transverse currents.

**Key observables.** Topological Hall conductivity ($\sigma_H$), Spin Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{SH}$), and the energy-dependent total scattering cross section ($\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Incident electron energy ($E$) relative to the exchange coupling strength ($J$); winding number ($m$) of the skyrmion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a full Green's function approach, which is non-perturbative and valid for strong coupling; approximations are noted when relating the magnetic field to Landau levels (adiabatic limit).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate time evolution of wave packets showing spin trapping at low energy ($E<J$), scattering cross sections ($\sigma_{\alphaeta}$) exhibiting resonances, and plots detailing $\sigma_H$ and $\sigma_{SH}$ dependence on incident energy $E$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of a full-coupling formalism to calculating spin-resolved scattering cross sections. It then derives the topological and spin Hall conductivities based on current asymmetries, mapping out distinct behavior in different energy regimes relative to the coupling strength.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron scattering from chiral spin textures such as skyrmions is fundamental to the understanding of transport in more complex systems, including skyrmion crystals. Most of the previous studies have focused on the weak-coupling regime, where the exchange interaction is small compared with the electron energy. Real materials, however, often lie in the strong-coupling regime, which exhibits qualitatively different behavior. Using the Lippmann-Schwinger equation and Green's function formalism, valid for all coupling strengths, we uncover several new features in the scattering cross section, including Ramsauer-Townsend minima, pronounced intermediate-coupling resonances, and Landau-level resonances for skyrmions with larger winding numbers. These features strongly influence the topological and spin Hall conductivities, which depend sensitively on the incident electron energy. Our work provides important insights into the Hall transport in collective chiral spin textures such as the skyrmion crystal.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a straintronic magnetic tunnel junction (s-MTJ) as a highly tunable analog AC voltage amplifier. The device's gain is uniquely dependent on an external supply voltage, offering advantages over fixed-gain transistors. By analyzing the magnetization dynamics, the authors derive the amplification characteristics and suggest structural modifications to enhance performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on spintronics/electronics, the underlying physics involves non-linear dynamics (LLGL simulations) coupled to an external control parameter ($V_G$), which touches upon concepts of parametric amplification and controlled quantum/classical response relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate a novel analog AC voltage amplifier utilizing the unique properties of straintronic magnetic tunnel junctions (s-MTJs).

**Main result.** The device exhibits linear amplification whose gain is tunable by an external power supply voltage, unlike traditional transistors.

**Method.** The authors modeled the magnetization dynamics and derived the amplification factor based on superimposing a small AC voltage onto a DC bias point within the s-MTJ's linear conductance regime.

**Model / system.** The system is a single straintronic magnetic tunnel junction (s-MTJ), whose resistance depends on the magnetization angle ($\phi$) and can be continuously tuned by an external gate voltage ($V_G$).

**Key observables.** Output voltage $	ilde{V}$, amplification factor $A$, conductance swing, and maximum allowed AC input amplitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The proportionality constant $\kappa$ (slope of linear conductance vs. $V_G$), the supply voltage $V_S$, and the ferromagnetic resonance frequency (GHz range).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The amplification remains distortion-free only if the AC gate voltage excursion stays within the established linear region, and the signal frequency must be low enough relative to the magnetization dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 5 illustrates the relationship between output voltage and input gate voltage swing, showing the derived linear amplification formula.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical basis of s-MTJs for analog applications by noting their linear conductance region. It then models the AC amplification process, deriving the gain expression, and finally discusses methods to improve performance by modifying device geometry or material properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs) are known for their digital applications (memory and logic). A special class of them called "straintronic" magnetic tunnel junctions (s-MTJ) has lately emerged as a potential platform for analog applications because their conductance can be varied continuously with mechanical strain generated with a gate voltage. The conductance versus gate voltage (transfer) characteristic always has a linear region and that can be leveraged for a variety of analog applications. Here, we discuss one such application, namely analog voltage amplification. If the s-MTJ's gate voltage is fixed around the midpoint of the linear region and a small ac voltage is superimposed on it, then the ac voltage can be amplified without distortion as long as its amplitude is small enough to avoid gate voltage excursion beyond the linear region. Unlike a transistor-based voltage amplifier whose amplification is determined solely by the transistor's internal parameters - namely the transconductance and Early resistance - here the amplification can be varied by an external power supply voltage. We examine the maximum allowed amplitude and frequency of input signal for distortion-free amplification by modeling the magnetization dynamics and derive an expression for the amplification.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the Spin Seebeck Effect (SSE) as a definitive experimental probe to identify altermagnetic materials. By modeling magnon-phonon coupling, the authors predict that altermagnets will exhibit unique directional anisotropy and field-dependent resonant peaks in the SSE that are absent or different in conventional antiferromagnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of quasiparticle descriptions (magnon-polarons) and non-equilibrium transport calculations touches upon concepts relevant to open quantum systems dynamics and energy/momentum transfer in correlated media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To find experimentally accessible transport signatures that can distinguish altermagnets from conventional ferromagnets or antiferromagnets.

**Main result.** The Spin Seebeck Effect (SSE) exhibits pronounced directional anisotropy and resonant peaks whose positions depend on the measurement direction in altermagnets, providing a robust diagnostic signature.

**Method.** Theoretical calculation using Boltzmann theory to model spin transport mediated by magnon-phonon hybridization. The analysis compares theoretical predictions for altermagnetic versus antiferromagnetic coupling regimes.

**Model / system.** The study uses a minimal lattice Hamiltonian describing collinear magnets with compensated antiparallel spins (altermagnets). It incorporates magnetoelastic interactions leading to hybridized 'magnon-polaron' modes.

**Key observables.** Spin Seebeck coefficient ($S_{\alphaeta}$), directional anisotropy of SSE ($S_{xx} 
eq S_{yy}$), and resonant peak positions as a function of applied magnetic field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field strength ($H$), exchange couplings ($J_1, J_2, J'_2$), and phonon coupling strengths ($\Gamma, \kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the spin-wave approximation for magnon dynamics and assumes that magnon-polaron modes are well-defined quasiparticles.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare magnon dispersion relations (A vs B modes) between altermagnetic ($J_2 
eq J'_2$) and antiferromagnetic ($J_2 = J'_2$) states, showing directional anisotropy in the SSE response as a function of magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical distinction between magnet types using magnon dispersion theory to calculating the measurable transport signature (SSE) by incorporating phonon coupling via the Boltzmann equation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets, distinguished by compensated antiparallel spins yet nondegenerate magnon spectra, bridge the gap between ferromagnets and antiferromagnets. Although several probes such as anisotropic transport and spectroscopic measurements have been proposed to identify altermagnetic order, experimentally accessible transport signatures remain highly desirable. Here, we show that in altermagnets subject to an external magnetic field, the spin Seebeck effect exhibits pronounced directional anisotropy. Specifically, the spin Seebeck coefficient differs significantly along in-plane directions, and this anisotropy increases with field strength. Magnetoelastic interactions further produce resonant peaks whose positions with respect to an applied magnetic field reflect the intrinsic magnon band anisotropy, and provide localized features that enhance the distinguishability of the altermagnetic response. The peaks provide a robust experimental signature of altermagnetic order. Our findings serve as signatures of altermagnetic order while laying the groundwork for their application in spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reveals that spin-polarized transport in the altermagnet $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ is governed by real-space, atom-selective conduction channels rather than just momentum-space splitting. By exploiting charge ordering to create distinct $	ext{Fe}^{2+}$ and $	ext{Fe}^{3+}$ sites, researchers show how doping can switch the current flow between these specific atomic pathways, enabling highly controllable spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the core concept—controlling quantum transport via engineered local chemical potential (doping) to select specific atomic pathways—is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where boundary conditions or site chemistry dictate coherence and current flow.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate spin-polarized transport in altermagnets by moving beyond conventional momentum-space descriptions to focus on real-space spin selectivity.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that charge ordering creates atom-selective conduction channels ($	ext{Fe}^{3+}$ vs $	ext{Fe}^{2+}$) which enable highly controllable, spin-polarized transport in the altermagnet $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$. This mechanism allows for orders-of-magnitude conductance modulation via junction design.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations (DFT/GGA+U) combined with non-equilibrium Green’s function (NEGF) formalism to simulate quantum transport.

**Model / system.** The system is the charge-ordered altermagnet $\alpha$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$, which possesses a puckered C-type antiferromagnetic stacking and exhibits inequivalent $	ext{Fe}^{2+}$ and $	ext{Fe}^{3+}$ sites due to charge ordering.

**Key observables.** Spin-resolved transmission coefficients ($\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}$), local conductance, spin polarization on sublattices, and junction conductance modulation (ON/OFF ratio).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron/hole doping levels ($n$ or $p$), the nature of the active atomic sites ($	ext{Fe}^{2+}$ vs $	ext{Fe}^{3+}$), and the degree of spin splitting near the Fermi level.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that real-space connectivity (atom selectivity) dictates transport properties even when momentum-space spin splitting is weak.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, band/DOS showing weak splitting, constant-energy surfaces for spin states, and schematic representations of bond-resolved transmission ($\mathcal{T}$ vs $\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}$) under different doping conditions in a tunnel junction model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the limitations of momentum-space analysis to identifying real-space charge/spin selectivity due to site inequivalence, demonstrating this through transport calculations, and finally proposing an all-in-one tunnel junction device concept based on channel matching.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets provide a promising platform for spin-polarized transport without net magnetization, but their transport properties are usually discussed in terms of momentum-space spin splitting. Here, using first-principles calculations and quantum transport simulations, we show that the charge-ordered altermagnet $α$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ exhibits a distinct form of real-space spin selectivity despite weak altermagnetic spin splitting near the Fermi level. The charge order creates inequivalent Fe$^{2+}$ and Fe$^{3+}$ sites within each sublattice, while the puckered C-type antiferromagnetic stacking suppresses inter-sublattice transport. As a result, electron and hole doping activate spin-polarized transport predominantly through Fe$^{3+}$- and Fe$^{2+}$-based channels, respectively. These atom-selective channels carry opposite spin polarizations on the two antiferromagnetic sublattices, giving rise to a globally compensated charge current with hidden Néel spin character. We further propose an all-in-one $α$-Fe$_2$PO$_5$ tunnel junction, where matching or mismatching atom-selective conduction channels yields orders-of-magnitude conductance modulation. Our findings establish a real-space design principle for atomically controlled spin functionality and spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically demonstrates that the magnon spin photocurrent in magnetoelectric oxides like $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ can be controlled by an external electric field. Using nonlinear response theory, the authors show that the DMI mediates this control, leading to electrically tunable spin photovoltaic effects via both one- and two-magnon processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from nonlinear optics (second-order response), magnetism (magnons/spintronics), and ferroelectricity (magnetoelectric coupling) in a solid-state context, which is highly relevant to quantum materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how an external electric field can electrically control the magnon spin photocurrent in magnetoelectric oxides.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that the Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (DMI) plays a key role, allowing for electrical tuning of the spin-current conductivity and confirming $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$ as a promising platform for tunable spin photovoltaic effects.

**Method.** Nonlinear response theory is employed to calculate the second-order spin current conductivity ($\sigma^{(2)}$), considering both one-magnon and two-magnon excitation processes.

**Model / system.** The primary system analyzed is $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{O}_3$, modeled using a general spin Hamiltonian that incorporates exchange interactions, anisotropy, and the electric-field induced DMI term. The analysis progresses from 1D effective models to realistic 3D lattice descriptions.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear spin current conductivity ($\sigma^{(2)}_{\mu
u\lambda}$), resonance frequencies, peak intensities, and the continuum spectrum resulting from two-magnon processes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric field strength ($E$), Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya coupling constant ($\lambda$), and magnon excitation energies ($\omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on spin-wave theory approximations, treating the system as bosonic magnons, and making specific assumptions about the validity of certain operators in the current calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic models (1D/3D), magnon dispersion relations under electric fields, and spectral plots comparing one-magnon and two-magnon contributions to spin conductivity ($\sigma_{z}^{xx}, \sigma_{z}^{zz}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using nonlinear response theory for bosonic systems, derives expressions for $\sigma^{(2)}$ from both one- and two-magnon terms, models the system using 1D and 3D spin Hamiltonians, and finally analyzes the field dependence of the resulting spectral features.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling magnetism by electric field or current is a central topic in spintronics. In this work, we argue that the magnon spin photocurrent can also be controlled by the electric field in magnetoelectrics. Taking Cr$_2$O$_3$ as an example, we demonstrate how the spin current is modified by the electric field, using nonlinear response theory. We find that the Dzyaloshinsky--Moriya interaction induced by the applied field plays a key role in modifying spin-current conductivity, which exhibits pronounced anisotropy with respect to the light polarization. In particular, both the resonance frequency and the peak intensity show distinct dependences on the external electric field $E$, demonstrating electrical control of the spin photocurrent. In addition, we show that the two-magnon processes give rise to a continuum spectrum, a consequence of the field-induced spin canting. These results show that Cr$_2$O$_3$ is a promising platform for realizing electrically tunable spin photovoltaic effect.

</details>
