**Summary.** The paper theoretically examines the stability of proposed chiral d+id' superconductivity in twisted cuprate bilayers against quantum phase fluctuations. The key finding is that these fluctuations severely destabilize the chiral state, forcing a first-order transition into a more conventional d-wave phase at low temperatures. This establishes quantum fluctuations as a critical constraint on realizing exotic superconducting symmetries in layered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how collective quantum fluctuations fundamentally constrain exotic superconducting phases (like chiral superconductivity) predicted by simpler theories, which is relevant for understanding emergent order parameters in strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how quantum phase fluctuations fundamentally alter the predicted phase diagram of chiral d+id' superconductivity in twisted cuprate bilayers, particularly concerning Time-Reversal Symmetry Breaking (TRSB).

**Main result.** Quantum phase fluctuations strongly suppress the stability of the chiral state, confining it to narrow windows of twist angle and ultra-low temperatures. This leads to a first-order transition into the regular d-wave state.

**Method.** The authors derived the fluctuation-corrected free energy by integrating out long-wavelength phase fluctuations from a continuum microscopic model Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted cuprate bilayers, modeled using a continuum approach incorporating interlayer tunneling and Coulomb repulsion. The analysis focuses on how collective phase fluctuations renormalize the superconducting order parameters.

**Key observables.** Minimal superconducting gap (2Delta_min), relative phase angle (2varphi), Josephson coupling strength (Delta_J), and the stability region in the twist-angle vs. Temperature plane.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($T/T_{1L}^c \lesssim 0.016$), specific interlayer tunneling strengths ($t$), and the twist angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats phase fluctuations by integrating out long-wavelength modes, which is appropriate for low temperatures; it also uses a simplified local form for the interlayer coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how quantum phase fluctuations renormalize the free energy and equilibrium phase angle across different twist angles, showing the suppression of the chiral state's stability region.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by developing a fluctuation-corrected free energy functional for the superconducting order parameters. It then analyzes this functional to map out the resulting phase diagram in terms of temperature and twist angle, comparing mean-field predictions with fluctuation corrections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Following theoretical proposals of chiral $d+id'$ superconductivity in twisted cuprate bilayers, experimental signatures of time-reversal symmetry breaking (TRSB) remain highly controversial. Here we demonstrate that quantum phase fluctuations fundamentally reshape the phase diagram of this proposed chiral state. Unlike regular superconducting orders, the chiral $d+id'$ state requires long-range coherence of an interlayer phase degree of freedom and is therefore intrinsically vulnerable to phase fluctuations. Incorporating these fluctuations nearly eliminates the chiral phase over most parts of the phase diagram, restricting it to a narrow twist-angle window and ultra-low temperatures. The fluctuation-driven destruction of chirality produces a first-order transition into the $d$-wave state, giving rise to coexistence and metastability. Meanwhile, Josephson phase locking is strongly weakened at the TRSB quantum critical point, which sits well within the superconducting regime. More broadly, our work establishes quantum phase fluctuations as a fundamental constraint on the emergence of TRSB phases in low-dimensional layered quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on modulating the anomalous Hall angle ($	heta_A$) in magnetic Weyl semimetals using $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$. By theoretically linking $	an	heta_A$ to the product of resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity, the authors experimentally achieved a large angle (~25°) through controlled doping and thickness reduction. This results in highly sensitive magnetic field detectors for spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is condensed matter transport (magnetotransport), the ability to engineer a macroscopic parameter like $	heta_A$ through controlled material modification (doping/strain) has parallels in controlling coupling strengths or interaction parameters in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of anomalous Hall sensors by maximizing the anomalous Hall angle ($	heta_A$), which quantifies current conversion efficiency.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated modulation of $	an	heta_A$ up to a magnitude of 0.46 (an angle of ~25°) in magnetic Weyl semimetals, leading to high magnetic field detectability ($23.5 	ext{ nT}/	ext{Hz}^{0.5}$).

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling relating $	an	heta_A$ to the product of resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity ($ho_{xx}\sigma^H_A$) with experimental measurements on fabricated nanoflakes.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on magnetic Weyl semimetals, specifically $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$, which are synthesized into single-crystalline nanoflakes. Modulation is achieved by tuning both the intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma^H_A$) and the longitudinal resistivity ($ho_{xx}$) via doping or thickness reduction.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall angle ($	an	heta_A$), Hall sensitivity ($7028 \mu\Omega\cdot	ext{cm/T}$), and magnetic field detectability ($23.5 	ext{ nT}/	ext{Hz}^{0.5}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The maximum achievable $	an	heta_A$ is the key parameter, which depends on tuning both $ho_{xx}$ and $\sigma^H_A$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalous Hall angle relationship is modeled as being proportional to the product of electrical resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity ($	an	heta_A \propto ho_{xx}\sigma^H_A$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $	an	heta_A$ dependence on $ho_{xx}$ and $\sigma^H_A$, showing optimal modulation paths, while other figures detail temperature and thickness dependencies of the AHE signal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (enhancing $	heta_A$), develops a theoretical framework linking $	an	heta_A$ to material parameters ($ho_{xx}, \sigma^H_A$), presents experimental results showing modulation via doping/thickness, and concludes with high-sensitivity device metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The anomalous Hall angle (θA) is a measure of the efficiency of converting a longitudinal driving current to a transverse spin-polarized Hall current. For anomalous Hall sensing, a large anomalous Hall angle can improve the sensitivity of magnetic field detection. However, modulation of this angle is challenging and magnetic materials typically have low angles of 0.1 to 3°. Here, we report modulation of the anomalous Hall angle in the magnetic Weyl semimetal Co3Sn2S2. We propose that the angle parameter tanθA can be formulated as a function of the product of electrical resistivity and anomalous Hall conductivity. Our scheme was utilized to demonstrate the modulation of tanθA up to a magnitude of 0.46, corresponding to an angle of around 25°. Microfabricated anomalous Hall devices using Fe-doped Co3Sn2S2 single-crystalline nanoflakes exhibit a high Hall sensitivity of 7028 μΩucm/T and a magnetic field detectability of 23.5 nT/Hz0.5 at 1 Hz.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes spin wave propagation in a ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer, showing that the dipolar coupling modifies the standard Damon-Eshbach modes. The key finding is that by tuning the material's magnetic permeability ($\mu$)—which can be controlled via temperature or external field near the Curie point—the surface spin waves can become unidirectional. This suggests the system is a promising platform for tunable magnonic logic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the concept of tunable wave directionality (unidirectionality) via material parameters ($\mu$) is analogous to controlling directional coupling in quantum optical or superconducting circuits, making it relevant for understanding engineered wave phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To calculate and analyze the spectra of bulk and surface spin waves (Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic modes) in a ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer, focusing on how dipolar coupling modifies these modes.

**Main result.** The system exhibits tunable unidirectional wave regimes for surface spin waves when the magnetic permeability ($\mu$) exceeds a critical value ($\mu_c$), allowing potential use in magnonic logic devices.

**Method.** Analytical solutions and numerical calculations were used to solve Laplace's equation under magnetostatic approximations, deriving dispersion relations for different propagation directions.

**Model / system.** The system is an FM/PM bilayer where the PM susceptibility ($\chi$) varies significantly near its Curie temperature ($T_C$), leading to a variable magnetic permeability $\mu$. The analysis covers surface and bulk spin waves propagating in various directions relative to the magnetization.

**Key observables.** Spin wave frequencies ($\Omega_n$), nonreciprocity ($\Delta\Omega$), group velocity ($v_g$), and the critical permeability values ($\mu_c$, $\mu_+^c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic susceptibility ($\chi \propto 1/(T-T_C)$), external magnetic field ($h$), film thickness ($d$), and propagation angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetostatic approximation is used, assuming the system can be described by a scalar potential $\phi$. The PM's behavior near $T_C$ dictates the key parameter $\mu$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the nonreciprocity ($\Delta\Omega 
eq 0$) for surface spin waves when $\mu 
eq 1$, show the onset of unidirectional behavior at $\mu > \mu_c$, and map out spectral regions for arbitrary propagation angles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the governing equations in the magnetostatic approximation to deriving specific dispersion relations for perpendicular ($\mathbf{k} \perp \mathbf{M}_0$) and general directions. It then analyzes the conditions for unidirectional wave propagation, discusses the spectral splitting between MSSW and BVMSW regimes, and concludes by linking tunability to temperature/field variations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the magnetostatic approximation, we calculate the spectra of bulk and surface spin waves in an in-plane magnetized ferromagnet/paramagnet bilayer. Due to the dipolar coupling between the layers, the paramagnet becomes polarized, which in turn modifies the spectrum of the Damon-Eshbach magnetostatic modes. We assume that the paramagnet is characterized by a magnetic susceptibility, $χ\propto 1/(T-T_C)$, which reaches large values when the system temperature $T$ is close to the Curie temperature $T_C$. We find the conditions under which surface spin waves become unidirectional, i.e., capable of carrying energy in only one direction, and determine the magnitude of their frequency nonreciprocity. We demonstrate the possibility of switching the unidirectional wave regime on and off by varying the external magnetic field or temperature, making the ferromagnet/paramagnet system an attractive platform for tunable magnonic logic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops and applies a reactive force field model to study defect migration in GaN under high electric fields. The key finding is that the interaction between the field, charge, and lattice structure is highly nonlinear and anisotropic. This complexity suggests that simple linear models fail to predict defect mobility accurately in operational electronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state defects rather than quantum information, the core concept—analyzing non-linear responses in a system due to external driving forces (electric field)—mirrors concepts in open quantum systems where environmental coupling leads to non-Markovian or complex dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to predict device reliability by understanding how external electric fields modulate defect transport energetics within Gallium Nitride (GaN).

**Main result.** Electric fields do not induce a simple linear bias; instead, they cause anisotropic and complex modifications to migration barriers via charge-lattice coupling. This nonlinearity arises from field-induced partial charge redistribution and local lattice distortion.

**Method.** The research utilizes a custom ReaxFF reactive force field, trained on DFT data, coupled with Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations and the Climbing-Image Nudged Elastic Band (CI-NEB) method to calculate defect barriers under applied fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Gallium Nitride (GaN), modeled using supercells containing various intrinsic point defects, including vacancies ($	ext{V}_{	ext{Ga}}, 	ext{V}_{	ext{N}}$) and interstitials. The methodology simulates the material response to electric fields up to $40 	ext{ MV/cm}$.

**Key observables.** Defect formation and migration energies ($	ext{E}_{	ext{F}}^{	ext{B}}$, $	ext{E}_{	ext{R}}^{	ext{B}}$), lattice parameters, partial charge redistribution, and local lattice distortion.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied electric field strength ($0$ to $0.04 	ext{ V}/	ext{\AA}$); defect type (vacancy vs. interstitial).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the QEq scheme limits the analysis to neutral defects, neglecting the full complexity of charge-state transitions ($	ext{V}^{3-}$ etc.). The ReaxFF potential is assumed accurate for capturing dynamic coupling effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons of Equation-of-State data (ReaxFF vs. DFT), and show how applied electric fields affect migration barriers, highlighting the non-linear changes in energy profiles for different defect types.

**Paper structure.** The paper first develops and validates a ReaxFF force field against known structural properties. It then applies this model to calculate zero-field defect energetics, followed by systematic investigation of how applied electric fields modulate these migration barriers, comparing linear dipole approximations with complex charge-lattice coupling effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A predictive understanding of defect transport in GaN under operating electric fields is critical for assessing device reliability in high-power and radiation environments. In this work, a ReaxFF reactive force field for GaN is developed using a density-functional-theory training set that includes structural, thermodynamic, and defect properties. The force field yields various properties such as lattice parameters, cohesive energies, and defect formation and migration energies in close agreement with prior first-principles and experimental results. Under externally applied electric fields, we find that migration barriers can be strongly modulated, with changes that depend on defect type and field orientation. Notably, the electric fields do not simply linearly bias defect motion in GaN, but can anisotropically modify migration barriers through charge-lattice coupling, leading to nonlinear transport behavior. The response arises from field-induced partial charge redistribution and local lattice distortion. These results demonstrate that electric fields can complexly modify the defect migration landscape, providing new insight into defect transport in GaN under high-field conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the existence of facet-selective ballistic supercurrent in weak topological insulators ($	ext{ZrTe}_5$) using Josephson junctions. By measuring SQUID oscillations and analyzing field dependence, the authors prove that superconductivity is confined to specific crystal facets hosting topological surface states. This establishes WTIs as a promising material platform for building next-generation superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete platform for realizing topological superconductivity in engineered solid-state devices, directly linking bulk crystal topology to macroscopic superconducting properties, which is highly relevant for quantum device architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to demonstrate and characterize facet-selective ballistic supercurrent flowing through weak topological insulators (WTIs) in three dimensions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that the supercurrent is spatially confined to specific crystallographic facets hosting gapless topological surface states, confirming WTI behavior and establishing these materials for facet-resolved superconducting devices.

**Method.** Measurements involve using Josephson junctions formed with WTIs ($	ext{ZrTe}_5$) coupled to superconductors ($	ext{Al}$ or $	ext{NbN}$) and probing the supercurrent via quantum interferometry and transport measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is based on Weak Topological Insulators (WTIs), specifically $	ext{ZrTe}_5$, which exhibit gapless topological surface states only on selected crystal facets, contrasting with Strong TIs. The physics involves proximity-induced superconductivity in these topological boundary states.

**Key observables.** SQUID-like critical current oscillations ($\Phi$-periodicity), distinct interference patterns upon magnetic field rotation, exponential temperature dependence of $I_c$, and sharp lobes in the Current-Phase Relation (CPR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($15 	ext{ mK}$ to $5.1 	ext{ K}$), superconducting critical currents ($I_c$), and magnetic field orientations ($\mathbf{B}_y$ vs $\mathbf{B}_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The supercurrent is assumed to be carried predominantly by the high-transmission topological surface channels, leading to ballistic transport signatures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SQUID oscillations and extracted supercurrent density profiles showing confinement to specific facets; $V$-$I$ curves demonstrate temperature dependence of $I_c$; and STM/STS data confirm gapless states at step edges.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical motivation by contrasting STI vs WTI surface state behavior, details the experimental setup using Josephson junctions on $	ext{ZrTe}_5$, presents transport measurements showing field-dependent supercurrent confinement, and concludes by confirming ballistic signatures via temperature dependence and CPR analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological superconductivity is widely pursued by inducing superconducting correlations in topologically protected boundary states. In two dimensions, this strategy has been realized using one-dimensional topological edge modes, but in three-dimensional crystals, spatially separated surface supercurrents confined to selected facets have not yet been achieved. Here we demonstrate facet-selective ballistic supercurrent in Josephson junctions based on the weak topological insulator ZrTe<sub>5</sub>. Superconducting quantum interferometry reveals SQUID-like critical current oscillations with flux-quantum periodicity, establishing that the supercurrent is spatially concentrated on specific crystallographic facets that host gapless topological surface states. Rotating the magnetic field yields markedly distinct interference patterns, linking the supercurrent distribution to the underlying bulk topology. The exponential temperature dependence of the critical current and triangular interference lobes provide signatures of ballistic transport due to high-transmission topological channels. These results establish weak topological insulators as a platform for facet-resolved superconducting devices and higher-order topological superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes guided optical modes ($	ext{TE}_0$ and $	ext{TM}_0$) in WS2 nanobeams and bulk crystals using advanced near-field techniques like s-SNOM. By mapping modal dispersion and measuring material absorption, the authors establish robust diagnostic protocols for assessing waveguiding quality in these promising 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on photonics, the detailed analysis of wave propagation in anisotropic 2D materials provides insights into boundary conditions and field confinement that are relevant for designing quantum optical cavities or waveguides interacting with quantum emitters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the limited experimental understanding of wavelength-dependent modal confinement and attenuation in WS2 waveguides, complicated by ambiguities in reported material extinction coefficients.

**Main result.** s-SNOM reliably captures relative modal trends for TE0 and TM0 modes across 800–1400 nm, confirming its utility as a diagnostic tool despite absolute loss values depending on measurement geometry.

**Method.** The authors employed hyperspectral cavity-enhanced imaging (iCEAS) to bound the extinction coefficient and utilized scattering-type s-SNOM microscopy to probe guided modes in both quasi-bulk and nanobeam WS2 waveguides.

**Model / system.** The physical system is WS2, a layered van der Waals material promising for nanophotonics due to its high refractive index and optical anisotropy. Measurements are performed on patterned nanobeams and larger quasi-bulk crystals deposited on an SiO2/Si substrate.

**Key observables.** Effective refractive indices (neff), modal confinement, propagation loss ($	ext{dB}/	ext{mm}$), and the extinction coefficient ($\kappa$) as functions of wavelength and geometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavelength range: 800–1400 nm; key modes: $	ext{TE}_0$ and $	ext{TM}_0$; critical thickness regimes (e.g., $112 \pm 5 	ext{ nm}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Absolute loss values measured by s-SNOM depend on measurement geometry, and nanobeam measurements can suffer from artifacts due to transverse interference or spatial sampling effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate iCEAS extinction maps, simulated/measured mode dispersion curves ($	ext{neff}$ vs. $\lambda$), field profiles showing TE0/TM0 confinement factors, and Fourier Transform peak fitting results for different modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically moves from characterizing the material absorption (iCEAS) to mapping modal behavior in quasi-bulk crystals using s-SNOM, and finally analyzing mode dispersion and artifacts in highly confined nanobeams across a wide spectral range.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The exceptionally high in-plane refractive index, low sub-bandgap absorption, and strong optical anisotropy of WS2 make it a promising material platform for next-generation integrated circuits for nanophotonics. Its layered van der Waals structure further enables heterogeneous integration with silicon photonics and emerging two-dimensional optoelectronic materials. However, despite increasing interest in the waveguiding properties of WS2, experimental studies of wavelength-dependent modal confinement and attenuation remain limited. Additionally, though the extinction coefficient of WS2 is expected to be near-negligible beneath the bandgap, reported values span orders of magnitude, leading to large uncertainty in predicted modal decay lengths and wafer-scale integration feasibility. To resolve these ambiguities we perform hyperspectral cavity-enhanced imaging, determining high-resolution upper and lower bounds on the extinction coefficient of WS2 within the visible-NIR edge. We further employ scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy (s-SNOM) to probe TE0, TM0, and higher-order modes in both quasi-bulk and nanobeam WS2 waveguides across the 800-1400 nm spectral range, enabling identification of mode-specific trends in wavevector dispersion and loss. This work simultaneously assesses s-SNOM as a probe of waveguide performance, and we find that while absolute loss values depend on measurement geometry, s-SNOM reliably captures relative modal trends and provides upper bounds on propagation loss, supporting its use as a diagnostic tool for anisotropic waveguides. We further identify significant artefacts in nanobeam measurements arising from transverse interference and spatial sampling effects when the structure size approaches the excitation wavelength, which can shift extracted effective indices by up to 0.25.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a rigorous symmetry framework using group theory to classify non-reciprocal responses in multi-terminal quantum ring devices. By analyzing the constraints imposed by symmetries like time-reversal and inversion, it predicts that only specific circulation patterns are physically observable in real systems. The findings are validated through detailed calculations on a superconducting quantum dot model.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, abstract mathematical tool (symmetry classification) to predict observable transport phenomena in mesoscopic superconducting circuits, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a symmetry-based framework to systematically classify non-reciprocal electrical responses occurring in multi-terminal quantum ring devices.

**Main result.** For an isosceles $n=3$ ring, the analysis predicts that only two types of non-reciprocal responses (uniform circulation and specific semi-circulation) are allowed due to residual geometric symmetries after breaking time-reversal ($T$) and inversion ($I$).

**Method.** The core method involves applying group theory (Burnside's Lemma) to classify the $2^n$ bond configurations into symmetry orbits, linking topological invariants like the winding number ($W$) to observable physical responses.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an $n$-vertex quantum ring device, analyzed using a minimal toy model of three quantum dots coupled to superconducting baths. The analysis extends from general ring structures to specific geometries like equilateral and isosceles triangles.

**Key observables.** Non-reciprocal transmission ($S_{nr}^{ab}$), the imaginary part of the current-current response function ($	ext{Im}[\chi_{nr}]$), and the absolute non-reciprocal weight ($R_{nr}^{tot}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of vertices $n$ (especially $n=3$), geometric symmetry type (equilateral vs. isosceles), and external field parameters like Zeeman fields ($\lambda$) or superconducting phase biases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the applicability of group theory to classify configurations, assuming that non-reciprocity requires the simultaneous breaking of both time-reversal ($T$) and spatial inversion ($I$) symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ring structure, show energy spectra splitting due to external fields, plot the reactive part of $	ext{Im}[\chi_{nr}]$ revealing distinct circulator/semi-circulator bands, and map out topological phase diagrams based on symmetry breaking.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general group theory framework for $n$-rings, then applying this to the minimal $n=3$ case, distinguishing between equilateral and isosceles geometries. It validates these theoretical predictions using a detailed quantum dot toy model calculation of linear response functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a symmetry-based framework to classify the non-reciprocal responses of multiterminal ring quantum devices. The device is modeled as a ring of $n$ vertices, where a binary variable $e_k\in\{+1,-1\}$ on each bond encodes the preferred direction of signal flow between terminals. Non-reciprocity corresponds to a preferred current configuration on the ring, and the symmetry group of the device partitions all $2^n$ configurations into equivalence classes(orbits) characterized by a topological winding number $W$. Using the minimal non-trivial case $n=3$, we establish two results independent of microscopic details. First, lifting the degeneracy within an orbit generates non-reciprocal responses. For $n=3$ this requires simultaneous breaking of both time-reversal $T$ and spatial inversion $I$. Breaking either alone is insufficient. Second, the residual geometry symmetry after $T$ and $I$ are broken determines which responses are observable. For an isosceles triangular geometry, only two types of response are allowed: uniform circulation (all bonds carrying current in the same direction) and semi-circulation with the reversed bond on the geometrically distinct base. Semi-circulation with the reversed bond on either equal leg is symmetry-forbidden. Both predictions are validated using a minimal toy model of three quantum dots coupled to superconducting baths, which demonstrates a reactive quantum circulator response.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes phase fluctuations in twisted d-wave superconducting bilayers, which are predicted to host chiral superconductivity. By retaining all orders of Josephson coupling, the authors show that these fluctuations significantly modify the collective mode spectrum. The key findings include a finite relative-phase stiffness even in the normal state and critical behavior indicating the breakdown of Josephson phase locking near quantum critical points.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the detailed analysis of collective mode excitations (phase dynamics) and symmetry breaking mechanisms (U(1) symmetry) provides general theoretical tools applicable to understanding order parameter fluctuations in various strongly correlated quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how higher-order Josephson coupling processes modify the low-energy phase fluctuations in twisted d-wave superconducting bilayers.

**Main result.** Phase fluctuations suppress the relative-phase stiffness but enhance the overall-phase stiffness in the d+id' phase, and the relative-phase mode acquires a gap strongly suppressed near the chiral quantum critical point.

**Method.** The authors use a theoretical analysis involving deriving an effective action for long-wavelength phase fluctuations by integrating out fermionic fields using a continuum microscopic model.

**Model / system.** The system is twisted cuprate bilayers exhibiting potential high-temperature chiral d+id' superconductivity, modeled via coupled d-wave superconducting monolayers with interlayer tunneling and Coulomb repulsion.

**Key observables.** Relative-phase stiffness, overall-phase stiffness, excitation gap of relative-phase fluctuations ($\omega_\pi(q)$), and the stability window of the d+id' phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interlayer tunneling amplitude ($t$), temperature ($T$), and twist angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the low-temperature regime where only long-wavelength fluctuations are relevant, and it assumes a mean-field framework for calculating fluctuation coefficients.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows how phase fluctuations narrow the stability window of the d+id' phase. Figure 2 displays superfluid stiffnesses ($D_0, D_\pi$) vs. temperature/twist angle. Figure 3 illustrates the Josephson gap ($\Delta_J$) and order parameter magnitude near the TRSB point.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an effective action for phase fluctuations by systematically including higher-order Josephson coupling terms, analyzes the resulting stiffnesses and gaps, and compares these results to mean-field predictions, focusing on critical behavior near quantum phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted cuprate bilayers have been predicted to host high-temperature chiral $d+id'$ superconductivity, originating from higher-order Josephson coupling processes. In such two-dimensional superconducting systems, long-wavelength fluctuations in the phase of the superconducting order parameter constitute gapless collective modes and therefore remain significant even at zero temperature. Here, we perform a theoretical analysis of the low-energy phase fluctuations in twisted $d$-wave superconducting bilayers, systematically retaining Josephson coupling to all orders. We demonstrate that higher-order Josephson coupling processes lead to nontrivial modifications of the phase dynamics. In particular, the stiffness of the relative phase between the two layers is finite even in the normal state, which is a direct consequence of the intralayer U(1) symmetry breaking explicitly induced by interlayer tunneling. Phase fluctuations suppress the relative-phase stiffness but enhance the overall-phase stiffness in the $d+id'$ phase. Furthermore, the relative-phase fluctuations acquire a small but finite excitation gap, which is no longer equal to the Josephson plasma frequency and is strongly suppressed upon approaching the chiral quantum critical point, indicating the breakdown of Josephson phase locking within the superconducting state.

</details>

**Summary.** The research pioneers the use of THz near-field photovoltage microscopy to image nanoscale defects within superconductors. By exciting the material with THz radiation, they measure localized voltage peaks that correlate directly with enhanced vortex dynamics at these weak spots. This technique provides a powerful new tool for understanding how material disorder influences superconducting properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges superconducting theory (vortex dynamics, gap energy) with advanced quantum optics/nano-spectroscopy techniques. The ability to probe local electronic states using THz photons is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium quantum phenomena in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is experimentally probing nanoscale inhomogeneities (weak spots) within superconductors, as their relevant energy scales are in the THz range, which far-field techniques cannot resolve.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy can map these defects by observing localized photovoltage peaks associated with enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at defect sites.

**Method.** They employed THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy using an AFM tip to confine THz radiation, achieving $\sim 300 	ext{ nm}$ spatial resolution while simultaneously measuring the local voltage response under a DC bias current.

**Model / system.** The study uses superconducting thin films (e.g., NbN) as the physical system. The physics involves quasiparticle excitation via THz photons, leading to vortex-assisted dissipation and measurable photovoltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{PV}}$). Theoretical support comes from Time-Dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulations.

**Key observables.** Photovoltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{PV}}$) spatial profiles showing localized peaks; the non-monotonic dependence of peak amplitude on bias current ($I_b$); and the constant Full Width at Half Maximum ($	ext{FWHM}$) of these peaks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz frequency (2.52 THz, $\hbar\omega = 10.4 	ext{ meV}$), spatial resolution ($\sim 300 	ext{ nm}$), and the superconducting gap energy ($2\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed $	ext{V}_{	ext{PV}}$ peak is directly attributed to enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at sites of reduced superfluid density (defects).

**Figures summary.** Figures show spatial maps and profiles of the measured $	ext{V}_{	ext{PV}}$, detailing how these peaks appear in the bulk, their dependence on bias current, and confirmation using autocorrelation functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the technical feasibility by describing the THz near-field setup. It then presents evidence via spatial mapping to locate defects (peaks), followed by analyzing the physics of the signal—showing its correlation with vortex dynamics and comparing results against theoretical TDGL simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanoscale inhomogeneities are a defining feature of many superconducting materials, yet their local electromagnetic response has remained difficult to access experimentally. This is because their relevant energy scale lies in the terahertz range, where wavelengths -- on the order of hundreds of microns -- are too large to spatially resolve nanoscopic variations. Here, we demonstrate the first application of THz near-field photovoltage nanoscopy in a superconductor, achieving 300 nm spatial resolution at 2.52 THz. Scanning a current-biased NbN strip, we reveal photovoltage peaks within the bulk associated with nanoscopic defects of reduced superfluid density. The observed photovoltage follows the evolution of the vortex-dissipative state and is attributed to enhanced vortex-antivortex pair nucleation at defect sites. Together, these results open a direct route to probing how material inhomogeneities influence light-matter interactions in superconductors, with implications for superconducting devices and strongly inhomogeneous systems such as high-Tc and moiré materials.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using electrostatically gated quantum dots in bilayer graphene to create controllable devices for manipulating electron valleys. By engineering the dot array geometry—especially by opposing the gate potentials—the system can function as a highly efficient valley filter or splitter. This establishes a realistic solid-state platform for generating and steering pure valley currents.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, tunable solid-state platform for valleytronics, which is highly relevant to quantum information processing by offering methods to manipulate spin or valley degrees of freedom without relying on external magnetic fields.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a tunable platform for achieving valley-dependent electron optics in bilayer graphene (BLG) using electrostatically defined quantum dots (QDs). This allows for controlling the flow of electrons based on their valley index ($K$ vs $K'$).

**Main result.** By engineering QD arrays—specifically using oppositely gated pairs—the system can act as an efficient valley filter or splitter, generating highly polarized currents with strong suppression of forward transmission.

**Method.** The analysis employs a four-band continuum model and solves the scattering problem using a generalized multiple-scattering formalism. The total current is decomposed into incident, scattered, and interference components for detailed characterization.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Bernal-stacked BLG containing electrostatically defined QDs. The mechanism relies on layer-antisymmetric gating to induce opposite signs of a mass-like term in the two valleys ($K$ and $K'$), enabling valley-dependent scattering without external fields.

**Key observables.** Valley-resolved currents ($\mathbf{j}_K$ vs $\mathbf{j}_{K'}$), angular profiles, and the degree of valley polarization quantified by $P(r, 	heta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dot radius ($R=10 	ext{ nm}$), layer asymmetry ($\Delta = 152 	ext{ meV}$), Fermi energy ($\approx 19 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of a four-band continuum model and approximates the incident beam using Gaussian profiles, which helps suppress interference effects compared to plane waves.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups (single/multi-dot arrays), far-field angular dependence of valley currents for both plane-wave and Gaussian incidence, and real-space current maps showing filtering or splitting actions based on dot gating geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the basic single-dot scattering mechanism to analyzing multi-dot configurations. It systematically compares the roles of identical vs. oppositely gated dots, culminating in the demonstration of combined filter/splitter functionality using engineered arrays.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electrostatically defined quantum dots (QDs) with layer-antisymmetric gating in Bernal-stacked bilayer graphene (BLG) open a local gap and generate a mass-like term with opposite sign in the two valleys, producing strongly valley-dependent scattering without magnetic fields, strain, or spin-orbit coupling. Building on this mechanism, we propose a tunable platform based on such QDs for valley-dependent electron optics in BLG. Using a four-band continuum model and a generalized multiple-scattering formalism, we analyze scattering of Gaussian electron beams from single- and multi-dot architectures and compute valley-resolved currents and angular profiles. A single dot produces distinct valley-dependent deflection, while multi-dot configurations enable enhanced control: identical-dot arrays act as valley splitters, whereas oppositely gated pairs function as valley filters. Combining these elements yields tunable generation, steering, and filtering of highly valley-polarized currents with strong suppression of forward transmission. The required energy scales, gate asymmetries, and device dimensions are within experimentally accessible regimes for dual-gated BLG, establishing quantum-dot arrays as a realistic platform for controllable valley-resolved electron optics.

</details>
